```text
   |          |        Dalmatia,   surrender
 NO         landings   Adalia,     29 Apr 1916
 DECISIVE   Apr/Aug;   Aegean      |
 BREAK-     withdrawn  islands,    FAILURE
 THROUGH    Dec 1915   Albania     |
   |          |           |        |
 FAILURE   FAILURE     "little     |
   |          |        headway"    |
   +----------+-----+-----+--------+
                    |
   CONSEQUENCE: Bulgaria joins the Central Powers (Oct 1915);
   Serbia is overrun; a Franco-British force lands at Salonika
   TOO LATE. 1915 is not a good year for the Allies.
```

*The chart is organised by strategic intention rather than by date, because 1915 is the year in which every available alternative to frontal attack was tried and none worked. ⚠️ That is the year's argument, and it is what converts a list of campaigns into an explanation of why attrition was adopted in 1916.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

After the Marne, a commander in the west had three options: attack the line anyway, find a flank, or find an ally who created one. ⚠️ 1915 is the year all three were attempted. The failure of all three is what made 1916 look the way it did.

✅ **Fact:** in the west the stalemate continued, though several attempts were made to break the trench line. The British tried at Neuve Chapelle and Loos, the French tried in Champagne, and the Germans attacked again at Ypres. ✅ Like all the attacks on the western front until 1918, these attempts failed to make a decisive breakthrough.

✅ **Fact:** in the east, Russia's fortunes were mixed: further successes against Austria, but defeat whenever they clashed with the Germans, who captured Warsaw and the whole of Poland. ⚠️ Note the shape that is emerging and that will hold for the rest of the war: Russia could beat Austria-Hungary and could not beat Germany, and Germany therefore had to keep rescuing its ally instead of concentrating.

### 2. The Dardanelles: why the idea was serious

⚠️ Gallipoli is often taught as a blunder. It is better taught as a **strategically intelligent idea executed under conditions that destroyed it**, because that is both more accurate and more useful in an answer.

✅ **Fact:** the Turkish blockade of the Dardanelles was beginning to hamper the Russians, who were already running short of arms and ammunition. ✅ It was partly to clear the Dardanelles and open up the vital supply line to Russia via the Black Sea that the Gallipoli Campaign was launched.

✅ **Fact:** the campaign was strongly pressed by Winston Churchill, Britain's First Lord of the Admiralty, as a way to escape from the deadlock in the west by eliminating the Turks, who were thought to be the weakest of the Central Powers because of their unstable government.

✅ The four objectives recorded are worth listing, because they show the scale of what was being attempted with a peripheral operation:

1. open the supply line to Russia through the Black Sea;
2. eliminate the weakest Central Power;
3. bring Bulgaria, Greece and Romania into the war on the Allied side;
4. make it possible to attack Austria from the south.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** an operation with four strategic objectives, any one of which would have changed the war, is not a frivolous operation. ⚠️ The correct verdict is that its logic was sound and its execution destroyed it - and that is a far better sentence than "Gallipoli was a disaster".

### 3. What actually went wrong

✅ **Fact:** the campaign was a total failure. The first attempt, in March, was an Anglo-French naval attack through the Dardanelles to capture Constantinople; it failed when the ships ran into a series of mines. ✅ This ruined the surprise element, so that when the British attempted landings at the tip of the Gallipoli peninsula in April, the Turks had strengthened their defences and no advance could be made.

✅ **Fact:** further landings by Australian and New Zealand troops - the Anzacs - in April, and by British troops in August, were equally useless, and positions could only be held with great difficulty. ✅ In December the entire force was withdrawn.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the causal hinge is the same one that ruled the western front - surprise, once lost, cannot be recovered. ⚠️ The March naval attempt was, in effect, a bombardment that warned the defender exactly where the attack would come, and the April landings then met a prepared defence on ground that favoured it. ⚠️ Gallipoli is therefore not an exception to the logic of Lesson 3; it is that logic operating on a beach instead of in a trench.

### 4. The four consequences, and the one that changed the map

✅ The recorded consequences were serious:

| ✅ Consequence | ⚠️ Significance |
|---|---|
| A blow to Allied morale | The cheap alternative to the western front had proved not to exist |
| It turned out to be the last chance of helping Russia via the Black Sea | ⚠️ The single most important consequence: Russia's supply crisis became structural, and the line to 1917 runs through it |
| It probably made Bulgaria decide to join the Central Powers | ⚠️ Neutral states were watching to see who would win; failure recruits for the other side |
| A Franco-British force landed at Salonika in neutral Greece to try to relieve Serbia, but it was too late | ⚠️ Creates the Salonika front, from which Bulgaria was eventually defeated in September 1918 |

✅ **Fact:** when Bulgaria entered the war in October 1915, Serbia was quickly overrun by Bulgarians and Germans.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** trace the chain, because it is examinable in itself. A failed naval attack in March produced a prepared defence in April; the failure of the landings encouraged a neutral to join the Central Powers in October; that entry destroyed Serbia, the state whose defence had been the original occasion of the war; and it opened a continuous land corridor from Berlin to Constantinople. ⚠️ Peripheral operations are not low-stakes operations.

### 5. Mesopotamia: the oil campaign and Kut

✅ **Fact:** even a British army sent to protect Anglo-Persian oil interests against a possible Turkish attack became bogged down in Mesopotamia as it approached Baghdad; it was besieged by Turks at Kut-al-Amara from December 1915 until it was forced to surrender on **29 April 1916**.

⚠️ **Date correction, stated once and used throughout.** The core printed account dates the surrender to March 1916. That is a source-inherited error. The garrison capitulated on **29 April 1916**, after a siege of about five months. This session uses 29 April 1916 everywhere, and the correction is recorded in the source ledger. ⚠️ An answer that writes "March 1916" is reproducing a textbook slip that a well-read examiner will recognise.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three things are examinable here. First, the explicit motive is **oil** - one of the earliest cases in which an industrial fuel supply shaped a campaign, and a useful fact in any answer about the war's modernity. Second, the force involved was largely raised in India, which connects directly to the colonial-manpower argument of Lesson 8. Third, the surrender at Kut was one of the war's significant reverses for British imperial prestige in Asia, and prestige is not a decorative category in an empire governed by consent as much as by force.

### 6. Italy: an ally bought with a secret treaty

✅ **Fact:** Italy declared war on Austria-Hungary in May 1915. The Italians hoped to seize Austria-Hungary's Italian-speaking provinces as well as territory along the eastern shore of the Adriatic Sea.

✅ **Fact:** a secret treaty was signed in London in which the Allies promised Italy Trentino, the South Tyrol, Istria, Trieste, part of Dalmatia, Adalia, some islands in the Aegean Sea and a protectorate over Albania.

✅ **Fact:** the Allies hoped that by keeping thousands of Austrian troops occupied, the Italians would relieve pressure on the Russians. But the Italians made little headway and their efforts were to no avail: the Russians were unable to stave off defeat.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this treaty is one of the most important documents in the entire topic, and it belongs in two later lessons as well. It is the reason Italy felt cheated at Paris in 1919; it is the clearest single contradiction of Wilson's later demand for the abolition of secret diplomacy, since it was a secret treaty promising territory over the heads of its inhabitants; and it is direct evidence that self-determination was not the principle on which the Allies actually recruited. ⚠️ Lessons 15, 16 and 21 all use it; introducing it here, with its contents named, is what allows those later arguments to be made in a sentence.

### 7. Comparison: four ways out of the deadlock, scored

| Route | Logic | ✅ What happened | ⚠️ Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| More force on the same front | Break the line by weight | Neuve Chapelle, Loos, Champagne, German attack at Ypres - no decisive breakthrough | ⚠️ Confirms the technical condition of Lesson 3 |
| A new theatre by sea | Turn the strategic flank, reopen Russia, recruit the Balkans | Gallipoli: mines in March, prepared defences in April, withdrawal in December | ⚠️ Sound aim, execution destroyed it; the most consequential failure of the year |
| A new ally | Open a front the enemy must garrison | Italy, May 1915, bought with the Treaty of London; little headway | ⚠️ Worked as a diversion, failed as relief; created a 1919 grievance |
| Protect a resource | Secure oil, threaten the Ottoman rear | Mesopotamia; siege and surrender at Kut | ⚠️ Modern motive, imperial cost |

⚠️ The scoring matters. ⚠️ Three of the four routes were *rational*, and all four failed - which is precisely why 1916 was fought the way it was. ⚠️ Attrition was not chosen because commanders lacked imagination; it was chosen after the imaginative alternatives had been tried and had failed within a single year.

### 8. The westerner-easterner dispute, and what survives of it

**The objection.** ⚠️ Was Gallipoli not simply an amateur strategist's adventure, and should the resources not have gone to the decisive theatre?

**The reply.** ⚠️ The "westerner" case is serious: the German army was the enemy's centre of gravity and it was in France. ⚠️ But the "easterner" case is serious too, and it is the one the evidence supports on its own terms: ✅ the Ottoman state was thought to be the weakest of the Central Powers because of its unstable government; ✅ success would have reopened the supply line to Russia and possibly recruited three Balkan neutrals. ⚠️ The decisive criticism is therefore not of the concept but of the sequencing - a naval attack that failed and thereby removed surprise before the landings were attempted. ⚠️ That criticism is compatible with thinking the idea sound.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Does blaming the loss of surprise not simply excuse the whole operation?

**The reply.** ⚠️ No, because the loss of surprise was not accidental in the relevant sense: it was the predictable consequence of attempting a naval forcing first and a landing afterwards against the same defender in the same place. ⚠️ The point is not that bad luck intervened; it is that the operation's own design gave the defender warning, which is the same design flaw that Lesson 3 identified in the western bombardment.

**The residual.** ⚠️ Whether Bulgaria's October 1915 decision was in fact caused by the Gallipoli failure is recorded only as a probability; ✅ the account says it *probably* made Bulgaria decide to join. ⚠️ Keep the qualifier.

### 9. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the demand this lesson most directly serves is the "why did it become a war of attrition" demand, because 1915 supplies the evidence that alternatives were tried.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "Attrition was not a preference but a residue." Discuss with reference to the campaigns of 1915. (Directive: discuss. Ceiling: 250 words.)
> ⚠️ Examine the strategic significance of the Gallipoli campaign. (Directive: examine. Ceiling: 150 words.)

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Use the four-route table as the skeleton and give each route one sentence of aim and one of outcome. ⚠️ For Gallipoli specifically, state the four objectives first, because they establish that the operation was serious, and then state the execution failure and the four consequences. ⚠️ The highest-value single sentence available is the one connecting Gallipoli to 1917: it was the last chance of helping Russia through the Black Sea.

### Traps

- Calling Gallipoli a stupid idea. ✅ It had four serious strategic objectives.
- Omitting that the March naval attack destroyed the surprise on which the April landings depended.
- Forgetting the Anzac dimension, which is where the campaign's imperial and commemorative significance lies.
- Dating the Bulgarian entry wrongly. ✅ October 1915, after the Gallipoli failure was evident.
- Omitting the Treaty of London or describing it vaguely. Its contents are the source of Italy's 1919 grievance.
- Treating Kut as a minor episode. It combines oil, imperial manpower and prestige in a single case.
- Dating the Kut surrender to March 1916. ✅ The garrison capitulated on 29 April 1916; the March date is a textbook error.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Western attempts of 1915: Neuve Chapelle and Loos (British), Champagne (French), a German attack at Ypres - no decisive breakthrough.
2. ✅ Germany captured Warsaw and the whole of Poland; Russia beat Austria but not Germany.
3. ✅ Gallipoli's purpose: clear the Dardanelles and reopen the supply line to Russia via the Black Sea.
4. ✅ Pressed by Churchill, First Lord of the Admiralty; the Turks were thought the weakest of the Central Powers because of unstable government.
5. ✅ Four objectives: help Russia; eliminate Turkey; bring in Bulgaria, Greece and Romania; attack Austria from the south.
6. ✅ March: Anglo-French naval attack failed on mines, ruining surprise. April: landings at the tip of the peninsula, including Anzac landings. August: further British landings. December: complete withdrawal.
7. ✅ Consequences: blow to Allied morale; the last chance of helping Russia via the Black Sea; probably decided Bulgaria; a Franco-British force landed at Salonika too late to save Serbia.
8. ✅ Bulgaria entered in October 1915; Serbia was quickly overrun by Bulgarians and Germans.
9. ✅ Mesopotamia: a British force sent to protect Anglo-Persian oil interests was besieged at Kut-al-Amara from December 1915 and surrendered on 29 April 1916.
10. ✅ Italy declared war on Austria-Hungary in May 1915 under a secret treaty signed in London promising Trentino, the South Tyrol, Istria, Trieste, part of Dalmatia, Adalia, some Aegean islands and a protectorate over Albania.
11. ✅ The Italians made little headway; the hope of relieving Russia failed.
12. ⚠️ The year's argument: every alternative to frontal attrition was tried in 1915 and failed, which is why 1916 was fought as attrition.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Gallipoli campaign matters more for what it foreclosed than for what it cost, and identify the specific later event that its failure made more likely.

**Model answer:** Gallipoli matters because it was the Allies' one serious attempt to alter the strategic geometry of the war rather than to win within it, and its failure closed that option permanently. The campaign had four objectives: to clear the Dardanelles and reopen the vital supply line to Russia through the Black Sea; to eliminate the Turks, thought to be the weakest Central Power because of their unstable government; to bring Bulgaria, Greece and Romania into the war on the Allied side; and to make it possible to attack Austria from the south. Its execution destroyed it. The Anglo-French naval attack in March ran into mines and failed, which ruined the surprise element, so that when British troops landed at the tip of the peninsula in April the Turks had strengthened their defences and no advance could be made; the Anzac landings in April and the British landings in August were equally useless, and the force was withdrawn in December. The consequences were disproportionate to the ground involved. It was a blow to Allied morale; it probably decided Bulgaria to join the Central Powers in October 1915, after which Serbia was quickly overrun; and, most importantly, it turned out to be the last chance of helping Russia via the Black Sea. That is the specific later event the failure made more likely: Russia, already short of arms and ammunition and already unable to replace the equipment lost in 1914, was left without a supply route, and the military collapse that produced the revolutions of 1917 and the Russian exit in December 1917 followed from that isolation as much as from anything on the battlefield.

**Misconception to avoid:** Judging a peripheral campaign by its own casualty figures or by the territory it gained. Peripheral operations are bets on changing the whole board, and the right question is what became impossible when the bet failed. On that test, Gallipoli was one of the most consequential failures of the war.

*Once going round had failed, the only remaining strategy was to wear the enemy down where he stood. 1916 is the year both sides said so out loud.*

## Lesson 5 - 1916: attrition chosen deliberately - Verdun, the Somme, Brusilov and Romania

Progress: 5/25 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: what attrition means as a strategy; Verdun's design and its recorded cost; the Somme's aims, its first day and its real importance; conscription in Britain; the recorded casualty figures and the discipline that governs their use; the "lions led by donkeys" charge and the historiographical reply; Lloyd George's arrival and the reorganisation of the British war economy; Brusilov's offensive; Romania's entry and destruction; Hindenburg's admission

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: attrition as an explicit strategy, and the arithmetic it depends on

```text
   THE ATTRITIONAL PROPOSITION
   "If I cannot break your line, I will destroy your army where it
    stands, and accept comparable losses, because my coalition can
    replace them and yours cannot."
                          |
        +-----------------+-----------------+
        |                                   |
   THE GERMAN VERSION                  THE ALLIED VERSION
   VERDUN, from February 1916          THE SOMME, 1 July to Nov 1916
   Falkenhayn attacks a fortress       Three stated aims:
   town the French must defend;        1. relieve pressure on the
   the aim is to draw in and              French at Verdun
   destroy the best French troops,     2. take over more of the
   then win with a final offensive        trench line as the French
        |                                  army weakened
   Petain defends; Germans abandon     3. keep the Germans fully
   the attack in June                     committed so they could not
        |                                  reinforce the east
   RECORDED COST                          |
   French about 315,000                RECORDED COST killed or wounded
   Germans over 280,000 killed         Germans 650,000
   NO TERRITORIAL GAIN                 British 418,000
        |                              French 194,000
        |                                  |
        +-----------------+-----------------+
                          |
   THE TEST OF THE PROPOSITION: does the losing side exhaust first?
   Hindenburg's memoirs admit the Germans could not have survived
   many more campaigns with heavy losses like Verdun and the Somme
                          |
   VERDICT: the proposition WORKED - and the political price of its
   working was the collapse of public faith in the commanders who
   applied it
```

*The chart states the year's uncomfortable finding. ⚠️ Attrition is usually taught as a failure of imagination. On the recorded evidence it was a strategy with a stated logic that produced the intended cumulative result, at a cost that permanently damaged the reputation of the armies that used it. ⚠️ Holding both halves of that sentence together is what a "critically examine" question on 1916 is testing.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

By the start of 1916 both high commands knew three things: the line could not be broken, the flanks could not be turned, and new allies had not changed the balance. ⚠️ What remains, when breakthrough and manoeuvre are both unavailable, is exhaustion - and exhaustion can be pursued deliberately. That is what "attrition" means as a strategy, and 1916 is the year in which both coalitions adopted it explicitly.

### 2. Verdun: an attack designed not to capture anything

✅ **Fact:** Verdun was an important French fortress town against which the Germans under Falkenhayn launched a massive attack in February. ✅ They hoped to draw all the best French troops to its defence, destroy them, and then carry out a final offensive to win the war.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read that aim carefully, because it is the purest statement of attritional thinking in the whole war. The objective was not the town. The objective was the French army, and the town was merely the bait that would compel its presence. ⚠️ An answer that says "the Germans attacked Verdun to capture it" has missed the only interesting thing about the battle.

✅ **Fact:** the French under Petain defended stubbornly, and in June the Germans had to abandon the attack. ✅ The French lost heavily - about 315,000 men, as the Germans intended - but so did the Germans themselves, with over 280,000 men killed and no territorial gains to show for it.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the design failed on its own terms. A plan to exchange losses favourably produced losses that were not favourable, against a defender who did not break. ⚠️ Verdun is therefore evidence for two conclusions at once: that attrition was deliberate, and that its arithmetic was not controllable by the side that initiated it.

### 3. The Somme: three aims, and the aim that is usually forgotten

✅ **Fact:** the Battle of the Somme was a series of attacks, mainly by the British, beginning on 1 July and lasting through to November. ✅ Its three aims were to relieve pressure on the French at Verdun; to take over more of the trench line as the French army weakened; and to keep the Germans fully committed so that they would be unable to risk sending reinforcements to the eastern front against Russia.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third aim is the one scripts omit, and it is the one that makes the Somme intelligible as coalition warfare. ⚠️ A British offensive in Picardy was in part a service rendered to Russia, and the coalition's ability to force the enemy to fight simultaneously on two fronts is one of the standing reasons the Central Powers lost. ⚠️ Lesson 13 will use this again.

✅ **Fact:** the attack began disastrously. British troops found themselves walking into deadly machine-gun fire; on the very first day 20,000 were killed and 60,000 injured. ✅ Haig, the British Commander-in-Chief, did not call off the attack; it continued at intervals for over four months.

✅ **Fact:** at the end of it all the Allies had made only limited advances varying between a few hundred yards and seven miles, along a thirty-mile front.

✅ **Fact:** the real importance of the battle was the blow to German morale, as they realised that Britain - where conscription was introduced for the first time in May 1916 - was a military power to be reckoned with.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice the two things that sentence does. It identifies the battle's actual effect as **psychological and political** rather than territorial, and it introduces conscription as the mechanism by which a liberal state with a small professional army converted itself into a continental military power. ⚠️ That second point belongs to the total-war argument of Lesson 9 as much as to the battle.

✅ **Fact:** losses on both sides, killed or wounded, were appalling: Germans 650,000; British 418,000; French 194,000.

### 4. The numbers discipline, stated once and applied everywhere

⚠️ This is the point at which the session's rule on figures must be made explicit, because the rest of the topic depends on it.

| Rule | Why |
|---|---|
| Use only figures recorded in the sources used here | ⚠️ Casualty figures for this war are compiled on different definitions and differ between authorities; invented or half-remembered numbers are the most easily detected error in an answer |
| State the basis where the source states it | ✅ The Somme figures are recorded as "killed or wounded"; ✅ the German Verdun figure is recorded as "killed". These are not the same measure and must not be compared as though they were |
| Introduce figures with a qualifier | ⚠️ "On the figures given in the standard accounts" costs seven words and protects the whole answer |
| Never total, average or extrapolate | ⚠️ Adding recorded figures together produces a number that no source supports |
| Prefer the argument to the arithmetic | ⚠️ A single figure used to prove a point is worth more than five figures used to decorate one |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the discipline is not merely defensive. The most effective use of a casualty figure in an answer is comparative and purposive - for example, that Verdun cost the Germans over 280,000 killed *for no territorial gain*, which is the evidence that attrition consumed its initiator as well as its target.

### 5. The commanders: the charge, and the reply

✅ **Fact:** the Allied generals, especially Haig, came under severe criticism for persisting with suicidal frontal attacks. ✅ In spite of the failures and the appalling casualties, both British and French generals remained convinced that mass infantry charges - the "big push" - were the only way to make a breakthrough, and none of them showed any sign of producing alternative tactics.

✅ **Fact:** the phrase that carries the charge has a recorded origin: it was after one of the disastrous attacks in 1915 that a German officer remarked that the British army were "lions led by donkeys". ✅ For the majority of historians Haig became the epitome of Allied incompetence and lack of imagination, and ✅ W. J. Laffin went so far as to title his book *British Butchers and Bunglers of World War 1* (1988), for whom the chief "donkey" was Haig.

✅ **Fact:** the reply is equally recorded, and it is not a whitewash. ✅ J. P. Harris, in *Douglas Haig and the First World War* (2008), is more balanced: he argues that Haig found it difficult to cope with the unprecedented situation on the western front, misjudged the strength of German forces, and was slow to see beyond the "big push", and must therefore bear much of the responsibility for the massive casualties - but that he did eventually show himself receptive to new techniques and strategies and played a vital role in the 1918 campaign. ✅ John Terraine presented an early defence in *Douglas Haig: The Educated Soldier* (1963), and ✅ Gary Sheffield went further in *The Chief: Douglas Haig and the British Army* (2011).

| Position | ✅ Evidence it rests on | ⚠️ What it concedes |
|---|---|---|
| Prosecution (Laffin) | Somme casualties; the persistence of the "big push"; "lions led by donkeys" | ⚠️ Concedes nothing; treats the record as a moral indictment |
| Balanced revision (Harris) | Unprecedented conditions; misjudgement of German strength; slowness beyond the "big push"; later receptiveness; the 1918 role | ⚠️ Concedes much of the prosecution case on 1916-17 while denying the caricature |
| Strong defence (Terraine, Sheffield) | Army reform before 1914; transformation of the British Expeditionary Force 1916-18; the wearing-down function of 1916-17; the 1918 generalship | ⚠️ Concedes the heavy losses and rests the case on the outcome |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the UPSC-safe verdict is the balanced one, and it can be stated in one sentence: Haig can be criticised for costly attritional methods, but the later historiography does not support a one-dimensional caricature. ⚠️ Lesson 13 gives Sheffield's four-part case in full, where it belongs, because it is an argument about *victory*, not about 1916.

### 6. The political consequence: a new kind of war government

✅ **Fact:** the horrors of the Somme contributed to the fall of the British prime minister, Asquith, who resigned in 1916 after criticism of British tactics mounted.

✅ **Fact:** Lloyd George took over as prime minister in December 1916, and his contribution to the Allied war effort and the defeat of the Central Powers was invaluable. ✅ Already as Minister of Munitions since May 1915 he had improved the supply of shells and machine-guns, encouraged the development of new weapons - the Stokes light mortar and the tank - which Kitchener, as Minister of War, had turned down, and taken control of mines, factories and railways so that the war effort could be properly centralised.

✅ **Fact:** as prime minister during 1917 he set up a small war cabinet so that quick decisions could be taken; he brought shipping and agriculture under government control; he introduced the Ministry of National Service to organise the mobilisation of men into the army; and he played an important part in the adoption of the convoy system.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is one of the most important paragraphs in the whole topic, and it is usually skipped because it is not a battle. ⚠️ It records the moment at which a liberal parliamentary state took direct control of mines, factories, railways, shipping and agriculture, created a ministry to allocate human beings between the army and industry, and concentrated executive decision in a small emergency body. ⚠️ That is the total-war state, described in a single career. Lesson 9 generalises it; Lesson 22 shows what parts of it survived the peace.

### 7. The east in 1916: the offensive that nearly broke Austria-Hungary

✅ **Fact:** in June 1916 the Russians under Brusilov attacked the Austrians, in response to a plea from Britain and France for some action to divert German attention away from Verdun. ✅ They managed to break the front and advanced a hundred miles, taking 400,000 prisoners and large amounts of equipment.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two things follow immediately. First, the offensive is the decisive proof that the trench condition was a western-front condition: no western attack in the entire war advanced a hundred miles. Second, the offensive was itself an act of coalition service, requested to relieve Verdun - which, with the Somme's third aim, shows that by 1916 the Allies were coordinating pressure across three fronts.

✅ **Fact:** the Austrians were demoralised, but the strain was exhausting the Russians as well.

✅ **Fact:** the Romanians invaded Austria in August, but the Germans swiftly came to the Austrians' rescue, occupied the whole of Romania and seized her wheat and oil supplies.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Romanian episode is a compressed lesson in coalition warfare. A neutral joined the side that appeared to be winning after Brusilov's success, was overrun within months, and delivered to the enemy precisely the two commodities - grain and oil - that a blockaded power most needed. ⚠️ It is also a reminder that the blockade of Germany was leaky at the edges: conquest was one way to defeat an economic siege.

✅ **Fact:** the account's own summing-up of the year is that the events of 1916 did contribute towards the eventual Allied victory, and that Hindenburg admitted in his memoirs that the Germans could not have survived many more campaigns with heavy losses like those at Verdun and the Somme.

### 8. Two hard questions about 1916

**The first question.** ⚠️ If attrition worked, why is it remembered as a catastrophe?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Because "worked" and "was worth it" are different judgements, and the sources support the first more clearly than the second. ✅ The account states both that the events of 1916 contributed to the eventual victory and that tens of thousands of lives were sacrificed for no apparent gain, and it records Hindenburg's admission on one side and the "lions led by donkeys" charge on the other. ⚠️ A confident answer reports the strategic finding and the political and moral cost as two separate findings, and refuses to collapse them.

**The second question.** ⚠️ Is it fair to judge commanders who faced a genuinely unprecedented problem?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Fairness requires a distinction rather than an acquittal. ⚠️ The existence of the deadlock is a technical condition for which commanders bear no responsibility. ⚠️ The persistence with a single method after repeated demonstration of its failure is a judgement for which they do. ✅ Harris's position occupies exactly that middle ground: much of the responsibility for the massive casualties, and a vital role in the 1918 campaign.

**The unresolved point.** ⚠️ Whether the German Verdun figure and the Allied Somme figures can be compared at all is uncertain, since the account records the former as killed and the latter as killed or wounded. ⚠️ This session does not compare them and no answer should.

### 9. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the demand map records that a question on attrition tests the technology-tactics mismatch rather than incompetence, and that the correct treatment of 1916 is as deliberate strategy rather than as failure.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "Verdun and the Somme were not failures of strategy but expressions of one." Critically examine. (Directive: critically examine. Ceiling: 250 words.)
> ⚠️ Discuss the view that the generalship of the First World War has been judged more harshly than the evidence warrants. (Directive: discuss. Ceiling: 250 words.)

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Open with the attritional proposition in one sentence and then use Verdun as the German version and the Somme as the Allied version, giving each its stated aim. ⚠️ Use exactly two figures, each with the qualifier and each doing argumentative work - the German loss at Verdun for no territorial gain, and the Somme's first day. ⚠️ Close with Hindenburg's admission, which converts a moral catastrophe into a strategic finding, and then state the cost explicitly so that the answer is not read as an apology.

### Traps

- Saying the Germans attacked Verdun in order to capture it. ✅ The aim was to draw in and destroy the best French troops.
- Omitting the Somme's third aim - preventing German reinforcement of the east - which is what makes it coalition warfare.
- Comparing "killed" figures with "killed or wounded" figures.
- Inventing a total casualty figure for either battle or for the war.
- Treating Lloyd George's administrative record as a digression. It is the total-war state in miniature.
- Forgetting Brusilov. A hundred-mile advance in 1916 refutes any claim that the whole war was static.
- Forgetting that Romania's grain and oil went to Germany, partially relieving the blockade.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ Attrition defined: destroying the enemy's army where it stands and accepting comparable losses, on the calculation that he exhausts first.
2. ✅ Verdun: German attack from February 1916 under Falkenhayn; the aim was to draw in and destroy the best French troops; Petain defended; the Germans abandoned the attack in June.
3. ✅ Recorded cost at Verdun: French about 315,000; Germans over 280,000 killed, with no territorial gains.
4. ✅ Somme: 1 July to November 1916, mainly British; three aims - relieve Verdun, take over more of the line, prevent German reinforcement of the east.
5. ✅ First day: 20,000 killed and 60,000 injured; Haig did not call off the attack; it continued at intervals for over four months.
6. ✅ Result: advances of a few hundred yards to seven miles along a thirty-mile front; the real importance was the blow to German morale.
7. ✅ British conscription was introduced for the first time in May 1916.
8. ✅ Recorded Somme losses, killed or wounded: Germans 650,000; British 418,000; French 194,000.
9. ✅ "Lions led by donkeys" was a German officer's remark after a disastrous attack in 1915; Laffin 1988 for the prosecution; Harris 2008 balanced; Terraine 1963 and Sheffield 2011 for the defence.
10. ✅ Asquith resigned in 1916; Lloyd George became prime minister in December 1916; as Minister of Munitions from May 1915 he had improved shell and machine-gun supply, backed the Stokes light mortar and the tank which Kitchener had refused, and taken control of mines, factories and railways.
11. ✅ As prime minister he created a small war cabinet, took control of shipping and agriculture, created the Ministry of National Service and pressed the convoy system.
12. ✅ Brusilov, June 1916: requested by Britain and France to relieve Verdun; broke the front, advanced a hundred miles, took 400,000 prisoners.
13. ✅ Romania invaded Austria in August 1916, was occupied by Germany, and lost her wheat and oil supplies to the occupier.
14. ✅ Hindenburg's memoirs admit the Germans could not have survived many more campaigns with losses like Verdun and the Somme.

### Concept check

**Question:** A question asks whether the strategy of attrition can be defended. Construct the strongest defence and the strongest indictment from the recorded evidence, and state the verdict you would actually write.

**Model answer:** The defence rests on three recorded findings. First, attrition was adopted only after alternatives had been tried and had failed: the western attacks of 1915 produced no decisive breakthrough, and the attempt to turn the strategic flank at Gallipoli ended in withdrawal in December 1915. Second, it had an explicit logic rather than being mere persistence: the German attack at Verdun from February 1916 was designed to draw in and destroy the best French troops so that a final offensive could win the war, and the Somme was launched with three stated aims including preventing German reinforcement of the eastern front. Third, the proposition was vindicated by the enemy's own testimony: Hindenburg admitted in his memoirs that the Germans could not have survived many more campaigns with heavy losses like those at Verdun and the Somme. The indictment rests on three equally recorded findings. The arithmetic was not controllable: Verdun cost the Germans over 280,000 killed with no territorial gains, so the initiator of the attritional battle suffered comparably to its target. The method was not revised: British and French generals remained convinced that mass infantry charges were the only route to breakthrough, and none showed any sign of producing alternative tactics. And the results were derisory in their own terms: advances of a few hundred yards to seven miles along a thirty-mile front after four months. The verdict I would write is that attrition was a rational residue rather than a strategic choice, that it achieved its cumulative purpose, and that the commanders are answerable not for adopting it but for the slowness with which they altered the method by which they applied it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Choosing between "attrition worked" and "attrition was a catastrophe" as though they were alternatives. They are both supported, they answer different questions, and an answer that holds them together while separating the strategic finding from the human cost is doing precisely what a "critically examine" directive requires.

*Verdun and the Somme were the war's most visible battles. The front on which it was arguably decided was invisible, and it had been operating since the first week of the war.*

## Lesson 6 - The war at sea: blockade, Jutland, the submarine gamble and the convoy answer

Progress: 6/25 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the three Allied naval aims; the caution of both fleets and the Dreadnought arithmetic; the destruction of German surface raiders; the blockade and the neutral-rights problem; the Lusitania and the American reaction; Jutland's tactical and strategic verdicts; unrestricted submarine warfare and the April 1917 peak; the convoy system and why it worked; why the submarine campaign was a strategic error

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's record of public expectation of a Trafalgar-style fleet battle and the caution of both navies; Jellicoe as the only man who could have lost the war in an afternoon; sixteen German Dreadnoughts against twenty-seven British; the three Allied naval aims; the Battle of the Falkland Islands and the destruction of German surface forces by the end of 1914; the blockade and the American objection to the stopping and searching of neutral ships; the Lusitania sinking, which that account places in April 1915 and describes as leaving 128 Americans among almost two thousand dead, together with the fact that she was armed and carrying munitions; Bethmann's toning-down of the campaign; the Battle of Jutland on 31 May 1916 with eleven German and fourteen British losses and the High Seas Fleet's retirement to Kiel; unrestricted submarine warfare from January 1917 with 430 ships lost in April 1917 and Britain down to six weeks' corn supply; and Lloyd George's insistence on convoys
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage for this session is held in Lesson 7
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two blockades, run on opposite principles, with opposite results

| Axis | **The British blockade of Germany** | **The German submarine blockade of Britain** |
|---|---|---|
| Instrument | Surface warships controlling the sea's surface | Submarines operating beneath it |
| Method | ✅ Stopping and searching all neutral ships and confiscating goods suspected of being intended for enemy hands | ✅ Attempting to sink all enemy and neutral merchant ships in the Atlantic |
| Effect on persons | ✅ Did not interfere with the safety of passengers and crews | ✅ Certainly did |
| Legal and diplomatic cost | ✅ The USA objected strongly, since they were anxious to continue trading with both sides | ✅ Brought a great power into the war against its user |
| Speed of effect | ⚠️ Slow, cumulative, invisible - years | ⚠️ Fast, dramatic, measurable - months |
| Peak of success | ⚠️ Food shortages among the German civilian population and crippled exports | ✅ April 1917: 430 ships lost; Britain down to about six weeks' corn supply |
| The counter | ⚠️ Conquest of food-producing territory, such as Romania's wheat and oil | ✅ The convoy system, which drastically reduced losses |
| Outcome | ✅ Decisive; listed first among the reasons the Central Powers lost | ✅ Failed; and the campaign itself was a mistake because it brought the USA into the war |

*The paired table is the lesson. ⚠️ Both belligerents attempted to starve the other's civilian population into surrender; one succeeded and one failed. ⚠️ The difference was not moral, and an answer that explains it morally has explained nothing. It was a difference of legal form, diplomatic cost, and the availability of a technical counter.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Britain's advantage was that it controlled the surface of the sea. Germany's problem was that it could not take that control away. ⚠️ What follows from those two sentences is the entire naval war: Germany could not fight for the surface, so it went under it; and going under it solved the military problem while creating a political one that destroyed Germany.

✅ **Fact:** the general public in Germany and Britain expected a series of naval battles between the rival Dreadnought fleets, something like Trafalgar. ✅ Both sides were cautious and dared not risk any action which might result in the loss of their main fleets. ✅ The British Admiral Jellicoe was particularly cautious; Churchill said he "was the only man on either side who could have lost the war in an afternoon". ✅ Nor were the Germans anxious for a confrontation, because they had only sixteen of the latest Dreadnoughts against twenty-seven British.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that Churchill remark is the single most efficient way to explain naval caution in an answer. A fleet that is losing can be replaced only over years; a fleet that exists, even in harbour, continues to impose a strategic effect. ⚠️ The pre-war naval race therefore produced instruments too valuable to risk - which is an irony worth one sentence.

### 2. The three Allied naval aims, and their fulfilment

✅ The Allies aimed to use their navies in three ways:

1. to blockade the Central Powers, preventing goods from entering or leaving, slowly starving them out;
2. to keep trade routes open between Britain, her empire and the rest of the world, so that the Allies themselves would not starve;
3. to transport British troops to the continent and keep them supplied via the Channel ports.

✅ **Fact:** the British were successful in carrying out these aims. They went into action against German units stationed abroad and destroyed one of the main German squadrons at the Battle of the Falkland Islands. ✅ By the end of 1914 nearly all German armed surface ships had been destroyed, apart from the main fleet, which did not venture out of the Heligoland Bight, and the squadron blockading the Baltic to cut off supplies to Russia.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third aim is the one that never appears in scripts and is arguably the most important. ⚠️ Every British soldier in France arrived by sea and was supplied by sea across a narrow channel that a German surface fleet never contested. ⚠️ Without that, the western front as it existed is unimaginable - and the Channel ports were kept available, as Lesson 2 showed, by the defence of Ypres in 1914.

### 3. The blockade and the neutral problem

✅ **Fact:** Britain was trying to prevent the Germans from using the neutral Scandinavian and Dutch ports to break the blockade; this involved stopping and searching all neutral ships and confiscating goods suspected of being intended for enemy hands. ✅ The USA objected strongly, since they were anxious to continue trading with both sides.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is important and usually missed. The British blockade was itself a serious violation of the neutral-rights regime that Britain had historically defended, and it generated real Anglo-American friction. ⚠️ What saved it politically was the difference recorded by the account: the British blockade did not interfere with the safety of passengers and crews, while German tactics certainly did. ⚠️ In other words, the two blockades were compared by their *visible human cost* rather than by their legality or their eventual lethality - a distinction that decided which belligerent the United States joined.

### 4. The Lusitania: an episode that must be stated completely

✅ **Fact:** at first the Germans respected neutral shipping and passenger liners, but it was soon clear that the submarine blockade was not effective - partly because they had insufficient U-boats and partly because of problems of identification: the British tried to fool the Germans by flying neutral flags and by using passenger liners to transport arms and ammunition.

✅ **Fact:** the British liner *Lusitania* was sunk by a torpedo attack off the southern Irish coast on **7 May 1915**. ✅ In fact the *Lusitania* was armed and carrying vast quantities of weapons and ammunition, as the Germans knew - hence their claim that the sinking was not just an act of barbarism against defenceless civilians.

✅ **Fact:** of roughly **1,959 people aboard**, **1,198 were killed**, and **128 Americans were among the dead**. ✅ President Wilson therefore found that the USA would have to take sides to protect her trade. ✅ For the time being, American protests caused Bethmann to tone down the submarine campaign, making it even less effective.

⚠️ **Date and figure correction, stated once and used throughout.** The core printed account places the sinking in April 1915 and writes of "almost two thousand dead". Both are source-inherited errors, and the second is the more dangerous because it conflates two different quantities. The sinking was on **7 May 1915**; the figure of roughly **1,959 is the number aboard**, not the number killed; and the recorded death toll is **1,198**, of whom **128 were Americans**. ⚠️ Never write "almost two thousand dead" for the *Lusitania*, and never imply that the American deaths were a proportion of two thousand.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the episode must be stated with both halves or not at all. An answer that gives only the sinking has written propaganda; an answer that gives only the cargo has written apologetics. ⚠️ The historically useful point is the third one: a belligerent that cannot distinguish combatant from non-combatant traffic, in a war where the enemy deliberately blurs the distinction, will eventually commit acts that convert neutrals into enemies. ⚠️ That is a structural problem of commerce warfare, and it recurs in 1917 with far greater consequences.

### 5. Jutland: the battle that decided nothing and settled everything

✅ **Fact:** the Battle of Jutland, 31 May 1916, was the main event at sea during 1916; it was the only time in the entire war that the main battle fleets emerged and engaged each other, and the result was indecisive.

✅ **Fact:** the German Admiral von Scheer tried to lure part of the British fleet out from its base so that that section could be destroyed by the numerically superior Germans. ✅ More British ships came out than he had anticipated, and after the two fleets had shelled each other on and off for several hours, the Germans decided to retire to base, firing torpedoes as they went.

✅ **Fact:** on balance the Germans could claim that they had won the battle, since they lost only eleven ships to Britain's fourteen. ✅ The real importance of the battle lay in the fact that the Germans had failed to destroy British sea power: the German High Seas Fleet stayed in Kiel for the rest of the war, leaving Britain's control of the surface complete.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Jutland is the standard examination example of the difference between a **tactical** and a **strategic** result, and it is worth using explicitly. Tactically, the Germans could claim a win on the exchange of ships. Strategically, they lost everything that mattered: the blockade continued, the Channel supply line continued, and the High Seas Fleet ceased to be a factor. ⚠️ A fleet that cannot leave port is a fleet that has been defeated without being destroyed - and, as Lesson 12 shows, the order that finally sent it out in 1918 produced a mutiny rather than a battle.

✅ **Fact:** the account draws the direct causal link: in desperation at the food shortages caused by the British blockade, the Germans embarked on unrestricted submarine warfare, and this was to have fatal results for them.

### 6. Unrestricted submarine warfare: the calculated gamble

✅ **Fact:** the campaign began in January 1917. Since the Germans had been concentrating on the production of U-boats since Jutland, it was extremely effective. ✅ They attempted to sink all enemy and neutral merchant ships in the Atlantic; although they knew that this was likely to bring the USA into the war, they hoped that Britain and France would be starved into surrender before the Americans could make any vital contribution.

✅ **Fact:** they almost did it. The peak of German success came in April 1917, when 430 ships were lost; Britain was down to about six weeks' corn supply; and although the USA came into the war in April, it was bound to be several months before their help became effective.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the only moment in the war at which Germany came close to a decisive result by its own action, and it deserves to be stated as a genuine calculation rather than as recklessness. The decision was: accept a new and enormous enemy in exchange for a chance to win before that enemy arrives. ⚠️ It was a race against a clock, and the clock was beaten by an administrative reform.

### 7. The convoy: why an old idea worked

✅ **Fact:** the situation was saved by Lloyd George, who insisted that the Admiralty adopt a convoy system. ✅ A convoy was a large number of merchant ships sailing together so that they could be protected by escorting warships. ✅ This drastically reduced losses and meant that the German gamble had failed.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the mechanism is worth one sentence because it is counter-intuitive and therefore memorable. Concentrating merchant ships does not make them a larger target in any useful sense; it makes the ocean emptier. A submarine of that era searched visually over a small radius, and a convoy occupies scarcely more sea than a single ship while removing dozens of independent targets from the shipping lanes - and it brings the escorts to the place where the enemy must come. ⚠️ The resistance to convoy was institutional rather than technical, which is why the political intervention mattered.

✅ **Fact:** the account's verdict is explicit on both counts: the submarine campaign was important because it brought the USA into the war, and the British navy, helped by the Americans, played a vitally important role in the defeat of the Central Powers; by the middle of 1918 it had achieved its three aims. ✅ Among the reasons the Central Powers lost, the account records that the German submarine campaign failed in the face of convoys protected by British, American and Japanese destroyers, and that the campaign itself was a mistake because it brought the USA into the war.

⚠️ Note the last detail: **Japanese** destroyers. ⚠️ It is a small fact that does disproportionate work, because it is concrete evidence that this was a world war in its conduct and not only in its causes.

### 8. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ Is it not anachronistic to call the blockade decisive when Germany was defeated on land in 1918?

**The reply.** ⚠️ The claim is cumulative, not exclusive. ✅ The account lists Allied sea power second among the reasons for defeat and describes it as decisive: enforcing the deadly blockade, which caused desperate food shortages among the civilian population and crippled exports, while making sure that the Allied armies were fully supplied. ⚠️ The land defeat of 1918 and the blockade are the same explanation viewed at two speeds: an army whose home front is short of food, whose allies are collapsing and whose population is in revolt is an army that surrenders when it is beaten in the field, instead of retreating and continuing.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Was the submarine decision not simply immoral, and should it be analysed as a calculation at all?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Both are true and they are different questions. ⚠️ Deliberately sinking neutral merchant shipping without warning was a violation of the law of the sea as then understood, and the human consequences were the reason it produced the diplomatic result it did. ⚠️ It was also, simultaneously, a rational gamble with stated odds: starve Britain before the Americans arrive. ⚠️ An answer that reports only the first has not explained why a government took the decision; an answer that reports only the second has not explained why it failed.

**The residual.** ⚠️ How far the blockade's effects on German civilian mortality can be quantified is contested and no figure is asserted here.

### 9. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the demand map records that a question on why the Central Powers lost tests cumulative causation across fronts, sea and economy, and that answering with one battle or one ally is the wrong response.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The First World War was won at sea." Examine this statement. (Directive: examine. Ceiling: 250 words.)
> ⚠️ Analyse the reasons for the failure of Germany's submarine campaign. (Directive: analyse. Ceiling: 150 words.)

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Build the answer from the paired-blockade table: same objective, opposite instruments, opposite outcomes. ⚠️ Use exactly two figures - April 1917's 430 ships and six weeks' corn supply - because together they establish how close the gamble came. ⚠️ Give Jutland one sentence on the tactical-strategic distinction, not a narrative. ⚠️ Close with the double verdict: sea power was decisive for the Allies, and the submarine campaign was the single largest strategic error of the war because it recruited the enemy that made Allied victory certain.

### Traps

- Calling Jutland a German victory without the strategic qualification, or a British victory without the tactical one.
- Omitting that the *Lusitania* was armed and carrying munitions, or omitting that 128 Americans were among the dead.
- Writing "almost two thousand dead" for the *Lusitania*. ✅ Roughly 1,959 were aboard and 1,198 were killed; those aboard and those killed are different quantities.
- Dating the sinking to April 1915. ✅ It was 7 May 1915.
- Saying the USA entered the war because of the *Lusitania*. ✅ The sinking was on 7 May 1915; the entry was in April 1917, after unrestricted warfare resumed.
- Treating the convoy as a technological solution. It was an organisational one, adopted over Admiralty resistance.
- Forgetting the third naval aim - transporting and supplying the army - which underwrites the western front.
- Describing the British blockade as legally unproblematic. ✅ The USA objected strongly.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Both fleets were cautious; Churchill on Jellicoe: the only man on either side who could have lost the war in an afternoon; sixteen German Dreadnoughts against twenty-seven British.
2. ✅ Three Allied naval aims: blockade the Central Powers; keep Allied trade routes open; transport and supply the army through the Channel ports.
3. ✅ Falkland Islands destroyed a main German squadron; by the end of 1914 nearly all German armed surface ships were destroyed apart from the main fleet in the Heligoland Bight and the Baltic squadron.
4. ✅ The blockade required stopping and searching all neutral ships; the USA objected strongly.
5. ✅ *Lusitania*, **7 May 1915**: armed and carrying munitions as the Germans knew; roughly 1,959 aboard, **1,198 killed**, 128 Americans among the dead; Wilson concluded the USA would have to take sides; Bethmann toned the campaign down.
6. ✅ Jutland, 31 May 1916: the only fleet action; indecisive; Germans lost eleven ships to Britain's fourteen; the High Seas Fleet stayed in Kiel for the rest of the war.
7. ⚠️ Jutland is the model case of a tactical result that is strategically irrelevant.
8. ✅ Unrestricted submarine warfare began January 1917, after U-boat production had been concentrated since Jutland.
9. ✅ Peak April 1917: 430 ships lost; Britain down to about six weeks' corn supply.
10. ✅ Lloyd George insisted on the convoy system; losses fell drastically; the gamble failed.
11. ✅ Convoys were protected by British, American and Japanese destroyers.
12. ✅ The campaign was a mistake because it brought the USA into the war; by mid-1918 the navy had achieved its three aims.
13. ✅ The blockade caused desperate food shortages among German civilians and crippled exports, and is listed among the decisive causes of Central Power defeat.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two belligerents attempted to starve each other's civilian population. Explain why one attempt succeeded and the other destroyed its user, without using a moral premise.

**Model answer:** The difference lies in legal form, visible human cost, and the availability of a counter. Britain's blockade was conducted on the surface by warships that stopped and searched neutral vessels and confiscated goods suspected of being intended for enemy hands. It was legally contentious and the United States objected strongly, since it wanted to trade with both sides; but it did not interfere with the safety of passengers and crews, so its costs fell on property and on German civilians slowly, invisibly and at a distance. Germany could not contest the surface, because nearly all its armed surface ships were destroyed by the end of 1914 and, after Jutland on 31 May 1916, its High Seas Fleet stayed in Kiel; so it went beneath the surface, where a submarine could not stop, search or rescue. Its blockade therefore had to be conducted by sinking without warning, which killed neutrals visibly and immediately. That is why the two campaigns were judged differently by the neutral whose decision mattered: 128 Americans were among the 1,198 killed when the *Lusitania* was sunk on 7 May 1915, and Wilson concluded that the United States would have to take sides to protect her trade. The military logic of the unrestricted campaign from January 1917 was nonetheless sound on its own terms: 430 ships were lost in April 1917 and Britain was down to about six weeks' corn supply. What defeated it was an organisational counter rather than a technical one - the convoy system, adopted on Lloyd George's insistence and escorted by British, American and Japanese destroyers - which drastically reduced losses. So the campaign failed militarily and succeeded in recruiting a decisive enemy, which is why the account calls it a mistake.

**Misconception to avoid:** Explaining the different outcomes by the different characters of the belligerents. Both were attempting economic warfare against civilians; the asymmetry was in the instruments available to each and in how the resulting deaths appeared to a neutral public. Reducing that to national temperament removes every mechanism from the answer.

*Sea power made the Ottoman fronts possible for the Allies and the Ottoman alliance valuable to Germany. It is on those fronts that this topic encounters the war's gravest crime, and the discipline for writing about it.*

## Lesson 7 - The Ottoman fronts, the Arab revolt and the Armenian catastrophe

Progress: 7/25 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: why the Ottoman entry mattered strategically; the Caucasus, Mesopotamian and Palestine fronts; Baghdad and Jerusalem; the Arab revolt and the expectation of independence; the incompatible wartime undertakings; the destruction of the Ottoman Armenian population in 1915 - dates, instruments, named authority, the contested characterisation and the discipline for writing about it; the armistice of Mudros; what this front bequeaths to the settlement

Lawrence; Arab unhappiness at the talk of establishing a Jewish national home in Palestine; and the Sevres provisions transferring Syria to France and Palestine, Iraq and Transjordan to Britain as mandates
