*The chart is deliberately built as two columns rather than one, because the two questions are asked separately and answered from overlapping but distinct evidence. ⚠️ Note that the causes in the right-hand column are of five different kinds - structural, maritime, self-inflicted, material and political. ⚠️ An answer that sorts them by kind is making an argument; an answer that lists them in the order it remembers them is reciting.*

### 1. Why the war lasted so long: the five recorded reasons

✅ **Fact:** when the war started the majority of people on both sides believed it would be over by Christmas, but Britain's Secretary for War, Lord Kitchener, himself a successful general, told the cabinet - much to its collective dismay - that it would last nearer three years than three months. ✅ He did not live to see the end of the war, having been drowned in 1916 on his way to Russia when his ship struck a mine, but he was one of the few who had judged the situation correctly.

⚠️ That detail is worth one clause in an answer because it establishes that the short-war expectation was an assumption rather than a certainty, and that it was contradicted at the time by a competent professional.

✅ **Fact:** the account gives five reasons for the duration.

1. ✅ **The two sides were fairly evenly balanced.**
2. ✅ **It quickly became a global conflict.** Other countries not in the original alliance systems decided to join - some because they saw a chance to gain new territory, others after waiting to see which side looked more likely to win. ✅ Italy joined in May 1915, Romania in August 1916, the USA in April 1917, and Japan; Turkey joined the Central Powers in November 1914 and Bulgaria in October 1915. ✅ Troops from the British Empire - India, Australia, New Zealand, Canada and South Africa - all played their part, and the fighting spread into the eastern Mediterranean, Asia and Africa.
3. ✅ **War aims were strongly held and incompatible.** ✅ The Germans, anxious to protect themselves from becoming encircled, aimed to take territory from Poland in the east and Belgium in the west as buffer zones against Russia and France. ✅ The French were obsessed with taking back Alsace-Lorraine, lost in 1871. ✅ The British would never allow Belgium, so near their coast, to be controlled by a hostile power like Germany. ✅ Austria-Hungary was desperate to preserve its empire against the ambitions of Serbia. ✅ Right from the beginning these competing aims meant that it would be almost impossible to reach an acceptable negotiated solution.
4. ✅ **The tactical condition favoured the defender.** ✅ The weapons available to the Central Powers as they defended their trenches were more deadly than those available to the attackers.
5. ✅ **Propaganda sustained morale.** ✅ Newspapers, posters, films and advertisements directed at all classes made populations proud of their own country while spreading horror stories about the enemy; ✅ in Germany, despite food shortages, labour unrest and general war-weariness, public support for the war continued.

✅ **Fact:** the account adds that the defeat of Russia encouraged the German generals to continue the struggle and to launch the last desperate attempt of spring 1918, and concludes that the combination of all these factors meant there would have to be a fight to the finish until one side was either overrun and occupied or so completely exhausted that it could not carry on fighting.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third reason is the one that converts this from a military into a political explanation, and it is the one most often omitted. ⚠️ Wars end by negotiation when both sides can imagine an acceptable settlement. ⚠️ Here, each belligerent's minimum was another's unacceptable maximum: Germany's buffer zones required permanent Belgian and Polish subjection, which was Britain's and Russia's casus belli; Austria-Hungary's survival required Serbia's subordination, which Russia would not permit. ⚠️ A war whose participants' minimum terms are mutually exclusive cannot be ended except by exhaustion, and that is a sentence worth committing to memory.

### 2. Why the Central Powers lost: the eight recorded reasons, sorted

⚠️ Sorting is the whole technique here, because the recorded list is long and a list loses marks that an argument earns.

| Kind of cause | ✅ Recorded reason | ⚠️ Weight and function |
|---|---|---|
| **Structural** | Once the plan for a rapid German victory had failed, it was bound to be a strain facing war on two fronts | ⚠️ The standing burden that every other cause compounded; present from September 1914 |
| **Maritime** | Allied sea power was decisive, enforcing the deadly blockade which caused desperate food shortages among the civilian population and crippled exports, while ensuring Allied armies were fully supplied | ⚠️ The slow cause. It does not win a battle; it decides whether a society can continue |
| **Self-inflicted** | The submarine campaign failed against convoys protected by British, American and Japanese destroyers, and was itself a mistake because it brought the USA into the war | ⚠️ The only cause that is a German *decision* rather than a German *condition*, which makes it the strongest single candidate for "the" reason |
| **Material** | US entry brought vast new resources, made up for Russia's departure, and meant the Allies could produce more war materials than the enemy - in the end decisive | ⚠️ Converts a war of endurance into an arithmetic the Central Powers cannot win |
| **Command and coalition** | Allied political leaders at the critical time - Lloyd George and Clemenceau - were probably more competent than those of the Central Powers; unity of command under Foch helped; Haig learned lessons from 1917 that proved crucial | ⚠️ Explains why Allied material superiority was actually converted into victory in 1918 rather than squandered |
| **Attritional strain** | The Germans lost their best troops in the 1918 offensive; replacements were young and inexperienced; American troops reached around two million; Spanish flu added to the difficulties; some 350,000 German troops surrendered in the last three months | ⚠️ The mechanism by which exhaustion became collapse |
| **Coalition failure** | Germany was badly let down by her allies; Bulgaria was defeated on 29 September 1918; Austria was defeated at Vittorio Veneto and Turkey surrendered in October | ⚠️ Shows that the alliance broke at its weakest member and propagated inward |
| **Political collapse** | The combination of military defeat and dire food shortages produced war-weariness, mutiny in the navy, destruction of army morale and revolution at home | ⚠️ The final cause, and the link between the blockade of 1914 and the republic of November 1918 |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the most defensible thesis available from this list is that the Central Powers were not defeated at a point but exhausted across a system - blockade, a second front, a failed submarine gamble and the arrival of American resources - and that the decisive German error was a decision rather than a defeat. ⚠️ If an answer must nominate one cause, the submarine campaign is the strongest nomination, because it is the only item that is simultaneously a military failure and a strategic self-injury, and because the account itself calls it a mistake.

### 3. The Haig rehabilitation, stated in full

⚠️ Lesson 5 gave the charge and the balance. This is where the strong defence belongs, because it is an argument about *victory* rather than about 1916.

✅ **Fact:** some historians believe the criticisms levelled at Haig are unfair. ✅ John Terraine was one of the first to present a defence, in *Douglas Haig: The Educated Soldier* (1963). ✅ Gary Sheffield went further in *The Chief: Douglas Haig and the British Army* (2011), arguing that, given that the British had no experience of trench warfare and were the junior partners to the French, Haig learned remarkably quickly and proved to be an imaginative commander.

✅ **Fact:** Sheffield's case rests on four contributions.

1. ✅ He took a leading part in reforming the army and preparing it for a major war before 1914.
2. ✅ Between 1916 and 1918 he was responsible for transforming the British Expeditionary Force from an inexperienced small force into a mass war-winning army.
3. ✅ His battles in 1916 and 1917 - the Somme, Arras and Third Ypres - though his troops suffered heavy losses, played a vital role in wearing down the Germans, whose losses were also heavy.
4. ✅ His generalship was a crucial component of the Allied victory in 1918: he had learned lessons about the effective use of tanks and about avoiding salients by using small groups of infantry attacking at different points along the trench line; his idea of transporting infantry in buses to accompany the cavalry was very effective; and there was eventually a great improvement in coordination between infantry, artillery and aerial observation.

✅ **Fact:** Sheffield's own summary is that "Douglas Haig might not have been the greatest military figure Britain has ever produced, but he was one of the most significant - and one of the most successful."

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the safe examination verdict is the one that holds the whole record: Haig can be criticised for costly attritional methods and for slowness in abandoning the "big push", and the later historiography does not support a one-dimensional caricature. ⚠️ Notice that the third of Sheffield's contributions is itself an attritional claim - that heavy losses in 1916-17 wore the enemy down - so the defence does not deny the cost; it re-describes the cost as a function. ⚠️ Whether that re-description is persuasive is precisely what a "critically examine" question wants the candidate to decide.

### 4. How to build a cumulative-causation answer

⚠️ Cumulative causation is easy to assert and hard to write, because the default form is a list. Three techniques convert it into an argument.

| Technique | ⚠️ How it works |
|---|---|
| **Sort by kind, not by date** | Structural, maritime, self-inflicted, material, command, attritional, coalition, political. The sorting itself is an analytical claim |
| **Rank openly** | Nominate a most important cause and say why - the submarine decision, because it is the only cause that is a choice; or sea power, because it is listed as decisive and operated for four years |
| **Show interaction** | State at least one chain rather than one cause: the blockade produced food shortage; food shortage produced the desperation that led to unrestricted submarine warfare; that brought in the USA; American resources produced material superiority; material superiority made the August method affordable |

⚠️ That chain is the single most valuable paragraph an answer on this question can contain, because it shows four recorded causes operating on one another rather than sitting side by side.

### 5. Pressure-testing the two answers

**The first pressure test.** ⚠️ Is the claim that the sides were "fairly evenly balanced" not contradicted by the Allies' far greater population, industrial capacity and imperial resources?

**The reply.** ⚠️ The balance was in *effective* rather than in *potential* strength, and that distinction resolves the apparent contradiction. ⚠️ The Central Powers held interior lines and could shift forces between fronts; Russia's enormous manpower was not matched by equipment; the Allied resources of empire and of the United States needed time, shipping and organisation before they could be applied. ⚠️ The account's own account of the defeat makes this explicit: American entry meant the Allies were *able to produce more war materials than the enemy*, which implies they were not doing so previously. ⚠️ Potential superiority became actual superiority in 1917-18, which is also when the war was decided.

**The second pressure test.** ⚠️ Does the emphasis on the blockade not sit uneasily with the emphasis on 1918's battles?

**The reply.** ⚠️ They are the same explanation at two speeds. ⚠️ The blockade determined the condition of the society and therefore of the army - food shortage, war-weariness, mutiny in the navy, revolution at home. ⚠️ The battles of 1918 determined the moment at which that condition became a request for an armistice. ⚠️ An army that is beaten in the field but whose society is intact retreats and continues; an army beaten in the field whose society is starving, whose allies have capitulated and whose sailors have mutinied asks for terms. ⚠️ Stating that relation is stronger than choosing between the two causes.

**The residual.** ⚠️ The relative weight of American material supply, American manpower and Allied tactical improvement in 1918 is not quantified in this session's evidence base and cannot be settled here.

### 6. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the demand map records two distinct demands and their traps: a "why did it become a war of attrition" question tests the technology-tactics mismatch and must not be answered with a battle list; a "why did the Central Powers lose" question tests cumulative causation across fronts, sea and economy and must not be answered with one battle or one ally.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Why did the First World War become a prolonged war of attrition rather than a short conflict? (Directive: explanatory "why". Ceiling: 250 words.)
> ⚠️ Account for the defeat of the Central Powers in the First World War. (Directive: account for. Ceiling: 250 words.)
> ⚠️ "The First World War was decided by economics rather than by generalship." Discuss. (Directive: discuss. Ceiling: 250 words.)

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the duration question, use the five reasons but lead with the war-aims point, because it is the political explanation and the others are conditions. ⚠️ For the defeat question, sort by kind, nominate one cause and defend the nomination, and include the four-step chain. ⚠️ For an economics-versus-generalship question, use the two-speeds reply in section 5, because it refuses a false choice while still delivering a verdict.

### Traps

- Listing reasons for defeat without sorting or ranking them.
- Omitting the war-aims reason for the war's duration, which is the only political one among the five.
- Claiming American troops alone won the war rather than American *resources* and production.
- Presenting Haig's rehabilitation as a full acquittal. ✅ Even the defence concedes heavy losses and re-describes them as attrition.
- Treating the blockade and the 1918 battles as alternatives rather than as the same cause at different speeds.
- Forgetting the coalition failure: Germany was let down by her allies, and they collapsed first.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Kitchener predicted nearer three years than three months and was one of the few who judged correctly; he drowned in 1916 when his ship struck a mine.
2. ✅ Duration, reason one: the two sides were fairly evenly balanced.
3. ✅ Duration, reason two: the conflict quickly became global; Italy 1915, Romania 1916, USA 1917 and Japan joined the Allies; Turkey 1914 and Bulgaria 1915 joined the Central Powers; imperial troops from India, Australia, New Zealand, Canada and South Africa fought.
4. ✅ Duration, reason three: incompatible war aims - German buffer zones in Poland and Belgium; French recovery of Alsace-Lorraine; British refusal to let Belgium fall to a hostile power; Habsburg survival against Serbia - made a negotiated solution almost impossible.
5. ✅ Duration, reason four: defending weapons were more deadly than attacking ones.
6. ✅ Duration, reason five: propaganda sustained morale through newspapers, posters, films and advertisements.
7. ✅ Russia's defeat encouraged the German generals to continue and to gamble in spring 1918.
8. ✅ Defeat, structural: the failure of the rapid-victory plan left Germany in a two-front war.
9. ✅ Defeat, maritime: Allied sea power was decisive; the blockade caused desperate civilian food shortages and crippled exports while keeping Allied armies supplied.
10. ✅ Defeat, self-inflicted: the submarine campaign failed against convoys escorted by British, American and Japanese destroyers and was itself a mistake because it brought in the USA.
11. ✅ Defeat, material: US entry brought vast new resources, compensated for Russia's departure and let the Allies out-produce the enemy, which in the end proved decisive.
12. ✅ Defeat, command: Lloyd George and Clemenceau were probably more competent than their opposite numbers; unity of command under Foch helped; Haig learned from 1917.
13. ✅ Sheffield's four Haig contributions: pre-1914 army reform; transformation of the British Expeditionary Force 1916-18; the wearing-down function of the Somme, Arras and Third Ypres; and the 1918 generalship, including tanks, avoidance of salients by small groups attacking at different points, infantry transported in buses, and infantry-artillery-air coordination.
14. ✅ Defeat, attritional strain: best troops lost in 1918; inexperienced replacements; around two million Americans; Spanish flu; some 350,000 German surrenders in the last three months.
15. ✅ Defeat, coalition: Bulgaria defeated 29 September 1918; Vittorio Veneto and the Turkish surrender in October.
16. ✅ Defeat, political: military defeat plus dire food shortages produced naval mutiny, destroyed army morale and revolution at home.
17. ⚠️ The four-step chain: blockade → food shortage → the submarine gamble → American entry → material superiority.

### Concept check

**Question:** Nominate the single most important reason for the defeat of the Central Powers, defend the nomination against the two strongest rival candidates, and show one causal chain linking at least four recorded causes.

**Model answer:** The strongest nomination is the resumption of unrestricted submarine warfare in January 1917, because it is the only item on the recorded list that is a German decision rather than a German condition, and because it failed twice over: it failed militarily against convoys escorted by British, American and Japanese destroyers, and the account explicitly calls the campaign itself a mistake because it brought the USA into the war. The two strongest rivals are sea power and the two-front burden. Sea power has an excellent claim, since the account calls it decisive: the blockade caused desperate food shortages among the German civilian population and crippled exports while keeping Allied armies fully supplied, and it operated continuously from 1914. But it is a condition the Central Powers could not have avoided once the plan for rapid victory failed, so it explains their weakness rather than their defeat. The two-front burden has a similar claim and the same limitation: it was a standing strain that every other cause compounded, but Germany survived it for three and a half years and was temporarily relieved of it when Russia withdrew in December 1917. The submarine decision is different in kind because it converted a survivable condition into an unwinnable one by recruiting the enemy whose resources decided the war. The chain runs as follows: Allied sea power imposed a blockade that produced desperate food shortages; desperation at those shortages led Germany to embark on unrestricted submarine warfare; that campaign brought the United States into the war in April 1917; American entry brought vast new resources, made up for Russia's departure, and let the Allies produce more war materials than the enemy, which in the end proved decisive.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that nominating one cause contradicts cumulative causation. It does not. Cumulative causation means no single cause is sufficient; it does not mean all causes are equal. An answer that ranks and justifies the ranking is stronger than one that declares everything important, and far stronger than one that picks a cause without defending the pick.

*The military argument is complete. Before the peace, there is a reckoning to state - and a discipline for stating it.*

## Lesson 14 - The reckoning: death tolls, the "lost generation" and the influenza that followed

Progress: 14/25 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the recorded military death tolls by country; what the figures do and do not include; the "lost generation" and Todman's correction; the influenza epidemic; the decline in Europe's prestige and the shift of world economic power; the discipline for writing about mass death; the boundary with the interwar economic owner

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's statement that the most striking effect of the war was the appalling death toll among the armed forces, with its country-by-country figures; its note that these did not include those crippled by the war or civilian casualties; the "lost generation" and the French proportion of men of military age; Dan Todman's argument in The First World War: Myth and Memory (2005) that the lost-generation interpretation is something of a myth and that overall just twelve per cent of fighting men died, with the first day of the Somme not typical of the war as a whole; the epidemic of deadly Spanish flu; the decline in Europe's prestige and the beginning of the end of European domination; and the record of Japan, China and the USA expanding trade, with the American share of world trade rising from ten per cent in 1914 to over twenty per cent by 1919
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage for this session is held in Lesson 7
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the recorded military death tolls, and what they exclude

| State | ✅ Recorded military deaths |
|---|---|
| Germany | Almost 2 million |
| Russia | 1.7 million |
| France | 1.5 million |
| Austria-Hungary | Over 1 million |
| Britain and her empire | About 1 million |
| Italy | Around 530,000 |
| Turkey | 325,000 |
| Serbia | 322,000 |
| Romania | 158,000 |
| USA | 116,000 |
| Bulgaria | 49,000 |
| Belgium | 41,000 |

✅ **What the table excludes, as recorded:** those crippled by the war, and civilian casualties.

⚠️ **What the table also excludes, by construction:** deaths in military labour corps that were not enumerated as military casualties; deaths from famine and disease attributable to blockade and occupation; and the mass violence against civilian populations described in Lesson 7. ⚠️ A table of military deaths is not a table of the war's deaths, and saying so in one clause is itself an analytical point.

*The table is given because it is recorded and because the figures do argumentative work - the ranking is not the ranking a candidate would guess, and three of the top five entries are states that ceased to exist in their 1914 form. ⚠️ But it must always be introduced with a qualifier, and it must never be totalled.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

⚠️ Mass death is the part of this topic where a candidate is most likely to write something both moving and wrong. ⚠️ The difficulty is that the subject demands feeling and the examination rewards precision, and the two pull in opposite directions. ⚠️ The resolution is to be exact about what is known, explicit about what is contested, and analytical about what the numbers mean - which is, in the end, also the more serious way of taking the deaths seriously.

✅ **Fact:** the account's own framing is that the most striking effect of the war was the appalling death toll among the armed forces. ⚠️ It then does something that a candidate should imitate: it gives the figures, states what they exclude, and then immediately records a historian who challenges the interpretation built on them.

### 2. The "lost generation" and Todman's correction

✅ **Fact:** a sizeable proportion of an entire generation of young men had perished - the "lost generation"; France, for example, lost around twenty per cent of men of military age.

✅ **Fact:** however, the military historian Dan Todman, in *The First World War: Myth and Memory* (2005), argues that as time has passed the public perception of the war has changed, and produces evidence suggesting that the "lost generation" interpretation is something of a myth. ✅ Casualties were certainly severe but were not the wholesale destruction of a generation that was claimed. ✅ According to Todman, overall just twelve per cent of fighting men died. ✅ Although some 20,000 British soldiers were killed on the first day of the Battle of the Somme, this was not typical of the war as a whole.

✅ **Fact:** Todman's position extends to the command question: in the circumstances, he insists, Haig had no alternative - his was the only rational strategy, and in the end, whatever the criticisms, the war was won. ✅ The account adds that many still find it difficult to put aside the long-held perception of the war as a "futile mud- and blood-bath", and that historians will continue to find it a controversial topic.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is a model of how to handle a historiographical correction, and four points should be made in an answer.

1. **The correction is about interpretation, not about denial.** Todman does not deny severity; he denies uniformity and totality.
2. **The two claims are compatible.** France losing around twenty per cent of men of military age and an overall fighting-man mortality of about twelve per cent are not contradictory, because they measure different populations - one a national cohort in the worst-affected belligerent, the other an aggregate across all fighting men.
3. **The correction has a methodological point:** public memory selects the extreme case. The first day of the Somme is the most-remembered day of the war and was not typical of it.
4. **It is nonetheless contested,** and the account says so: many find the older perception difficult to put aside, and it remains controversial.

⚠️ The sentence to write is therefore: the war was catastrophic, and historians distinguish between emotional memory and exact demographic interpretation. ⚠️ That formulation concedes nothing that should not be conceded and claims nothing that cannot be defended.

### 3. Influenza: the pandemic that arrived with the peace

✅ **Fact:** an epidemic of deadly Spanish flu added to German difficulties in the final retreat, and morale was low.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three careful statements, and one refusal.

1. The 1918-19 influenza pandemic overlapped with the war's final phase and its immediate aftermath, spreading along the same routes of troop movement, demobilisation and shipping that the war had created.
2. It struck combatants and civilians alike, and it fell heavily on populations already weakened by four years of food shortage, displacement and overcrowding - which is a direct consequence of the war economy described in Lesson 9.
3. It is therefore part of the war's mortality story even though it was not caused by the fighting, and any account of the war's demographic effect that omits it understates the disruption of 1918-19.
4. ⚠️ **The refusal:** global mortality estimates for the pandemic vary enormously between authorities and rest on very weak data for much of the world, including South Asia. ⚠️ No figure is asserted here, and an answer should say "estimates vary very widely" rather than choose one.

### 4. The structural consequence: Europe's primacy ends

✅ **Fact:** the conflict caused a decline in Europe's prestige in the eyes of the rest of the world. ✅ The fact that the region which had been thought of as the centre of civilisation could have allowed itself to experience such appalling carnage and destruction was a sign of the beginning of the end of European domination of the rest of the world.

✅ **Fact:** the empires which had dominated central and eastern Europe for over two hundred years disappeared almost overnight.

✅ **Fact:** on the other hand, some countries outside Europe - particularly Japan, China and the USA - took advantage of Europe's preoccupation with the war to expand their trade at Europe's expense. ✅ The USA's share of world trade grew from ten per cent in 1914 to over twenty per cent by 1919. ✅ Since they were unable to obtain European imports during the war, Japan and China began their own programmes of industrialisation.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** these three facts together constitute the war's largest structural consequence, and they answer the "wider world" half of any effects question. ⚠️ The mechanism is worth stating: a war fought by the industrial powers of Europe suspended their exports, diverted their production to munitions, consumed their capital and disrupted their shipping - which created, for four years, an opening for producers elsewhere. ⚠️ Import substitution in Asia and export expansion in North America were not incidental; they were the predictable effect of removing the world's dominant manufacturers from the world's markets.

✅ **Fact:** during the 1920s the Americans enjoyed a great economic boom and their future prosperity seemed assured; within a few years it became clear that they had made the mistakes of over-confidence and over-expansion, and in October 1929 the Wall Street Crash heralded the beginning of a severe economic crisis which spread throughout the world and became known as the Great Depression.

⚠️ **Boundary.** ⚠️ The Depression itself, its transmission and its political consequences belong to the interwar and world-economy owners. ⚠️ This session records the shift of economic weight out of Europe as a consequence of the war, notes where it led, and hands over. ⚠️ Lesson 22 takes the reparations and debt half of the same story, because that is directly a creation of the settlement.

### 5. The discipline for writing about mass death, stated as rules

| Rule | ⚠️ Application |
|---|---|
| Use recorded figures, introduced as recorded | "On the figures given in the standard accounts, Germany lost almost two million military dead" |
| State the measure | Military deaths; ✅ not including those crippled by the war or civilian casualties |
| Never total, average or extrapolate | ⚠️ A sum of the recorded national figures is a number no source supports |
| Report a contested interpretation as contested | ✅ Todman against the "lost generation", with the account's own note that the older perception persists |
| Refuse figures that the evidence does not support | ⚠️ Influenza mortality; Armenian mortality; colonial labour mortality; civilian deaths from blockade |
| Prefer the comparative use | ⚠️ France losing around twenty per cent of men of military age says more in an answer than five absolute totals |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** applying this discipline visibly is itself worth marks, because it demonstrates that the candidate knows the evidence is constructed rather than given. ⚠️ Examiners notice a script that writes "estimates differ" where every other script writes a confident number.

### 6. Comparison: three ways the war's human cost is measured

| Measure | ⚠️ What it captures | ⚠️ What it hides |
|---|---|---|
| **Absolute military deaths by state** | Scale and ranking; ✅ Germany almost 2 million down to Belgium 41,000 | Population size, so a small state's catastrophe looks minor - ✅ Serbia's 322,000 against a small population |
| **Proportion of a cohort** | Demographic depth; ✅ France losing around twenty per cent of men of military age | Absolute scale, and it exists for few states in the record |
| **Aggregate mortality among fighting men** | ⚠️ The corrective; ✅ Todman's overall twelve per cent | Extreme variation between armies, arms, years and days - ✅ 20,000 British killed on one day at the Somme was not typical |

⚠️ The three measures answer different questions and a good answer names which it is using. ⚠️ The commonest error in scripts is to use an absolute figure to support a claim about demographic devastation, which requires a proportion.

### 7. Is precision here a form of minimisation?

**The objection.** ⚠️ Does Todman's correction not risk minimising a catastrophe, and is repeating it not a kind of complacency?

**The reply.** ⚠️ The correction is about a specific claim - the wholesale destruction of a generation - and not about severity. ✅ Todman concedes that casualties were certainly severe. ⚠️ What he denies is uniformity and totality, and the denial matters historically because the "lost generation" image shaped interwar politics, including the reluctance to contemplate another war. ⚠️ An interpretation that influenced a generation's political choices is worth getting right. ⚠️ Moreover, precision about mortality is not minimisation: it is the only basis on which the loss can be described accurately at all.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Is it not artificial to separate military deaths from civilian deaths when the whole argument of Lesson 9 is that the distinction collapsed?

**The reply.** ⚠️ It is artificial, and the artificiality is a property of the *record* rather than of the past. ✅ The account itself flags the gap by noting that its figures do not include civilian casualties. ⚠️ The correct response is to state the limitation rather than to fill it with an invented number - and to note that the categories of person most systematically undercounted are precisely those the total-war argument identifies as newly exposed: blockaded civilians, military labourers, deported populations and the victims of famine and epidemic.

**The residual.** ⚠️ Civilian mortality, colonial labour mortality and influenza mortality are all unsettled in this session's evidence base, and all three are left unsettled.

### 8. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the demand map warns that an effects question is testing institutional and social effects plus the shift of world power, and that casualty statistics are the wrong centre of gravity for such an answer.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The First World War marked the beginning of the end of European domination of the world." Examine. (Directive: examine. Ceiling: 250 words.)
> ⚠️ Discuss the demographic and social consequences of the First World War. (Directive: discuss. Ceiling: 150 words.)

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Use at most two figures and make each of them do argumentative work: the French proportion of men of military age, and Todman's aggregate as a corrective. ⚠️ For the European-primacy question, lead with the structural facts - prestige, the disappearance of the central and eastern European empires, and the American trade share moving from ten per cent to over twenty per cent - and treat the death tolls as one supporting element rather than as the answer.

### Traps

- Making an effects answer a casualty list. ✅ The demand map warns against it explicitly.
- Totalling the recorded national figures into a war total.
- Comparing absolute figures across states of very different populations without saying so.
- Asserting an influenza mortality figure. Estimates vary very widely.
- Presenting Todman as a denial of the war's severity. ✅ He concedes that casualties were severe.
- Forgetting the excluded categories: the crippled, civilians, military labourers, and the victims of famine and epidemic.
- Carrying the Great Depression into this answer. Note the link and hand over.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The most striking effect was the appalling death toll among the armed forces.
2. ✅ Recorded military deaths: Germany almost 2 million; Russia 1.7 million; France 1.5 million; Austria-Hungary over 1 million; Britain and empire about 1 million; Italy around 530,000; Turkey 325,000; Serbia 322,000; Romania 158,000; USA 116,000; Bulgaria 49,000; Belgium 41,000.
3. ✅ These figures do not include those crippled by the war or civilian casualties.
4. ✅ The "lost generation": France lost around twenty per cent of men of military age.
5. ✅ Todman, *The First World War: Myth and Memory* (2005): the lost-generation interpretation is something of a myth; casualties were severe but not the wholesale destruction of a generation; overall just twelve per cent of fighting men died; the 20,000 British killed on the first day of the Somme was not typical.
6. ✅ Todman also holds that Haig had no alternative and that his was the only rational strategy.
7. ✅ Many still find the perception of a futile mud- and blood-bath difficult to put aside; the topic remains controversial.
8. ✅ An epidemic of deadly Spanish flu added to German difficulties in the final retreat. ⚠️ Global mortality estimates for the pandemic vary very widely and none is asserted.
9. ✅ The war caused a decline in Europe's prestige and marked the beginning of the end of European domination.
10. ✅ The empires that had dominated central and eastern Europe for over two hundred years disappeared almost overnight.
11. ✅ Japan, China and the USA expanded trade at Europe's expense; the American share of world trade grew from ten per cent in 1914 to over twenty per cent by 1919; Japan and China began their own industrialisation programmes.
12. ✅ The 1920s American boom ended with the Wall Street Crash of October 1929 - noted and handed over to the interwar and world-economy owners.
13. ⚠️ Three measures of human cost: absolute deaths by state; proportion of a cohort; aggregate mortality among fighting men. Each answers a different question.

### Concept check

**Question:** A script asserts that "the First World War destroyed an entire generation of European men." Evaluate that claim using the recorded evidence, and state what should be written instead.

**Model answer:** The claim contains a defensible core and an indefensible generalisation. The core is that mortality was enormous and that it fell disproportionately on young men: on the recorded figures, Germany lost almost two million military dead, Russia 1.7 million, France 1.5 million, Austria-Hungary over a million and Britain and her empire about a million, and France lost around twenty per cent of men of military age, which is the strongest single piece of evidence for demographic depth. The generalisation fails on three counts. First, it universalises a national extreme: the French proportion is not the European proportion, and smaller belligerents such as Belgium with 41,000 and Bulgaria with 49,000 experienced something very different in scale. Second, it mistakes the memorable for the typical. Dan Todman argues in The First World War: Myth and Memory (2005) that the lost-generation interpretation is something of a myth, that casualties were severe but were not the wholesale destruction of a generation, that overall just twelve per cent of fighting men died, and that although some 20,000 British soldiers were killed on the first day of the Somme, this was not typical of the war as a whole. Third, the claim rests on figures that exclude the categories most exposed by total war - those crippled by the war, civilian casualties, military labourers and the victims of famine and epidemic - so it is simultaneously an overstatement about soldiers and an understatement about societies. What should be written instead: the war was demographically catastrophic and unevenly so, historians distinguish between emotional memory and exact demographic interpretation, and the recorded military figures exclude civilian and disabled casualties entirely.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Todman correction as licence to downgrade the war's human cost. His argument is about the accuracy of a particular interpretation that shaped interwar politics, not about whether the war was terrible. The examinable skill is holding severity and precision together, and a script that abandons either has failed the question.

*The reckoning is stated. The victors who assembled in Paris in January 1919 had to build a settlement on top of it - and what they built was shaped by what each of them had said, during the war, that they were fighting for.*

## Lesson 15 - War aims and the Fourteen Points: what each side said it was fighting for

Progress: 15/25 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: why none of the belligerents began with stated aims; the aims that emerged and why they emerged when they did; Lloyd George's statement of January 1918; Wilson's Fourteen Points in full; the two points Wilson added later; what the Points did and did not promise; the gap between announced aims and secret treaties; why the Points became the central document of the German grievance

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's statement that when the war started none of the participants had any specific ideas about what they hoped to achieve except that Germany and Austria wanted to preserve the Habsburg Empire and thought this required destroying Serbia; its record of Lloyd George's January 1918 statement of British aims; the full text of Wilson's Fourteen Points of January 1918; the record that these achieved publicity when the Germans later claimed they had expected the peace terms to be based on them; the two further points Wilson added after Brest-Litovsk and the German destruction during the retreat; and the secret Treaty of London of 1915 promising Italy named territories

### Visual: aims announced late, and aims agreed in secret

```text
                            |

                 protectorate over Albania

                            |

                 giving them some clear objectives to fight for"
                            |
        +-------------------+--------------------+
        |                                        |
   LLOYD GEORGE, January 1918              WILSON, January 1918
   defence of democracy; righting          THE FOURTEEN POINTS
   the injustice of 1871 over              - four general principles
   Alsace and Lorraine; restoration        - nine territorial and
   of Belgium and Serbia; an                 national settlements
   independent Poland; democratic          - one new institution
   self-government for the                          |
   nationalities of Austria-               LATER ADDITIONS, after
   Hungary; self-determination for         Brest-Litovsk and the German
   the German colonies; an                 destruction during the retreat:
   international organisation to           Germany to pay for damage to
   prevent war; and Germany to pay         civilian population and
   reparations for all the damage          property, and to be reduced to
   they had done                           "virtual impotence"
        |                                        |
        +-------------------+--------------------+
                            |
   THE CONSEQUENCE: the Germans later claimed they had expected the
   peace terms to be based on the Fourteen Points and that, since
   this was not the case, they had been cheated
```

*The chart has a deliberate shape: silence, then secrecy, then publicity. ⚠️ The order matters because the secret treaties were made *before* the public principles were announced, and the conference of 1919 had to honour both. ⚠️ That is the structural contradiction that produced Italy's grievance, the Arab grievance and the German grievance, and it is a stronger explanation of the settlement's difficulties than any judgement about the personalities involved.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

✅ **Fact:** when the war started, none of the participants had any specific ideas about what they hoped to achieve, except that Germany and Austria wanted to preserve the Habsburg Empire and thought this required them to destroy Serbia.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that sentence is one of the most useful in the topic and is almost never used. ⚠️ A war begun without stated objectives is a war whose objectives can expand indefinitely with its cost, because there is no agreed measure of success against which to negotiate. ⚠️ This is why the war-aims explanation of the conflict's duration in Lesson 13 is so powerful: aims were formulated *during* the war, in proportion to what had already been spent, and each formulation made a compromise peace harder.

✅ **Fact:** as the war progressed, some governments began to list their war aims - the account suggests, perhaps to encourage their troops by giving them some clear objectives to fight for.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** note the recorded motive. Announced war aims were, in the first instance, an *instrument of morale* in societies undergoing total mobilisation. ⚠️ That connects this lesson directly to Lesson 9: a population asked to endure indefinite sacrifice must be told what the sacrifice is for, and by 1918 both Britain and the United States had concluded that "victory" was not a sufficient answer.

### 2. Lloyd George's statement, January 1918

✅ **Fact:** the British prime minister mentioned in January 1918: the defence of democracy; the righting of the injustice done to France in 1871 when she lost Alsace and Lorraine to Germany; the restoration of Belgium and Serbia; an independent Poland; democratic self-government for the nationalities of Austria-Hungary; self-determination for the German colonies; and an international organisation to prevent war. ✅ He was also determined that Germany should pay reparations for all the damage they had done.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read that list against the eventual settlement and something becomes visible immediately. Almost every item was delivered - Alsace-Lorraine, Belgium and Serbia restored, an independent Poland, successor states for the Habsburg nationalities, an international organisation, reparations. ⚠️ The one item that was *not* delivered in the sense a reader would expect is "self-determination for the German colonies", which became instead a transfer of those colonies to the victors as mandates. ⚠️ That single divergence, held against the recorded aim, is the most economical possible demonstration of the self-determination contradiction that Lesson 21 analyses.

### 3. The Fourteen Points, in full

✅ **Fact:** American President Woodrow Wilson stated US war aims in his Fourteen Points of January 1918:

1. ✅ abolition of secret diplomacy;
2. ✅ free navigation at sea for all nations in war and peace;
3. ✅ removal of economic barriers between states;
4. ✅ all-round reduction of armaments;
5. ✅ impartial adjustment of colonial claims in the interests of the populations concerned;
6. ✅ evacuation of Russian territory;
7. ✅ restoration of Belgium;
8. ✅ liberation of France and restoration of Alsace and Lorraine;
9. ✅ readjustment of Italian frontiers along the lines of nationality;
10. ✅ self-government for the peoples of Austria-Hungary;
11. ✅ Romania, Serbia and Montenegro to be evacuated and Serbia given access to the sea;
12. ✅ self-government for the non-Turkish peoples of the Turkish Empire and permanent opening of the Dardanelles;
13. ✅ an independent Poland with secure access to the sea;
14. ✅ a general association of nations to preserve peace.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Points are best learned in four groups rather than as fourteen items, because the grouping is itself an analytical statement and because it is far easier to reproduce under time pressure.

| Group | Points | ⚠️ What the group is about |
|---|---|---|
| **General principles of international conduct** | 1-4 | ⚠️ Open diplomacy, freedom of the seas, free trade and disarmament: an attempt to remove the *system* that produced the war |
| **The colonial clause** | 5 | ⚠️ "Impartial adjustment of colonial claims in the interests of the populations concerned" - ambiguous by design, and the ambiguity is the subject of Lessons 20 and 21 |
| **Territorial and national settlements** | 6-13 | ⚠️ Evacuation, restoration, liberation, readjustment and self-government - the map clauses, and the ones most nearly delivered |
| **The institution** | 14 | ⚠️ A general association of nations, which became the League |

⚠️ Observe two things about the list. ⚠️ First, point 1 - abolition of secret diplomacy - had already been contradicted by the Allies' own secret Treaty of London of May 1915, and point 9 - readjustment of Italian frontiers along the lines of nationality - directly conflicted with what that treaty had promised Italy. ⚠️ Second, points 10 and 12 promise *self-government* for the peoples of Austria-Hungary and for the non-Turkish peoples of the Turkish Empire, not independence, and point 5 promises an impartial *adjustment of claims*, not the end of colonial rule. ⚠️ Precision about that wording is the difference between understanding 1919 and being puzzled by it.

### 4. The two additional points, and why they matter

✅ **Fact:** by November 1918, German tactics - Brest-Litovsk, and the destruction of mines, factories and public buildings during their retreat through France and Belgium - had hardened the Allied attitude and led Wilson to add two further points: that Germany should pay for the damage to civilian population and property, and that Germany should be reduced to "virtual impotence", in other words disarmed.

✅ **Fact:** the Germans were aware of this when they accepted the armistice.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** these two additions are the pivot of the entire German-grievance argument, and an answer that knows them can dispose of a great deal of rhetoric in two sentences. ⚠️ The German claim was that the peace departed from the Fourteen Points. ⚠️ The reply is that the programme accepted at the armistice was the Fourteen Points *plus two additions covering reparations and disarmament* - which are precisely the two subjects on which the German complaint was loudest. ⚠️ Lesson 18 completes the audit.

### 5. The gap between announced aims and secret treaties

⚠️ Four wartime commitments were live in January 1919 and could not all be satisfied. Setting them out as a table converts a vague sense of hypocrisy into a specific analytical finding.

| Commitment | ✅ Content | ⚠️ In conflict with |
|---|---|---|
| Treaty of London, May 1915 | Italy promised Trentino, the South Tyrol, Istria, Trieste, part of Dalmatia, Adalia, some Aegean islands and a protectorate over Albania | ⚠️ Point 1 (secret diplomacy) and Point 9 (Italian frontiers along the lines of nationality) - the South Tyrol and Dalmatia were not Italian by nationality |
| Sykes-Picot understanding, May 1916 | Anglo-French division of the post-Ottoman Middle East into zones, with Russian assent | ⚠️ Point 12 (self-government for the non-Turkish peoples) and the expectations of the Arab revolt |
| Undertakings to Arab leaders from 1915-16 | Support for Arab independence in exchange for revolt against the Ottomans | ⚠️ Sykes-Picot and the Balfour Declaration |
| Balfour Declaration, 2 November 1917 | British support for a national home for the Jewish people in Palestine | ⚠️ Arab expectations of independence in the same territory |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the conference of 1919 therefore opened with a structural problem that no negotiator could solve by skill. ⚠️ It had to honour public principles announced to sustain morale and secret bargains made to buy allies, and where they conflicted it had to choose. ⚠️ Every choice it made produced a grievance - Italian, Arab, German - and the grievances are consequences of the commitments rather than of the peacemakers' character. ⚠️ That is a far stronger explanation than the usual one about Clemenceau's vindictiveness.

### 6. Comparison: three visions of the peace, before the conference met

| Axis | **Wilson's Fourteen Points** | **Lloyd George's January 1918 statement** | **The secret treaties** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Basis of legitimacy | ⚠️ Principle: nationality, open dealing, collective security | ⚠️ A mixture: principle plus restitution plus reparation | ⚠️ Bargain: territory in exchange for belligerency |
| Treatment of colonies | ✅ Impartial adjustment of colonial claims in the interests of the populations concerned | ✅ Self-determination for the German colonies | ⚠️ Division into zones and protectorates |
| Treatment of Germany | ⚠️ Restoration and liberation, later plus reparation and disarmament | ✅ Reparations for all the damage done | ⚠️ Not directly addressed |
| Institutional provision | ✅ A general association of nations | ✅ An international organisation to prevent war | ⚠️ None |
| Relationship to the eventual settlement | ⚠️ Partially delivered, conspicuously not in the colonial clause | ⚠️ Almost entirely delivered | ⚠️ Partially honoured, which is why Italy felt cheated |

⚠️ The last row is the examinable conclusion: the settlement of 1919 is best described not as Wilson's peace or Clemenceau's peace but as a *compromise between a published programme and a set of prior bargains*, in which the programme supplied the vocabulary and the bargains supplied much of the map.

### 7. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ Were the Fourteen Points not simply propaganda, given that they were issued in the same month as Lloyd George's statement and served the same morale purpose?

**The reply.** ⚠️ They were partly an instrument of morale - ✅ the account itself suggests that aims were listed perhaps to encourage troops - and they were also a genuine programme, because they were acted upon. ⚠️ The League was created; Belgium was restored; Alsace and Lorraine were returned; Poland was reconstituted with access to the sea; successor states emerged from Austria-Hungary. ⚠️ The test of a programme is whether it constrained behaviour, and this one did, unevenly. ⚠️ Where it did not constrain behaviour - the colonial clause - the divergence is itself evidence of what the powers would and would not concede.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Did the Germans not have a fair point that the eventual treaty departed from the Points?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Partly, and Lesson 18 adjudicates it in detail. ⚠️ The preliminary answer is threefold. ✅ The Fourteen Points had never been accepted as official by any of the states involved. ✅ The Germans themselves had ignored them in January 1918, when there still seemed a chance of outright German victory. ✅ And two further points on reparation and disarmament had been added before the armistice, of which the Germans were aware. ⚠️ So the claim that the peace was a swindle against an agreed basis overstates both the status of the Points and the innocence of the party invoking them.

**The residual.** ⚠️ The precise content of the wartime undertakings to Arab leaders is not verified in this session and is not quoted; only their existence and their incompatibility with the other commitments is asserted.

### 8. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the demand map records that a question on the peace settlement tests the cluster of treaties and their structural instability, and this lesson supplies the origin of that instability in the war-aims phase.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The peace of 1919 failed because the victors had promised incompatible things during the war." Examine. (Directive: examine. Ceiling: 250 words.)
> ⚠️ Discuss the significance of Wilson's Fourteen Points for the post-war settlement. (Directive: discuss. Ceiling: 250 words.)

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Reproduce the Points in the four groups rather than as fourteen items; it reads as analysis rather than as recall. ⚠️ Always mention the two additions on reparation and disarmament, because most scripts do not know them and they change the German-grievance argument. ⚠️ Use the commitments table to explain Italian, Arab and German grievances from a single cause.

### Traps

- Saying the belligerents went to war with clear aims. ✅ None had specific ideas except Germany and Austria on the Habsburg Empire and Serbia.
- Reciting the Points as a list without grouping them.
- Saying the Points promised independence to the peoples of Austria-Hungary and the Ottoman Empire. ✅ They promised *self-government*.
- Saying the Points promised decolonisation. ✅ Point 5 promised an impartial adjustment of colonial claims in the interests of the populations concerned.
- Omitting the two additional points, which are decisive for Lesson 18.
- Treating Italy's grievance as irrational. ✅ It rested on a signed treaty of May 1915.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ At the outbreak none of the participants had specific aims, except that Germany and Austria wanted to preserve the Habsburg Empire and thought this required destroying Serbia.
2. ✅ Aims were listed later, perhaps to encourage troops by giving them clear objectives.
3. ✅ Lloyd George, January 1918: defence of democracy; righting the injustice of 1871 over Alsace and Lorraine; restoration of Belgium and Serbia; an independent Poland; democratic self-government for the nationalities of Austria-Hungary; self-determination for the German colonies; an international organisation to prevent war; and German reparations for all damage done.
4. ✅ Wilson's Fourteen Points, January 1918, in four groups: general principles (abolition of secret diplomacy, free navigation, removal of economic barriers, all-round reduction of armaments); the colonial clause (impartial adjustment of colonial claims in the interests of the populations concerned); territorial and national settlements (evacuation of Russian territory; restoration of Belgium; liberation of France and restoration of Alsace and Lorraine; readjustment of Italian frontiers along the lines of nationality; self-government for the peoples of Austria-Hungary; evacuation of Romania, Serbia and Montenegro with Serbian access to the sea; self-government for the non-Turkish peoples of the Turkish Empire and permanent opening of the Dardanelles; an independent Poland with secure access to the sea); and the institution (a general association of nations to preserve peace).
5. ✅ Two further points were added after Brest-Litovsk and the German destruction during the retreat: Germany to pay for damage to civilian population and property, and to be reduced to virtual impotence, that is, disarmed. The Germans were aware of this when they accepted the armistice.
6. ✅ The Germans later claimed they had expected the peace terms to be based on the Points and that they had been cheated.
7. ✅ Four incompatible wartime commitments: the Treaty of London of May 1915; the Sykes-Picot understanding of May 1916; undertakings to Arab leaders; and the Balfour Declaration of 2 November 1917.
8. ⚠️ The Treaty of London contradicted Point 1 and Point 9 simultaneously.
9. ⚠️ Points 10 and 12 promised self-government, not independence; Point 5 promised adjustment of claims, not decolonisation.
10. ⚠️ The settlement is best described as a compromise between a published programme and a set of prior bargains.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Fourteen Points became the central document of the German grievance against the peace, and assess how strong that grievance was on the evidence available before the conference opened.

**Model answer:** They became central because of the way the war ended. Ludendorff, convinced on 3 October 1918 that Germany faced defeat in the spring of 1919, insisted that the German government ask President Wilson for an armistice, hoping to get less severe terms based on the Fourteen Points; the approach was therefore made to the author of the Points rather than to the European Allies, and a German expectation was created that the settlement would follow them. The Points had genuine content to create that expectation: they promised the abolition of secret diplomacy, free navigation, removal of economic barriers, an all-round reduction of armaments, an impartial adjustment of colonial claims in the interests of the populations concerned, and a series of territorial settlements, together with a general association of nations. On the evidence available before the conference opened, the grievance was weak in three respects. First, the Points had never been accepted as official by any of the states involved, so they were not an agreed basis binding anyone. Second, the Germans themselves had ignored them in January 1918, when there still seemed a chance of outright German victory, which makes the later appeal to them opportunistic. Third, and decisively, Wilson had added two further points before the armistice - that Germany should pay for damage to civilian population and property, and should be reduced to virtual impotence, that is disarmed - in response to Brest-Litovsk and the German destruction of mines, factories and public buildings during the retreat through France and Belgium; and the Germans were aware of this when they accepted the armistice. Since reparations and disarmament were the two clauses most bitterly resented, the grievance attacks precisely the terms that had been notified in advance.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Fourteen Points as a contract that the Allies broke. They were a unilateral statement of one belligerent's aims, never formally adopted by the coalition, amended before the armistice, and simultaneously contradicted by secret treaties the Allies had already signed. Understanding their status is what allows an answer to assess the German claim rather than merely repeat or dismiss it.

*Aims are not settlements. Between the two stands a conference of unprecedented size, working under deadlines, publicity and the weight of everything the previous fourteen lessons have described.*

## Lesson 16 - Paris 1919: who decided, by what machinery, under what constraints

Progress: 16/25 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the conference's opening and scale; the Council of Ten and the Council of Four; the three national positions and the domestic pressures behind them; the constraint of decisions already taken on the ground; the absences - Germany, Russia and the defeated states; the timetable and the five treaties; why the machinery shaped the outcome

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's record that when the peace conference met in January 1919 it was soon obvious that a settlement would be difficult because of basic disagreements among the victorious powers, and its treatment of the French, British and American positions; its record of the Big Four at Versailles; its statement that many important decisions had already been taken before the conference met because the Habsburg Empire had disintegrated and that the task of the conference was to formalise and recognise what had taken place; its record that Russia was ignored because the powers did not want to negotiate with its Bolshevik government; that the Germans were not allowed into the discussions and were simply presented with the terms; and that the USA failed to ratify the settlement and never joined the League. Sections of the printed page covering the three national positions are damaged in the available scan; the positions are stated here in their standard form and the limitation is recorded in the source ledger
