━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the machinery, the positions and the constraints

```text
   18 JANUARY 1919   THE CONFERENCE OPENS IN PARIS
                     delegations from the victorious and associated
                     powers; the defeated states are NOT present
                                |
   THE MACHINERY     COUNCIL OF TEN (heads of government and foreign
                     ministers of the five principal powers)
                              -> too large and too slow
                     COUNCIL OF FOUR: WILSON (USA), LLOYD GEORGE
                     (Britain), CLEMENCEAU (France), ORLANDO (Italy)
                              -> the effective decision-making body
                                |
   THREE POSITIONS
   +----------------+----------------------+----------------------+
   | FRANCE         | BRITAIN              | UNITED STATES        |
   | Clemenceau     | Lloyd George         | Wilson               |
   | a HARSH peace: | a LESS SEVERE        | a LENIENT peace      |
   | cripple        | settlement, so that  | organised around the |
   | Germany        | Germany could        | Fourteen Points and  |
   | economically   | resume its role as   | a general            |
   | and militarily | a major customer     | association of       |
   | so that she    | for British trade -  | nations; but         |
   | can never      | BUT he had just won  | disillusioned by the |
   | invade France  | an election on talk  | German conduct at    |
   | again          | of hanging the       | Brest-Litovsk        |
   |                | Kaiser and squeezing |                      |
   |                | Germany until the    |                      |
   |                | pips squeak          |                      |
   +----------------+----------------------+----------------------+
                                |
   FOUR CONSTRAINTS THE CONFERENCE COULD NOT REMOVE
   1 FACTS ON THE GROUND: Austria-Hungary had already dissolved; the
     nationalities had declared independence; the task was to
     FORMALISE AND RECOGNISE what had taken place
   2 PRIOR COMMITMENTS: the secret treaties of 1915-17
   3 ABSENCES: Germany was not admitted to the discussions; Russia
     was ignored because the powers would not negotiate with the
     Bolsheviks
   4 DOMESTIC OPINION: electorates that had endured total war and
     expected a settlement proportionate to their sacrifice
                                |
   28 JUNE 1919      VERSAILLES SIGNED (Hall of Mirrors)
   10 JANUARY 1920   IT COMES INTO FORCE, with the League Covenant
   MARCH 1920        THE UNITED STATES SENATE REFUSES RATIFICATION
```

*The chart is organised around constraints rather than personalities, because the personalities are what scripts already know and the constraints are what they miss. ⚠️ Read the four constraints together and an important conclusion follows: the room for manoeuvre at Paris was far smaller than the rhetoric of the conference suggested, and several of the settlement's worst features were consequences of decisions taken before anyone arrived.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

⚠️ The conference had to do four things at once: end a war legally, redraw the map of two continents, construct an international institution, and satisfy electorates that had been mobilised for four years. ⚠️ Any one of those is a large task; the four together, under a deadline, with the defeated absent and one great power excluded, is the actual situation of 1919.

✅ **Fact:** when the peace conference met in January 1919 it was soon obvious that a settlement would be difficult because of basic disagreements among the victorious powers. ✅ The conference opened on 18 January 1919.

### 2. The machinery: from ten to four

✅ **Fact:** decision-making began in a Council of Ten comprising the heads of government and foreign ministers of the principal powers, and was concentrated in a Council of Four - Wilson for the United States, Lloyd George for Britain, Clemenceau for France and Orlando for Italy - which became the effective decision-making body. ✅ The four are recorded together in the account's own photographic record of the conference.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the machinery is not a procedural detail; it shaped the result in three ways.

1. **Concentration produced speed at the cost of consultation.** A body of four could decide; it could not represent. Smaller allied states, and the populations whose frontiers were being drawn, had no vote in it.
2. **Disagreement among four is resolved by compromise, not by principle.** Where Wilson's principles conflicted with French security requirements or British commitments, the outcome was frequently a middle term that satisfied the logic of neither - the Saar arrangement and the Rhineland provisions are the clearest examples.
3. **The absence of the defeated from the room converted negotiation into notification.** ✅ The Germans were not allowed into the discussions at Versailles; they were simply presented with the terms and told to sign, and although they were allowed to criticise the treaty in writing, all their criticisms were ignored except one.

### 3. The three national positions

⚠️ These are stated here in their standard form. ⚠️ The relevant pages of the available scan of the core printed account are damaged, and the limitation is recorded in the source ledger rather than concealed; the substance below is what the legible portions and the surrounding argument support.

| Power | Objective | ⚠️ Why | ⚠️ Domestic constraint |
|---|---|---|---|
| **France (Clemenceau)** | A harsh peace that would cripple Germany economically and militarily so that she could never invade France again | ⚠️ France had been invaded within living memory and had just been fought over for four years; security was the whole question | ⚠️ A public that had seen its territory devastated and expected permanent protection |
| **Britain (Lloyd George)** | A less severe settlement, so that Germany could resume its role as a major customer | ⚠️ British prosperity depended on European trade, and a permanently impoverished Germany was a permanently shrunken market | ⚠️ He had just won an election in a climate of talk about hanging the Kaiser and squeezing Germany until the pips squeaked |
| **United States (Wilson)** | A lenient settlement organised around the Fourteen Points and a general association of nations | ⚠️ A programme of principle rather than of territorial interest; the USA had entered late and had no European territorial claim | ⚠️ Disillusionment produced by German conduct at Brest-Litovsk; and, fatally, a Senate that would refuse ratification |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the British position is the one that repays attention, because it contains the settlement's central political problem in miniature. ⚠️ Lloyd George's *analysis* pointed towards moderation and his *mandate* pointed towards severity. ⚠️ A statesman who believes one thing and has been elected to do another will produce a compromise that is neither generous enough to reconcile nor severe enough to disable - which is exactly the verdict the account eventually returns on the settlement as a whole: it left the Germans with a sense of resentment and grievance but did not leave them too weak to retaliate and seek revenge.

✅ **Fact:** the treaty could in fact have been even more harsh: if Clemenceau had had his way, the Rhineland would have become an independent state and France would have annexed the Saar. ⚠️ That fact is essential to any balanced answer on severity, because it establishes that the terms actually imposed were themselves a moderation of the French position.

### 4. Four constraints the conference could not remove

⚠️ This section is the lesson's analytical core.

**Constraint one: facts already on the ground.** ✅ When Austria was on the verge of defeat the Habsburg Empire disintegrated as the various nationalities declared themselves independent; Austria and Hungary separated and declared themselves republics; many important decisions had therefore already been taken before the peace conference met, and the task of the conference was to formalise and recognise what had taken place. ⚠️ The peacemakers did not design central Europe; they adjudicated it.

**Constraint two: prior commitments.** ⚠️ The secret treaties of 1915-17 bound the Allies to arrangements that the public principles of 1918 contradicted, as Lesson 15 set out. ⚠️ A negotiator cannot simultaneously honour a promise and repudiate the practice of making such promises.

**Constraint three: absences.** ✅ Germany was not admitted to the discussions. ✅ Russia was ignored because the powers did not want to negotiate with its Bolshevik government. ⚠️ The consequences were structural: the largest state in Europe had no part in the settlement of eastern Europe, and the frontier between Germany and the new Poland was fixed with neither Germany nor Russia present.

**Constraint four: domestic opinion.** ⚠️ Every government at Paris answered to an electorate that had endured total war. ⚠️ This is the point at which Lesson 9 and Lesson 16 meet: societies that had been mobilised totally expected settlements proportionate to their sacrifice, and no delegation could return home with a peace that looked like a negotiated draw.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the four constraints together dispose of the most common explanation of the settlement's defects, which is that Clemenceau was vengeful. ⚠️ The stronger explanation is that the conference operated inside a box built by the war itself - a dissolved empire, a set of prior bargains, two absent great powers and four mobilised electorates - and that the box determined more of the outcome than the negotiators did.

### 5. The timetable and the five treaties

| Date | ✅ Event |
|---|---|
| 18 January 1919 | The conference opens in Paris |
| 28 June 1919 | The Treaty of Versailles with Germany is signed in the Hall of Mirrors, where the German Empire had been proclaimed less than fifty years earlier |
| 1919 | The Treaty of St Germain with Austria; the Treaty of Neuilly with Bulgaria |
| 10 January 1920 | Versailles comes into force, on the same day that the League of Nations formally came into existence |
| 1920 | The Treaty of Trianon with Hungary, delayed by political uncertainties in Budapest; the Treaty of Sevres with Turkey |
| March 1920 | The United States Senate finally refuses ratification; ✅ the USA never joined the League |
| 1923 | The Treaty of Lausanne replaces Sevres |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the settlement therefore took four and a half years to complete and was already being rewritten before it was finished. ⚠️ Note also the symbolism of the Hall of Mirrors, recorded by the account: the room in which the German Empire had been proclaimed in 1871 was chosen for the treaty that dismantled its position - an act of deliberate reversal that gave the German grievance a ceremonial as well as a substantive object.

### 6. Comparison: what each power wanted and what it got

| Power | ⚠️ Principal objective | ⚠️ Outcome |
|---|---|---|
| France | Permanent security against Germany | ⚠️ Partial: Alsace-Lorraine returned, the Rhineland demilitarised and occupied, German armaments limited - but ✅ the Anglo-American guarantee of her frontiers lapsed when the USA failed to ratify, leaving France disenchanted with the whole thing |
| Britain | A settlement compatible with trade and with imperial security | ⚠️ Largely achieved: the German fleet and colonies removed, mandates acquired, Europe not dominated by a single power |
| United States | A new international order under a general association of nations | ✅ The League was created and the USA never joined it |
| Italy | The territories promised in 1915 | ✅ Italy felt cheated because she had not received all the territory promised her in 1915 |
| Japan | Confirmation of its wartime gains and standing | ⚠️ Gained mandated territory and recognition as a principal power |
| Germany | Terms based on the Fourteen Points | ⚠️ Excluded from the discussions; all written criticisms ignored except one |
| Russia | Nothing was sought and nothing was given | ✅ Ignored, because the powers would not negotiate with the Bolshevik government |

⚠️ Read the second column downwards. ⚠️ Not one participant obtained what it had principally sought, and the two powers whose absence mattered most - Russia and, after March 1920, the United States - were outside the system the conference built. ⚠️ That is the structural weakness Lesson 24 has to weigh.

### 7. Does the constraint argument excuse the peacemakers?

**The objection.** ⚠️ Is the emphasis on constraints not a way of excusing the peacemakers?

**The reply.** ⚠️ It is a way of locating responsibility accurately, which is different. ⚠️ Some features of the settlement were choices and are properly criticised: the exclusion of Germany from the negotiations; the reparations figure; the form of Article 231. ⚠️ Others were not available to be chosen: the dissolution of Austria-Hungary had already occurred; the secret treaties had already been signed; the Bolshevik government was not going to be invited. ⚠️ An answer that criticises the peacemakers for the second category is criticising them for the weather.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Given the disagreements, is it not surprising that any settlement was reached at all?

**The reply.** ⚠️ That was the view of participants at the time, and it is recorded. ✅ An American delegate, Gilbert White, remarked that given the intricacy of the problems involved, "it is not surprising that they made a bad peace: what is surprising is that they managed to make peace at all". ⚠️ Against that stand two equally recorded contemporary judgements: ✅ the British diplomat Harold Nicolson wrote, "If I were the Germans, I shouldn't sign for a moment", and ✅ Keynes, a senior British delegate and economic adviser, was so disillusioned that he resigned in protest and came home. ⚠️ The strongest answer uses all three, because together they establish that the settlement's weaknesses were recognised at the time by people inside the conference, which forecloses the defence that they were visible only in hindsight.

**The residual.** ⚠️ The relevant pages of the available scan covering the three national positions in detail are damaged, and the positions are therefore stated in their standard form rather than quoted; the limitation is recorded in the source ledger.

### 8. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** a question on the peace settlement tests the cluster of treaties and their structural instability, and this lesson supplies the process explanation for that instability.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The Paris settlement was shaped more by constraint than by intention." Examine. (Directive: examine. Ceiling: 250 words.)
> ⚠️ Discuss the aims of the principal powers at the Paris Peace Conference and the extent to which they were achieved. (Directive: discuss. Ceiling: 250 words.)

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Open with the three positions in one sentence each, because that is what the question expects, and then immediately introduce the four constraints, because that is what will distinguish the answer. ⚠️ Use the "what each power got" table as the conclusion: nobody obtained their principal objective, and the two decisive absentees were outside the system. ⚠️ Use the three contemporary judgements - White, Nicolson, Keynes - rather than a modern verdict.

### Traps

- Explaining the settlement by Clemenceau's vindictiveness alone. ✅ The terms actually imposed were a moderation of the French position.
- Saying the conference broke up Austria-Hungary. ✅ It formalised and recognised what had already taken place.
- Forgetting that Germany was excluded from the discussions and that all its written criticisms were ignored except one.
- Forgetting that Russia was ignored because the powers would not negotiate with the Bolsheviks.
- Omitting the American failure to ratify, and its specific consequence for France.
- Treating the conference as a single event. It ran from January 1919 to 1923 and produced five treaties.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The conference opened on 18 January 1919; disagreements among the victors were immediately obvious.
2. ✅ Decision-making moved from a Council of Ten to a Council of Four: Wilson, Lloyd George, Clemenceau and Orlando.
3. ⚠️ France sought a harsh peace to cripple Germany economically and militarily; Britain sought a less severe settlement to preserve Germany as a customer while bound by an election fought on severity; the United States sought a lenient settlement on the Fourteen Points, disillusioned by Brest-Litovsk.
4. ✅ If Clemenceau had had his way the Rhineland would have become an independent state and France would have annexed the Saar.
5. ✅ Constraint one: Austria-Hungary had already dissolved; the conference's task was to formalise and recognise what had taken place.
6. ⚠️ Constraint two: the secret treaties of 1915-17 bound the Allies to arrangements the public principles contradicted.
7. ✅ Constraint three: Germany was not admitted to the discussions and all its written criticisms were ignored except one; Russia was ignored because the powers would not negotiate with its Bolshevik government.
8. ⚠️ Constraint four: electorates mobilised by total war expected settlements proportionate to their sacrifice.
9. ✅ Versailles was signed on 28 June 1919 in the Hall of Mirrors, where the German Empire had been proclaimed less than fifty years earlier.
10. ✅ Versailles came into force on 10 January 1920, the day the League formally came into existence; the United States Senate refused ratification in March 1920 and the USA never joined the League.
11. ✅ France was left disenchanted because the Anglo-American guarantee of her frontiers could no longer apply.
12. ✅ Italy felt cheated because she had not received all the territory promised her in 1915.
13. ✅ Contemporary verdicts: Gilbert White - surprising that they made peace at all; Harold Nicolson - if I were the Germans, I shouldn't sign for a moment; Keynes resigned in protest and came home.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Paris settlement satisfied none of its principal authors, using the machinery and the constraints rather than the personalities of the negotiators.

**Model answer:** It satisfied none of them because the conference was required to reconcile incompatible objectives inside a box that the war had already built. The three positions were genuinely opposed: France under Clemenceau wanted a peace that would cripple Germany economically and militarily so that she could never invade France again; Britain under Lloyd George wanted a less severe settlement so that Germany could resume its role as a major customer, while being bound by an election fought in a climate of talk about hanging the Kaiser; and the United States under Wilson wanted a lenient settlement organised around the Fourteen Points and a general association of nations. Decision-making moved from a Council of Ten to a Council of Four, which produced speed at the cost of representation and resolved disagreement by compromise rather than by principle. Four constraints then removed most of the remaining freedom. Central Europe had already been settled on the ground, because the Habsburg Empire disintegrated as its nationalities declared themselves independent, so the conference's task was to formalise and recognise what had taken place. Prior secret treaties bound the Allies to arrangements their public principles contradicted. Germany was excluded from the discussions and Russia was ignored because the powers would not negotiate with its Bolshevik government, so the settlement of eastern Europe was made without either of the powers that bordered it. And every delegation answered to an electorate that had endured total war. The outcome was that France lost the Anglo-American guarantee when the United States Senate refused ratification in March 1920, Italy felt cheated of the territory promised in 1915, Wilson's League was created without his own country in it, and Germany was presented with terms it had not negotiated.

**Misconception to avoid:** Explaining 1919 through the character of the negotiators. Clemenceau's severity is real but it was itself moderated - had he prevailed, the Rhineland would have become an independent state and France would have annexed the Saar. The settlement's shape follows from the machinery and the constraints, and an answer built on those is both more accurate and harder to argue against.

*The machinery produced five treaties. The first and most famous of them is also the most misdescribed, so it is worth reading its terms exactly.*

## Lesson 17 - The Treaty of Versailles: the terms, clause by clause

Progress: 17/25 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the territorial clauses in Europe; the plebiscite provisions; the Baltic states as self-determination in practice; the Anschluss prohibition; the colonial clause; the military clauses and the Rhineland; Article 231 and what it actually did; reparations and the 1921 figure; the Covenant as part of the treaty; the signature and its symbolism; how to reproduce the terms under time pressure

### Visual: the treaty sorted into six kinds of clause

*The six-column sort is the fastest reliable way to reproduce this treaty under examination conditions. ⚠️ Learn the headings first and hang the clauses on them; a candidate who can name six kinds of clause will not omit a whole category, which is the usual way marks are lost here.*

### 1. Territorial losses in Europe

✅ **Fact:** Germany had to lose territory in Europe:

- ✅ Alsace-Lorraine to France;
- ✅ Eupen, Moresnet and Malmedy to Belgium;
- ✅ North Schleswig to Denmark, after a plebiscite - that is, a vote by the people;
- ✅ West Prussia and Posen to Poland, though Danzig, the main port of West Prussia, was to be a free city under League of Nations administration because its population was wholly German;
- ✅ Memel was detached from Germany and placed under the Principal Allied and Associated Powers. ⚠️ Its later passage to Lithuania is **not** a Versailles provision, and an answer that says the treaty gave Memel to Lithuania has compressed two separate events into one.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three of these are worth a sentence each in an answer because each illustrates a different principle at work.

| Transfer | ⚠️ Principle illustrated |
|---|---|
| Alsace-Lorraine to France | **Restitution.** It had been taken in 1871 and was named in both Lloyd George's January 1918 statement and Point 8 of the Fourteen Points |
| North Schleswig to Denmark after a plebiscite | **Consent.** Where the settlement used a plebiscite, it applied the nationality principle by asking the population - and the fact that it did so *somewhere* is what makes its failure to do so elsewhere a legitimate criticism |
| Danzig as a free city under League administration | **Compromise.** Poland needed the port; the population was wholly German. The result satisfied neither principle and created a permanent dispute |

### 2. The Saar: a bespoke arrangement

✅ **Fact:** the area known as the Saar was to be administered by the League of Nations for fifteen years, when the population would be allowed to vote on whether it should belong to France or Germany; in the meantime, France was to have the use of its coal mines.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Saar arrangement is the clearest single specimen of how the Council of Four resolved disagreement. ⚠️ France wanted annexation; the nationality principle forbade it; the compromise gave France the economic asset immediately and deferred the sovereignty question to a future vote. ⚠️ It is simultaneously a concession to principle and a transfer of resources, and Lesson 16's point about compromise producing outcomes that satisfy neither logic is visible in a single clause.

### 3. The Baltic states, and the treaty's own claim about self-determination

✅ **Fact:** Estonia, Latvia and Lithuania, which had been handed over to Germany by Russia by the Treaty of Brest-Litovsk, were taken away from Germany and set up as independent states. ✅ The account describes this as an example of self-determination being carried into practice.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this clause is doubly useful. ⚠️ First, it is direct evidence that the settlement *did* apply self-determination and not merely proclaim it, which any balanced answer must concede. ⚠️ Second, it is a reversal of Brest-Litovsk - the treaty Germany had imposed on Russia - which supplies the retort to the German complaint about dictated peace. ⚠️ Lesson 18 uses it.

### 4. The Anschluss prohibition

✅ **Fact:** union between Germany and Austria was forbidden.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** hold this clause carefully, because it is the sharpest self-inflicted wound in the treaty. ✅ Austria was a completely German state with a population of some seven million, and ✅ all these Germans wanted to become part of Germany. ✅ The union was specifically forbidden - the account suggests, probably because it would have made Germany larger and more powerful even than in 1914. ⚠️ So a settlement whose organising public principle was national self-determination prohibited the one union that the principle most obviously required, and did so for a reason of power. ⚠️ Lesson 21 treats this as the decisive evidence in the self-determination argument, and ✅ the account elsewhere notes that there was an excellent *economic* case for the same union.

### 5. The colonial clause

✅ **Fact:** Germany's African colonies were taken away and became "mandates" under League of Nations supervision, which meant that various member states of the League "looked after" them.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** note the quotation marks in the source, which are doing work. ✅ The account elsewhere states the position without them: this was really a device by which the Allies seized the colonies without actually admitting that they were being annexed. ⚠️ Lesson 20 examines the mandate system as an institution and states the ambiguity on both sides; here it is enough to record the clause and to observe that Britain, France, Belgium, South Africa and Japan all emerged from a peace conference larger than they entered it.

### 6. The military clauses

✅ **Fact:** German armaments were strictly limited: a maximum of 100,000 troops; no conscription, that is, no compulsory military service; no tanks, armoured cars, military aircraft or submarines; and only six battleships.

✅ **Fact:** the Rhineland was to be permanently demilitarised. This meant that all German territory on the left bank of the Rhine, together with a fifty-kilometre strip on the right bank, was to be closed to German troops and was to be occupied by Allied troops for at least ten years.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the military clauses are usually recited and rarely analysed. Three observations earn marks.

1. **The prohibition on conscription is more significant than the manpower cap.** A 100,000-man army with conscription is a training cadre for a much larger force; without conscription it is a long-service professional force that cannot expand rapidly. The clause was aimed at capacity, not at numbers.
2. **The banned categories are precisely the offensive and modern ones** - tanks, armoured cars, military aircraft and submarines. The intention was to leave Germany able to police itself and unable to attack anyone, which is exactly the "virtual impotence" of Wilson's additional point.
3. **The Rhineland provision is a *security* clause, not a punitive one.** It creates a demilitarised buffer on France's frontier with an Allied occupation to enforce it, and it is the closest the treaty comes to meeting the French objective directly. ⚠️ Its later remilitarisation belongs to the interwar owner.

### 7. Article 231 and reparations

✅ **Fact:** the War Guilt clause fixed the blame for the outbreak of the war solely on Germany and her allies, and proposed that the ex-Kaiser should be put on trial for war crimes.

✅ **Fact:** Germany was to pay reparations for damage done to the Allies; the actual amount was not decided at Versailles, but it was announced later, in 1921, after much argument and haggling, as £6600 million.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three precise points, each of which corrects a common error.

1. **The clause's legal function and its political meaning are different things.** ✅ The Allies wanted the Germans to admit responsibility so that they would be liable to pay reparations; ✅ the article is recorded as grounds for German resentment because of its political "war-guilt" meaning, though its immediate legal function was reparations liability. ⚠️ An answer should state both.
2. **The figure was not in the treaty.** ✅ It was announced in 1921 after argument and haggling. ⚠️ This matters because it means the treaty imposed an *open-ended* liability, which was itself a grievance, and because the figure therefore belongs to the reparations diplomacy of the 1920s as much as to the settlement.
3. **The proposal to try the ex-Kaiser is worth a clause.** ⚠️ It is the first serious attempt in modern European history to attach personal criminal responsibility to a head of state for the conduct of a war, and it connects to the Allied declaration of 24 May 1915 about personal accountability for crimes against humanity. ⚠️ Neither succeeded, but the principle they asserted reappears after 1945.

### 8. The Covenant, and the signature

✅ **Fact:** a League of Nations was formed; its aims and organisation were set out in the League Covenant.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Covenant was part of the treaty rather than a separate instrument, and ✅ the same was true of the treaties with Austria and Hungary, both of which contained the Covenant. ⚠️ The consequence is important and Lesson 20 develops it: membership of the new international organisation was legally inseparable from acceptance of the peace settlement, so a state that rejected the settlement rejected the organisation - which is precisely what happened in the United States Senate.

✅ **Fact:** the Germans had little choice but to sign the treaty, though they objected strongly. ✅ The signing ceremony took place in the Hall of Mirrors at Versailles, where the German Empire had been proclaimed less than fifty years earlier.

### 9. Comparison: the treaty's clauses ranked by long-term consequence

| Clause | ⚠️ Immediate effect | ⚠️ Long-term consequence |
|---|---|---|
| Alsace-Lorraine to France | Restitution of an 1871 loss | ⚠️ Minor; widely accepted as legitimate, including in the Fourteen Points |
| The Polish corridor and Danzig | Poland gains access to the sea | ⚠️ Major; a German population under Polish rule and a divided German state |
| The Saar | France gains coal; sovereignty deferred | ⚠️ Moderate; resolved by the later vote |
| Anschluss forbidden | Austria kept separate | ⚠️ Major; the most visible contradiction of self-determination |
| Colonies to mandates | Germany loses its empire; victors gain territory | ⚠️ Major; shapes Africa and the Middle East for a generation |
| Military limits | Germany disarmed | ⚠️ Major; resented, and ✅ impossible to enforce fully because the Germans were determined to exploit every loophole |
| Article 231 | Legal basis for reparations | ⚠️ Major; a permanent political rallying point |
| Reparations, later £6600 million | Open-ended liability, then a very large figure | ⚠️ Major; economically and politically disastrous, as Lesson 22 shows |
| The Covenant | A new international organisation | ⚠️ Major; and fatally linked to a settlement one great power refused to ratify |

⚠️ The ranking is itself an argument: the clauses that caused the most trouble were not the territorial ones, which were largely defensible on nationality grounds, but the ones concerning responsibility, payment and the prohibition of a union that nationality required.

### 10. Two challenges to a clause-by-clause treatment

**Challenge one.** ⚠️ Is a clause-by-clause treatment not simply memorisation?

**Reply.** ⚠️ Only if the clauses are listed rather than sorted. ⚠️ The six-category sort is analytical: it distinguishes restitution from security, security from punishment, and punishment from institution-building, and it allows an answer to say which category caused which problem. ⚠️ A script that writes "Germany lost territory, was disarmed and had to pay" has recalled the treaty; a script that writes "the territorial clauses were largely defensible on nationality grounds, the security clauses were the only ones that directly served the French objective, and the trouble came from the responsibility and payment clauses" has analysed it.

**Challenge two.** ⚠️ Was the treaty exceptionally harsh by the standards of its time?

**Reply.** ⚠️ Two comparisons cut against the claim and one supports it. ✅ Germany had itself imposed Brest-Litovsk harshly on Russia, which the account also calls a "Diktat". ✅ The treaty could have been even more harsh: had Clemenceau prevailed, the Rhineland would have become an independent state and France would have annexed the Saar. ⚠️ Supporting the claim is the reparations figure, which ✅ many historians now agree was far too high at £6600 million and which ✅ Keynes at the time thought should have been £2000 million. ⚠️ The balanced verdict, developed in Lesson 18, is that the treaty was harsh in specific clauses rather than uniformly severe, and that its worst feature was the combination of severity with non-enforcement.

**The open point.** ⚠️ The detailed schedule under which the 1921 figure was to be paid is not held in this session's evidence base and is not described here.

### 11. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the demand map warns that treating Versailles as the whole settlement is an error and that the answer must name the other treaties; it also warns against giving any reparations figure other than £6600 million, later reduced under the Young Plan.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Critically evaluate the Treaty of Versailles. (Directive: critically evaluate. Ceiling: 250 words.)
> ⚠️ Examine the main provisions of the Treaty of Versailles and their significance. (Directive: examine. Ceiling: 250 words.)

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Use the six categories as paragraph headings in your own mind and give each one or two clauses; this guarantees complete coverage in about eighty words. ⚠️ Then spend the remaining words on consequence rather than on further recall, using the ranking table. ⚠️ Always add one sentence naming the other four treaties, so the answer cannot be accused of mistaking Versailles for the settlement.

### Traps

- Saying Article 231 was called the "war-guilt clause" in the treaty text. ✅ It fixed blame and provided the legal basis for reparations; the political label attached to it afterwards.
- Giving a reparations figure other than ✅ £6600 million, announced in 1921 and later reduced to ✅ £2000 million under the Young Plan of 1929.
- Saying the reparations figure was fixed at Versailles. ✅ It was not decided there.
- Forgetting the plebiscites, which are the evidence that the settlement sometimes did apply consent.
- Forgetting the Baltic states, which the account calls self-determination carried into practice.
- Omitting the Anschluss prohibition, which is the single best piece of evidence for the self-determination contradiction.
- Forgetting that the Covenant was part of the treaty.
- Saying Versailles gave Memel to Lithuania. ✅ The treaty detached it to the Principal Allied and Associated Powers; Lithuanian control came afterwards.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Signed 28 June 1919 in the Hall of Mirrors, where the German Empire had been proclaimed less than fifty years earlier; in force 10 January 1920.
2. ✅ Territory: Alsace-Lorraine to France; Eupen, Moresnet and Malmedy to Belgium; North Schleswig to Denmark after a plebiscite; West Prussia and Posen to Poland; Memel detached to the Principal Allied and Associated Powers, its later transfer to Lithuania not being a Versailles provision.
3. ✅ Danzig, the main port of West Prussia, became a free city under League administration because its population was wholly German.
4. ✅ The Saar was to be administered by the League for fifteen years and then voted on; France was to have the use of its coal mines meanwhile.
5. ✅ Estonia, Latvia and Lithuania, handed to Germany by Russia at Brest-Litovsk, were taken away and set up as independent states - an example of self-determination being carried into practice.
6. ✅ Union between Germany and Austria was forbidden.
7. ✅ Germany's African colonies became mandates under League supervision, with member states "looking after" them.
8. ✅ Armaments: maximum 100,000 troops; no conscription; no tanks, armoured cars, military aircraft or submarines; only six battleships.
9. ✅ The Rhineland was permanently demilitarised: the left bank plus a fifty-kilometre strip on the right bank closed to German troops and occupied by Allied troops for at least ten years.
10. ✅ The War Guilt clause fixed blame on Germany and her allies and proposed that the ex-Kaiser be tried for war crimes.
11. ✅ Reparations were not fixed at Versailles; the figure was announced in 1921 as £6600 million.
12. ✅ A League of Nations was formed, with aims and organisation set out in the League Covenant, which was part of the treaty.
13. ⚠️ Six categories: territory in Europe; plebiscite and special regimes; colonies; military; responsibility and payment; institution.
14. ⚠️ The clauses that caused most trouble were responsibility, payment and the Anschluss prohibition, not the territorial transfers.

### Concept check

**Question:** Sort the terms of the Treaty of Versailles into categories, and identify which category caused the most durable damage and why.

**Model answer:** Six categories cover the treaty completely. Territory in Europe: Alsace-Lorraine to France, Eupen, Moresnet and Malmedy to Belgium, North Schleswig to Denmark after a plebiscite, West Prussia and Posen to Poland, and Memel detached to the Principal Allied and Associated Powers rather than granted to Lithuania. Special regimes: Danzig as a free city under League administration because its population was wholly German, and the Saar under League administration for fifteen years with France using its coal mines and a vote to follow. Colonies: Germany's African colonies became mandates under League supervision, with member states looking after them. Military: a maximum of 100,000 troops, no conscription, no tanks, armoured cars, military aircraft or submarines, only six battleships, and a permanently demilitarised Rhineland comprising the left bank and a fifty-kilometre strip on the right, occupied by Allied troops for at least ten years. Responsibility and payment: the War Guilt clause fixing blame on Germany and her allies and proposing the trial of the ex-Kaiser, and reparations left unquantified at Versailles and announced in 1921 as £6600 million. Institution: the League of Nations, with its aims and organisation set out in the Covenant. The most durable damage came from the responsibility and payment category, together with the prohibition of union with Austria. The territorial transfers were largely defensible on nationality grounds and two were made by plebiscite or by restoring independence, as with Estonia, Latvia and Lithuania - which the account calls self-determination carried into practice. But Article 231 supplied a permanent political rallying point, the open-ended and then very large reparations liability was economically and politically disastrous, and the prohibition of Anschluss denied the nationality principle to a completely German state of some seven million people, for a reason of power rather than of principle.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that the territorial clauses were the treaty's main grievance. Most of the German losses could be justified on grounds of nationality, and the settlement used plebiscites where it chose to. The grievances that endured attached to blame, to money and to a union that the treaty's own stated principle required.

*Germany objected on every count. The next lesson takes each objection in turn and asks how much of it survives examination - which is what a "critically evaluate" question is actually asking for.*

## Lesson 18 - Were the German objections justified? A five-count audit

Progress: 18/25 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the dictated-peace objection and the Brest-Litovsk reply; the Fourteen Points objection and its three defects; territorial losses, the Upper Silesia concession and the genuine self-determination complaint; the colonial objection and the impartial-adjustment test; disarmament and enforceability; Article 231 and the six-week commission; reparations and the Keynes alternative; the verdict a balanced answer should return

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on why the Germans objected and how far their objections were justified, covering the dictated peace and the Brest-Litovsk parallel, the Fourteen Points claim and the reasons it is probably not valid, territorial losses with the Upper Silesia vote and the genuine self-determination complaint about Germans left under Polish and Czechoslovak rule and the Austrian prohibition, the African colonies and the seizure-without-admitting-annexation point, the disarmament clauses and the enforcement problem, Article 231 and the Special Commission on War Responsibility, and reparations with the Keynes figure and the Young Plan reduction; and the advanced material's tabulated verdicts on each issue
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage for this session is held in Lesson 7
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: five objections, audited

| # | German objection | ✅ The strongest point in its favour | ✅ The reply | ⚠️ Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **It was a dictated peace** | The Germans were not allowed into the discussions; they were presented with the terms and told to sign, and all their written criticisms were ignored except one | They could scarcely have expected better treatment after the harsh way they had dealt with the Russians at Brest-Litovsk - also a "Diktat" | ⚠️ **Understandable but self-defeating.** Some historians think it would have been reasonable to let them take part, which might have toned down harsher terms and would have deprived Hitler of the "Diktat" argument |
| 2 | **Many provisions were not based on the Fourteen Points** | The Germans had approached Wilson in October 1918 expecting terms based on the Points | The Points had never been accepted as official by any state; the Germans themselves ignored them in January 1918 when outright victory still seemed possible; and two further points on civilian damage and disarmament were added before the armistice, of which Germany was aware. Most of the terms did comply with the Points and the additions | ⚠️ **Probably not a valid objection** |
| 3 | **Loss of territory in Europe** | Around a million Germans were left under Polish rule; almost three million in the Sudetenland under Czechoslovakia; Austria was a completely German state of some seven million and union was specifically forbidden | Alsace-Lorraine and West Prussia were both mentioned in the Points; the one concession to German written objections was Upper Silesia, where after a vote Germany kept about two-thirds; most of the German losses could be justified on grounds of nationality | ⚠️ **Split verdict: weak on the transfers, strong on self-determination** |
| 4 | **Loss of the African colonies** | This was hardly an "impartial adjustment" of colonial claims in the interests of the populations concerned, as Point 5 required | The colonies were transferred to League mandates rather than annexed outright | ⚠️ **Probably good grounds for objection.** The mandate was really a device by which the Allies seized the colonies without actually admitting that they were being annexed |
| 5 | **The disarmament clauses** | 100,000 troops were claimed to be insufficient to keep law and order at a time of political unrest; and no other power disarmed, despite Point 4's "all-round reduction of armaments" | The French desire for a weak Germany was understandable | ⚠️ **Justified to some extent**; and in any case disarmament was impossible to enforce fully, because the Germans were determined to exploit every loophole |

*Two further objections - Article 231 and reparations - are audited separately in sections 6 and 7 because they carry most of the weight and because their verdicts differ from each other.*

### 1. How to run an audit rather than a debate

⚠️ A "critically evaluate" directive is asking for a graded verdict, not a balanced-sounding paragraph. ⚠️ The technique used here has four steps, and it works on any evaluative question in this topic.

1. State the objection in its **strongest** form, using the objector's own best evidence.
2. State the reply, using recorded evidence rather than assertion.
3. Return a **verdict with a grade** - valid, partly valid, probably invalid - rather than "there are arguments on both sides".
4. Say what the verdict implies for the settlement as a whole.

⚠️ Steps 3 and 4 are where the marks are, and they are the two that scripts most often omit.

### 2. The dictated peace

✅ **Fact:** the Germans were not allowed into the discussions at Versailles; they were simply presented with the terms and told to sign. ✅ Although they were allowed to criticise the treaty in writing, all their criticisms were ignored except one.

✅ **Fact:** some historians feel that the Germans were justified in objecting, and that it would have been reasonable to allow them to take part in the discussions; this might have led to a toning-down of some of the harsher terms, and it would certainly have deprived the Germans of the argument much used by Hitler, that because the peace was a "Diktat" it should not be morally binding.

✅ **Fact:** on the other hand, it is possible to argue that the Germans could scarcely have expected any better treatment after the harsh way they had dealt with the Russians at Brest-Litovsk - also a "Diktat".

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two halves of that assessment answer different questions, and keeping them apart is the whole skill.
- *Was it fair?* ⚠️ Arguably not, and the objection is understandable.
- *Was it imprudent?* ⚠️ Yes, and this is the stronger criticism, because it is about consequences rather than about justice: excluding Germany created a grievance whose political value to the settlement's enemies exceeded any negotiating advantage the exclusion secured.
- *Was Germany entitled to complain?* ⚠️ Weakened considerably by Brest-Litovsk, which Germany had imposed on a defeated enemy in March 1918 by exactly the method it objected to in June 1919.

### 3. The Fourteen Points objection

✅ **Fact:** the Germans claimed they had been promised terms based on Wilson's Fourteen Points, that many provisions were not based on them, and that the settlement was therefore a swindle.

✅ **Fact:** this is probably not a valid objection, for three recorded reasons. ✅ The Points had never been accepted as official by any of the states involved. ✅ The Germans themselves had ignored them in January 1918, when there still seemed a chance of outright German victory. ✅ And by November, German tactics - Brest-Litovsk, and the destruction of mines, factories and public buildings during the retreat through France and Belgium - had hardened the Allied attitude and led Wilson to add two further points: that Germany should pay for damage to civilian population and property, and should be reduced to "virtual impotence". ✅ The Germans were aware of this when they accepted the armistice, and in fact most of the terms did comply with the Points and the additions.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the cleanest verdict in the audit, and the reason is structural. ⚠️ An objection that a promise was broken requires that a promise was made, that it was accepted by both parties, and that the complaining party had honoured it. ⚠️ All three conditions fail here. ⚠️ An answer that states the three failures in one sentence has disposed of the most-cited German grievance more efficiently than a paragraph of assertion.

### 4. Territorial losses: the split verdict

✅ **Fact:** the objection covered Alsace-Lorraine and especially West Prussia, which gave Poland access to the sea. ✅ However, both were mentioned in the Fourteen Points.

✅ **Fact:** originally Upper Silesia, an industrial region with a mixed population of Poles and Germans, was to be given to Poland, but this was the one concession made to the German written objections: after a vote among the population, Germany was allowed to keep about two-thirds of the area.

✅ **Fact:** in fact most of the German losses could be justified on grounds of nationality.

✅ **Fact:** where the Germans did have genuine cause for protest was on the question of national self-determination. ✅ Right from the start of the peace conference the Allies had emphasised that all nationalities should have the right to choose which country they wanted to belong to; this principle had been applied in the case of non-Germans, but the settlement left around a million Germans under Polish rule and almost three million in the Sudetenland controlled by the new state of Czechoslovakia. ✅ In addition, Austria was a completely German state with a population of some seven million; all these Germans wanted to become part of Germany, but unification was specifically forbidden, probably because that would have made Germany larger and more powerful even than in 1914.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the split verdict is the most important single finding in this lesson, and it should be written in exactly this form: *the German objection to the transfers is weak, and the German objection to the inconsistency is strong*. ⚠️ Germany lost territory that was largely not German; Germans were left outside Germany where they were; and the union that nationality required was prohibited for a reason of power. ⚠️ A candidate who can state that distinction has understood the treaty better than one who simply calls it harsh.

✅ **Fact:** the account also records the Allied justification for the Sudeten and Polish cases - that the new states needed those populations in order to be economically viable - and its own comment that it was unfortunate that both cases later gave Hitler an excuse to begin territorial demands on those countries. ⚠️ The boundary with the interwar owner is here; this session records the provision and its later exploitability and stops.

### 5. The colonial objection

✅ **Fact:** the Germans probably had good grounds for objection to the loss of their African colonies, which was hardly an "impartial adjustment". ✅ The mandate system allowed Britain to take over German East Africa, or Tanganyika, and parts of Togoland and the Cameroons; France to take most of Togoland and the Cameroons; and South Africa to acquire German South West Africa, now Namibia. ✅ But this was really a device by which the Allies seized the colonies without actually admitting that they were being annexed.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two things must be held together here, and a good answer says so explicitly. ⚠️ The German objection is *technically* well founded - the transfer was not an impartial adjustment in the interests of the populations concerned, as Point 5 had promised. ⚠️ But the objection is *morally* peculiar, because it is a complaint by one colonial power that its colonies were given to other colonial powers rather than returned to it, and neither party proposed consulting the populations. ⚠️ Stating that asymmetry converts a treaty-audit point into a point about the whole imperial order, which is what Lesson 21 needs.

### 6. Article 231: the six-week commission

✅ **Fact:** the Germans objected to being saddled with the entire blame for the outbreak of war. ✅ There are some grounds for objection here, because although later research seems to indicate Germany's guilt, it was hardly possible to arrive at that conclusion in the space of six weeks during 1919, which is what the Special Commission on War Responsibility did. ✅ However, the Allies wanted the Germans to admit responsibility so that they would be liable to pay reparations.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most carefully balanced finding in the whole audit and it deserves to be reproduced with its structure intact.

| Component | ⚠️ Finding |
|---|---|
| The substantive question of responsibility for 1914 | ✅ Later research seems to indicate Germany's guilt - though that question belongs to the previous topic, not to this one |
| The procedure by which the 1919 conclusion was reached | ✅ A commission working for six weeks could hardly settle it - a genuine ground for objection |
| The clause's actual legal function | ✅ To establish liability for reparations |
| The clause's political effect | ✅ It became known as the war-guilt clause and supplied permanent grievance; ✅ the advanced material records it as grounds for German resentment because of its political meaning, though its immediate legal function was reparations liability |

⚠️ The lesson generalises: the objection to Article 231 is best understood as an objection to a *judicial* claim being made for a *fiscal* purpose on an *inadequate* procedure. ⚠️ That formulation is defensible on all three counts and does not require the candidate to take a position on the causes of the war, which belong elsewhere.

### 7. Reparations: the strongest objection in the audit

✅ **Fact:** reparations were the final humiliation for the Germans. ✅ Though there could be little valid objection to the general principle of reparations, many historians now agree that the actual amount decided on was far too high at £6600 million.

✅ **Fact:** some people thought so at the time, including J. M. Keynes, who was an economic adviser to the British delegation at the conference and urged the Allies to take £2000 million, which he said was a more reasonable amount that Germany would be able to afford.

✅ **Fact:** the figure of £6600 million enabled the Germans to protest that it was impossible to pay, and they soon began to default on their annual instalments. ✅ This caused resentment among the Allies, who were relying on German cash to help them pay their own war debts to the USA, and there was international tension when France tried to force the Germans to pay. ✅ Eventually the Allies admitted their mistake and reduced the amount to £2000 million under the Young Plan of 1929 - but not before reparations had proved disastrous, both economically and politically.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice the structure of this finding, which is unlike the others. ⚠️ The *principle* is conceded to the Allies; the *quantum* is conceded to the Germans; and the verdict is delivered by events, because the figure was in fact reduced to precisely the sum Keynes had proposed a decade earlier. ⚠️ That is as close to a decided question as this topic offers, and an answer should say so. ⚠️ The economic mechanism - default, Allied dependence on German payments to service American debt, and the resulting international tension - belongs to Lesson 22.

### 8. The verdict a balanced answer should return

⚠️ Assembling the seven findings produces a graded conclusion rather than a verdict of "harsh" or "not harsh".

| Objection | ⚠️ Grade |
|---|---|
| Dictated peace | Understandable, weakened by Brest-Litovsk, and imprudent of the Allies |
| Not based on the Fourteen Points | Probably invalid |
| Territorial transfers | Largely invalid; most losses defensible on nationality |
| Self-determination applied inconsistently | **Valid and serious** |
| Loss of colonies | Probably valid on the stated principle; morally peculiar in the mouth of a colonial power |
| Disarmament | Valid to some extent; and unenforceable in practice |
| Article 231 | Valid as to procedure and political effect; the substantive question belongs elsewhere |
| Reparations quantum | **Valid, and effectively conceded by the Young Plan reduction** |

⚠️ **The sentence to write:** ✅ Versailles was harsh enough to embitter Germany but not harsh enough to render Germany permanently incapable of recovery - which is a judgement about the *relation* between severity and enforcement rather than about severity alone.

### 9. Objections to the audit itself

**The objection.** ⚠️ Does auditing German complaints not centre the perpetrator's grievance at the expense of the states Germany had invaded?

**The reply.** ⚠️ The audit exists because the German grievance became a political force with consequences, not because it is the most deserving claim of 1919. ⚠️ The balancing facts belong in the same answer and are recorded: ✅ much of France, Poland and the Balkans had been ravaged by occupying troops, whereas German territory was virtually untouched; ✅ Germany had imposed Brest-Litovsk; ✅ German forces destroyed mines, factories and public buildings during the retreat through France and Belgium. ⚠️ An answer that audits the objections without those facts has produced an unbalanced sympathy; an answer that includes them has produced an assessment.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ If most objections fail, why did the grievance prove so powerful?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Because political force does not track legal merit. ⚠️ The grievance was powerful because of the *form* in which the settlement was delivered - exclusion from negotiation, a clause that read as a confession of guilt, an unquantified and then enormous bill, and a prohibition on a union that the victors' own principle required - and because the German army had marched home intact from foreign soil. ⚠️ Lesson 12 established the last point; Lesson 24 draws the conclusion.

**The residual.** ⚠️ The substantive question of responsibility for the outbreak of the war is owned by the previous topic and is not adjudicated here.

### 10. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the advanced material records the framework for this question directly: state the terms briefly, present the German objections, evaluate them one by one, and conclude that reparations and political symbolism were especially damaging.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Was the Treaty of Versailles too harsh? Critically examine. (Directive: critically examine. Ceiling: 250 words.)
> ⚠️ "German objections to the Treaty of Versailles were more politically potent than legally sound." Discuss. (Directive: discuss. Ceiling: 250 words.)

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Do not attempt all seven objections at 250 words; take four - dictated peace, Fourteen Points, self-determination and reparations - and grade each. ⚠️ Use Brest-Litovsk once, as the reply to the dictation complaint. ⚠️ Use the Keynes figure once, as the evidence that the quantum objection was conceded in 1929. ⚠️ Close with the harsh-enough-but-not-harsh-enough formulation, which is a verdict rather than a summary.

### Traps

- Concluding that the treaty was simply harsh, or simply justified. The findings differ by objection.
- Omitting Brest-Litovsk, which is the single most effective reply to the dictation complaint.
- Missing the split verdict on territory: weak on transfers, strong on inconsistency.
- Saying the colonies were annexed. ✅ They became mandates - a device for seizure without admitting annexation.
- Forgetting the Upper Silesia concession, which is the only German written objection that succeeded.
- Giving Keynes's figure as the amount imposed. ✅ £6600 million was imposed; ✅ £2000 million was his proposal and later the Young Plan figure.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Dictated peace: Germans excluded from discussions, presented with terms, all written criticisms ignored except one; but they could scarcely have expected better after Brest-Litovsk, also a "Diktat".
2. ✅ Allowing German participation might have toned down harsher terms and would have deprived Hitler of the "Diktat" argument.
3. ✅ The Fourteen Points objection is probably invalid: the Points were never accepted as official by any state; Germany ignored them in January 1918; two further points on civilian damage and disarmament were added before the armistice with German knowledge; most terms complied.
4. ✅ Alsace-Lorraine and West Prussia were both mentioned in the Points; most German losses were justifiable on nationality grounds.
5. ✅ Upper Silesia was the one concession to German written objections: after a vote Germany kept about two-thirds.
6. ✅ Genuine self-determination grievance: around a million Germans under Polish rule; almost three million in the Sudetenland; Austria a completely German state of some seven million with union specifically forbidden.
7. ✅ The Allies justified the Sudeten and Polish cases on the economic viability of the new states; the account calls it unfortunate that both later gave Hitler an excuse.
8. ✅ Colonies: probably good grounds for objection, since this was hardly an impartial adjustment; the mandate was really a device for seizure without admitting annexation; Tanganyika, parts of Togoland and the Cameroons to Britain, most of Togoland and the Cameroons to France, German South West Africa to South Africa.
9. ✅ Disarmament: the objection was justified to some extent; no other power disarmed despite Point 4; full enforcement was impossible because the Germans exploited every loophole.
10. ✅ Article 231: some grounds, because a Special Commission on War Responsibility could hardly settle the question in six weeks in 1919; the Allies wanted the admission so that Germany would be liable for reparations.
11. ✅ Reparations: little valid objection in principle; the amount of £6600 million is widely judged too high; Keynes urged £2000 million; Germany defaulted; the Allies relied on German cash for their own American war debts; the amount was reduced to £2000 million under the Young Plan of 1929, after reparations had proved disastrous economically and politically.
12. ⚠️ The verdict: Versailles was harsh enough to embitter Germany but not harsh enough to render it permanently incapable of recovery.

### Concept check

**Question:** Grade the German objections to the Treaty of Versailles, and identify the one that is strongest and the one that is weakest, with reasons.

**Model answer:** The objections grade very differently, and an assessment that treats them as a single complaint loses the argument. The weakest is the claim that the treaty departed from the Fourteen Points. It fails three times over: the Points had never been accepted as official by any of the states involved; the Germans themselves ignored them in January 1918 when outright victory still seemed possible; and two further points - that Germany should pay for damage to civilian population and property and should be reduced to virtual impotence - were added before the armistice in response to Brest-Litovsk and to the German destruction of mines, factories and public buildings during the retreat, with Germany aware of the additions when it accepted the armistice. The territorial objection is almost as weak, since Alsace-Lorraine and West Prussia were both named in the Points and most German losses could be justified on grounds of nationality, while Upper Silesia was actually conceded after a vote in which Germany kept about two-thirds. The dictated-peace objection is understandable but undercut by Brest-Litovsk, which Germany had imposed by the same method. The strongest is reparations, and the evidence is decisive: many historians agree the figure of £6600 million announced in 1921 was far too high; Keynes, an economic adviser to the British delegation, urged £2000 million at the time as an amount Germany could afford; Germany defaulted; and the Allies eventually reduced the sum to exactly £2000 million under the Young Plan of 1929 - conceding the objection a decade late and after reparations had proved disastrous economically and politically. A close second is the self-determination inconsistency: around a million Germans were left under Polish rule, almost three million in the Sudetenland, and union with a completely German Austria of some seven million was specifically forbidden.

**Misconception to avoid:** Answering "was Versailles too harsh?" with a single adjective. The treaty was harsh in specific clauses and defensible in others, and its decisive defect was not severity but the combination of severity with an unwillingness to enforce - it was harsh enough to embitter Germany and not harsh enough to disable it.

*Versailles is one treaty of five. The other four are where the map of Europe and West Asia was actually redrawn - and one of them was simply refused.*

## Lesson 19 - The other four treaties, and why Lausanne is the proof case

Progress: 19/25 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: why the settlement is a cluster and not a treaty; St Germain and the Austrian losses; Trianon, Bela Kun and the Hungarian losses; Neuilly and the Bulgarian grievance; Sevres, the Turkish rejection, Chanak and Lausanne; the economic consequences for Austria and Hungary; the tariff problem in the Danube basin; what Lausanne proves about the whole settlement

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's sections on the peace treaties with Austria-Hungary and on the settlement with Turkey and Bulgaria, including the full St Germain and Trianon transfers, the delay to Trianon caused by Bela Kun's communist seizure of power in Budapest and its overthrow, the statement that both treaties contained the League Covenant, the assessment that on the whole they kept to the spirit of self-determination while leaving three million Germans in the Sudetenland and a million under Polish rule, the population and industrial consequences for Austria and Hungary and the League loans, the tariff problem, the full Sevres terms, the Turkish rejection under Mustafa Kemal, the expulsion of the Greeks from Smyrna, the Italian and French withdrawal from the Straits leaving British troops at Chanak, the revision at Lausanne in 1923, the statement that Turkey was the first state to challenge the Paris settlement successfully, the Arab and Palestine legacy, and the Neuilly terms with the Bulgarian claim about a million Bulgars under foreign governments
