CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: five treaties, five defeated states, one refusal

| Treaty | Year | Defeated state | ✅ Principal transfers and terms | ⚠️ Fate |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Versailles** | 1919 | Germany | See Lesson 17 in full | ⚠️ Accepted under protest; revised piecemeal later |
| **St Germain** | 1919 | Austria | ✅ Bohemia and Moravia, wealthy industrial provinces with a population of ten million, to the new state of Czechoslovakia; ✅ Dalmatia, Bosnia and Herzegovina to Serbia, which with Montenegro became Yugoslavia; ✅ Bukovina to Romania; ✅ Galicia to the reconstituted state of Poland; ✅ the South Tyrol as far as the Brenner Pass, Trentino, Istria and Trieste to Italy | ⚠️ Accepted; Austria left economically unviable |
| **Neuilly** | 1919 | Bulgaria | ✅ Territory lost to Greece, depriving her of her Aegean coastline, and also to Yugoslavia and Romania | ⚠️ Accepted under protest; ✅ Bulgaria could claim with some justification that at least a million Bulgars were under foreign governments |
| **Trianon** | 1920 | Hungary | ✅ Slovakia and Ruthenia to Czechoslovakia; ✅ Croatia and Slovenia to Yugoslavia; ✅ Transylvania and the Banat of Temesvar to Romania | ⚠️ Delayed to 1920 by political uncertainty in Budapest, where the communists under Bela Kun seized power and were later overthrown |
| **Sevres** | 1920 | Turkey | ✅ Eastern Thrace, many Aegean islands and Smyrna to Greece; ✅ Adalia and Rhodes to Italy; ✅ the Straits permanently open; ✅ Syria a French mandate, and Palestine, Iraq and Transjordan British mandates | ✅ **REJECTED.** Revised by the Treaty of Lausanne, 1923 |

✅ **Fact:** both the Austrian and Hungarian treaties contained the League of Nations Covenant.

*The table is the answer to the commonest single error on this topic. ⚠️ Read the last column: four treaties were accepted and one was refused, and the one that was refused was refused successfully. ⚠️ That asymmetry is the settlement's most revealing feature and the subject of section 5.*

### 1. Why the settlement is a cluster

⚠️ An answer that says "the Treaty of Versailles" when the question says "the peace settlement" has made a factual error in its first sentence and will be marked accordingly. ⚠️ There were five treaties, negotiated over four and a half years, with five defeated states, on different principles and with different results. ⚠️ The differences are the analysis.

✅ **Fact:** the task facing the conference in central Europe was not design but ratification. ✅ When Austria was on the verge of defeat the Habsburg Empire disintegrated as the various nationalities declared themselves independent; Austria and Hungary separated and declared themselves republics; many important decisions had therefore already been taken before the peace conference met, and the task of the conference was to formalise and recognise what had taken place.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** so the central European treaties are best described as *recognition instruments with adjustments*, and the adjustments - which populations went with which state, which frontier ran where - are where the grievances lie. ⚠️ The empire was destroyed by its own nationalities; the *frontiers* were drawn at Paris.

### 2. The assessment of St Germain and Trianon

✅ **Fact:** these settlements may seem harsh, but much of what was agreed had already happened, and on the whole they did keep to the spirit of self-determination. ✅ More people were placed under governments of their own nationality than ever before in Europe - though they were not always as democratic as Wilson would have liked, especially in Hungary and Poland.

✅ **Fact:** there were nevertheless deviations from the pattern: the three million Germans in the Sudetenland who now found themselves in Czechoslovakia, and the million Germans placed under Polish rule. ✅ The Allies justified this on the grounds that the new states needed them in order to be economically viable. ✅ It was unfortunate that both these cases gave Hitler an excuse to begin territorial demands on these countries.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most balanced paragraph in the whole settlement material and it should be reproduced in substance in any answer on self-determination. ⚠️ It concedes achievement - more people under governments of their own nationality than ever before in Europe - and identifies the exceptions and their justification, which was *economic viability* rather than principle. ⚠️ The pattern is consistent: where nationality conflicted with the viability or the security of a new state, viability and security won. ⚠️ Lesson 21 generalises this into the settlement's organising rule.

### 3. The economic consequences for Austria and Hungary

✅ **Fact:** the treaties left both Austria and Hungary with serious economic problems.

✅ **Fact:** Austria was a small republic, its population reduced from twenty-two million to six and a half million; most of its industrial wealth had been lost to Czechoslovakia and Poland. ✅ Vienna, once the capital of the huge Habsburg Empire, was left high and dry, surrounded by farming land which could hardly support it. ✅ Not surprisingly, Austria was soon facing a severe economic crisis and was constantly having to be helped out by loans from the League of Nations.

✅ **Fact:** Hungary was just as badly affected, her population reduced from twenty-one million to seven and a half million, with some of her richest corn land lost to Romania.

✅ **Fact:** matters were further complicated when all the new states quickly introduced tariffs - import and export duties. ✅ These hampered the flow of trade through the whole Danube area and made the industrial recovery of Austria particularly difficult. ✅ In fact there was an excellent economic case to support a union between Austria and Germany.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is one of the most valuable and least used passages in the topic, and it carries three arguments at once.

1. **The successor states were economically as well as militarily weak.** A currency area, a customs union and an integrated industrial economy had been divided into a set of small protectionist states, each of which then taxed the others' goods.
2. **The tariff point is a direct causal mechanism**, not a general observation: the trade of the Danube basin was hampered by the new frontiers themselves, so the settlement's political success was purchased at an economic cost that it did not then relieve.
3. **The economic case for Anschluss is recorded by the account**, which makes the prohibition of union doubly vulnerable - denied on national grounds despite nationality, and denied on political grounds despite economics.

⚠️ Austria surviving on League loans is also, incidentally, evidence that the new international organisation did substantive economic work, which is worth remembering when Lesson 20 assesses it.

### 4. Bulgaria: the smallest treaty and a real grievance

✅ **Fact:** by the Treaty of Neuilly of 1919, Bulgaria lost territory to Greece, depriving her of her Aegean coastline, and also to Yugoslavia and Romania. ✅ She could claim, with some justification, that at least a million Bulgars were under foreign governments as a result.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Bulgarian case is a miniature of the whole settlement's problem and is worth one sentence in a long answer. ⚠️ A defeated state lost a coastline - an economic and strategic asset - and a population of co-nationals, on the same principle that was used to justify creating states for other co-nationals. ⚠️ It is also a reminder that revisionism was not a German monopoly: the settlement created a bloc of dissatisfied states, and the account's verdict records exactly that division between states that wanted to revise the settlement and those that wanted to preserve it.

### 5. Sevres, Chanak and Lausanne: the proof case

✅ **Fact:** by the Treaty of Sevres of 1920, Turkey was to lose Eastern Thrace, many Aegean islands and Smyrna to Greece; Adalia and Rhodes to Italy; the Straits, the exit from the Black Sea, were to be permanently open; Syria became a French mandate, and Palestine, Iraq and Transjordan British mandates.

✅ **Fact:** however, the loss of so much territory to Greece, especially Smyrna on the Turkish mainland, outraged Turkish national feeling - self-determination was being ignored in this case. ✅ Led by Mustafa Kemal, the Turks rejected the treaty and chased the Greeks out of Smyrna. ✅ The Italians and French withdrew their occupying forces from the Straits area, leaving only British troops at Chanak. ✅ Eventually a compromise was reached and the settlement was revised by the Treaty of Lausanne of 1923, by which Turkey regained Eastern Thrace including Constantinople, and Smyrna.

✅ **Fact:** Turkey was therefore the first state to challenge the Paris settlement successfully.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the single most instructive episode in the entire settlement, and it should be treated as a controlled experiment rather than as a regional curiosity. ⚠️ Set the conditions out.

| Condition | ⚠️ Turkey in 1920-23 | ⚠️ Germany in 1919-23 |
|---|---|---|
| Defeated | ✅ Yes, and an armistice signed at Mudros on 30 October 1918 | ✅ Yes |
| Treaty imposed without negotiation | ✅ Yes | ✅ Yes |
| Territory lost to a neighbour on grounds the state rejected | ✅ Yes - Smyrna and Eastern Thrace to Greece | ✅ Yes - West Prussia and Posen to Poland |
| Possessed an organised armed force willing and able to fight | ✅ Yes, the nationalist movement under Mustafa Kemal | ❌ No - the army was limited to 100,000 and the Rhineland was occupied |
| Faced victors willing to fight to enforce the treaty | ❌ No - the Italians and French withdrew from the Straits area, leaving only British troops at Chanak | ⚠️ Partially - France did attempt enforcement over reparations |
| **Outcome** | ✅ **Treaty rewritten in the defeated state's favour** | ⚠️ Treaty maintained in form and eroded over time |

⚠️ **The conclusion the table licenses:** the treaties of 1919-23 rested on power, not on consent. ⚠️ Where a defeated state had the military and political capacity to resist, and the victors lacked the will to compel, the settlement was simply rewritten. ⚠️ That single finding explains more about the settlement's instability than any argument about harshness, because it identifies the operative variable as *enforcement capacity* rather than *treaty content*. ⚠️ Lesson 24 builds its verdict on it.

✅ **Fact:** one legacy of Sevres was to cause problems later: the mandates were peopled largely by Arabs who had been hoping for independence as a reward after their struggle against the Turks, led by T. E. Lawrence; nor were the Arabs happy about the talk of establishing a Jewish "national home" in Palestine.

### 6. Comparison: how the five defeated states fared

| State | ⚠️ Kind of loss | ⚠️ Capacity to resist | ⚠️ Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Germany** | Territory, colonies, armed forces, money and reputation | ⚠️ Disarmed by treaty; economically intact and recovering | ⚠️ Grievance without immediate means; revision deferred |
| **Austria** | ✅ Population from 22 million to 6.5 million; most industrial wealth lost | ⚠️ None; dependent on League loans | ⚠️ A small state with an oversized capital and no viable economy |
| **Hungary** | ✅ Population from 21 million to 7.5 million; richest corn land lost to Romania | ⚠️ None; internal political turmoil under and after Bela Kun | ⚠️ The most aggrieved successor state in central Europe |
| **Bulgaria** | ✅ Aegean coastline and territory to three neighbours; ✅ at least a million Bulgars under foreign governments | ⚠️ None | ⚠️ A permanent revisionist in the Balkans |
| **Turkey** | ✅ Under Sevres: Thrace, Aegean islands, Smyrna, Adalia, Rhodes, the Arab provinces and control of the Straits | ✅ **Real** - an organised nationalist movement under Mustafa Kemal | ✅ **Sevres rejected; Lausanne 1923 restored Eastern Thrace including Constantinople, and Smyrna** |

⚠️ The column that predicts the outcome is the third, not the second. ⚠️ Severity did not determine the result; capacity did.

### 7. How far the Lausanne argument can be pushed

**The first objection.** ⚠️ Is it fair to call the central European treaties "harsh" when the Habsburg Empire had already dissolved itself?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Partly not, and the account's own assessment says so: much of what was agreed had already happened, and on the whole the treaties kept to the spirit of self-determination, placing more people under governments of their own nationality than ever before in Europe. ⚠️ What was genuinely imposed, and is properly criticised, is the *economic* result: ✅ Austria reduced from twenty-two million to six and a half million with most of its industrial wealth gone; ✅ Hungary from twenty-one million to seven and a half; ✅ a tariff regime that hampered trade through the whole Danube area. ⚠️ The criticism that survives is therefore economic rather than national.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Does the Lausanne argument not prove too much - that any defeated state could have rewritten its treaty by force?

**The reply.** ⚠️ No, because the conditions were specific and can be enumerated: an organised armed movement, a defensible national territory, a victor coalition whose members had divergent interests in the region, and an opposing claimant - Greece - whose position could be defeated militarily without engaging the great powers directly. ✅ The Italians and French withdrew from the Straits area, leaving only British troops at Chanak, which is precisely the divergence of victor interests the argument requires. ⚠️ Germany in 1919-23 satisfied none of the first three conditions. ⚠️ The claim is not that resistance always works; it is that the settlement's durability depended on the victors' willingness to enforce, and that this willingness was already conditional by 1922.

**What is not settled.** ⚠️ The detailed terms of Lausanne beyond the recovery of Eastern Thrace, Constantinople and Smyrna are not held in this session's evidence base and are not described here.

### 8. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the demand map warns explicitly against treating Versailles as the whole settlement and requires an answer to distinguish Versailles for Germany, St Germain for Austria, Trianon for Hungary, Sevres and Lausanne for Turkey, and Neuilly for Bulgaria.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Discuss the major terms and major weaknesses of the post-1919 peace settlement. (Directive: discuss. Ceiling: 250 words.)
> ⚠️ "The peace settlement rested on power rather than on consent." Examine with reference to the Treaty of Lausanne. (Directive: examine. Ceiling: 250 words.)

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Name all five treaties with their states in one sentence; this costs about twenty-five words and immediately distinguishes the answer. ⚠️ Give St Germain and Trianon together, since they are the same dissolution in two instruments. ⚠️ Spend the analytical weight on Lausanne, using the controlled-experiment table, because it converts a description of treaties into an argument about the settlement.

### Traps

- Writing "the Treaty of Versailles" for "the peace settlement".
- Attaching the wrong treaty to the wrong state. ✅ St Germain - Austria; Trianon - Hungary; Neuilly - Bulgaria; Sevres and Lausanne - Turkey.
- Dating Trianon to 1919. ✅ It was delayed to 1920 by political uncertainty in Budapest under and after Bela Kun.
- Saying Turkey accepted Sevres. ✅ The Turks rejected it and forced revision at Lausanne.
- Omitting the economic consequences for Austria and Hungary, which are the strongest criticism available of the central European treaties.
- Forgetting that both the Austrian and Hungarian treaties contained the League Covenant.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Five treaties: Versailles (Germany, 1919); St Germain (Austria, 1919); Neuilly (Bulgaria, 1919); Trianon (Hungary, 1920); Sevres (Turkey, 1920), revised by Lausanne (1923).
2. ✅ St Germain: Bohemia and Moravia, with ten million people, to Czechoslovakia; Dalmatia, Bosnia and Herzegovina to Serbia, which with Montenegro became Yugoslavia; Bukovina to Romania; Galicia to Poland; the South Tyrol as far as the Brenner Pass, Trentino, Istria and Trieste to Italy.
3. ✅ Trianon: Slovakia and Ruthenia to Czechoslovakia; Croatia and Slovenia to Yugoslavia; Transylvania and the Banat of Temesvar to Romania; delayed to 1920 by the communist seizure of power under Bela Kun and its overthrow.
4. ✅ Both treaties contained the League Covenant.
5. ✅ On the whole they kept to the spirit of self-determination, placing more people under governments of their own nationality than ever before in Europe, though not always as democratically as Wilson would have liked, especially in Hungary and Poland.
6. ✅ Deviations: three million Germans in the Sudetenland; a million Germans under Polish rule; justified on the economic viability of the new states.
7. ✅ Austria: population from twenty-two million to six and a half million; most industrial wealth lost to Czechoslovakia and Poland; Vienna surrounded by farmland that could hardly support it; severe economic crisis; repeated League loans.
8. ✅ Hungary: population from twenty-one million to seven and a half million; richest corn land lost to Romania.
9. ✅ New states introduced tariffs, hampering trade through the whole Danube area; there was an excellent economic case for union between Austria and Germany.
10. ✅ Neuilly: Bulgaria lost its Aegean coastline to Greece and territory to Yugoslavia and Romania; at least a million Bulgars were left under foreign governments.
11. ✅ Sevres: Eastern Thrace, many Aegean islands and Smyrna to Greece; Adalia and Rhodes to Italy; the Straits permanently open; Syria a French mandate; Palestine, Iraq and Transjordan British mandates.
12. ✅ Turkish rejection under Mustafa Kemal; the Greeks driven from Smyrna; Italian and French withdrawal from the Straits leaving British troops at Chanak; revision by Lausanne in 1923 restoring Eastern Thrace including Constantinople, and Smyrna.
13. ✅ Turkey was the first state to challenge the Paris settlement successfully.
14. ⚠️ The finding: the treaties rested on power, not consent; capacity to resist, not severity of terms, predicted the outcome.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Treaty of Lausanne is the most analytically important document of the entire peace settlement, even though it concerns the smallest of the defeated great powers.

**Model answer:** It is the most important because it is the settlement's only controlled test of what the treaties actually rested on. Under Sevres in 1920 Turkey was to lose Eastern Thrace, many Aegean islands and Smyrna to Greece, Adalia and Rhodes to Italy, and the Arab provinces as mandates - Syria to France, Palestine, Iraq and Transjordan to Britain - with the Straits permanently open. The loss of Smyrna on the Turkish mainland outraged Turkish national feeling, since self-determination was being ignored in that case. What then happened distinguishes Turkey from every other defeated state: led by Mustafa Kemal, the Turks rejected the treaty and drove the Greeks out of Smyrna; the Italians and French withdrew their occupying forces from the Straits area, leaving only British troops at Chanak; and a compromise produced the Treaty of Lausanne in 1923, by which Turkey regained Eastern Thrace including Constantinople, and Smyrna. Turkey was therefore the first state to challenge the Paris settlement successfully. The analytical value lies in isolating the variable. Germany, Austria, Hungary and Bulgaria all received imposed treaties and all accepted them; Turkey received an imposed treaty and rewrote it. The difference was not the severity of the terms, since Sevres was at least as severe as any of the others, and it was not the justice of the cause. It was capacity: Turkey possessed an organised armed movement and a defensible national territory, and the victors' interests in the region diverged sufficiently that two of them withdrew rather than fight. The conclusion is that the 1919-23 settlement rested on the victors' continuing willingness to enforce it, and that this willingness was already conditional by 1922.

**Misconception to avoid:** Filing Lausanne as a Middle Eastern footnote. It is the settlement's own demonstration of its central weakness, and a candidate who uses it as such is making an argument that the examiner rarely sees.

*The treaties redrew frontiers. The settlement also created machinery intended to make those frontiers durable - and the machinery is where the contradictions of 1919 are written down in legal form.*

## Lesson 20 - The League, the Covenant, the mandates and the minorities system

Progress: 20/25 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the origins of the League as a collective project rather than one man's idea; the Covenant as part of the treaties and what that linkage did; the mandate mechanism and its classes; the supervising machinery; the ambiguity of the mandate as an instrument; the minorities treaties and the complaint procedure; what the machinery achieved and what it could not; the boundary with the interwar owner

### Visual: three pieces of machinery, built into the treaties themselves

```text
   Covenant Article 22                        MINORITIES TREATIES

   territories transferred to victor          enlarged states as a

   and prepare them for independence          beginning with the Polish
        |                                     treaty of 28 June 1919

            nearest to independence,          CONTENT: civil, political

            must prevent abuses               education and language use

            areas, administered as            ENFORCEMENT: complaints
            integral portions of the          could be brought to the
            mandatory's territory             League
        |                                           |

   receives and scrutinises annual reports and      |
   advises the League Council - but has NO          |
   ENFORCEMENT POWER                                |

               territorial integrity and political independence

               an act of war against all members, who may impose
               economic and military sanctions

   Covenant, refused to ratify the settlement and NEVER JOINED
```

*The chart divides the machinery into three because they are three different instruments doing three different jobs - territorial administration, minority protection and collective security. ⚠️ The League's record in operation belongs to the interwar owner. ⚠️ What belongs here is the design, the ambiguity built into it, and the defect that was already visible when it came into force.*

### 1. The origins: not one man's idea

✅ **Fact:** the League is often spoken of as the brainchild of the American President Woodrow Wilson. ✅ Although Wilson was certainly a great supporter of the idea of an international organisation for peace, the League was the result of a coming together of similar suggestions made during the First World War by a number of world statesmen: ✅ Lord Robert Cecil of Britain, Jan Smuts of South Africa and Leon Bourgeois of France put forward detailed schemes showing how such an organisation might be set up.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the correction matters for two reasons. ⚠️ First, it is a small factual point on which many scripts are wrong, and getting it right signals reading. ⚠️ Second, it changes the interpretation of the American refusal to join: if the League had been Wilson's personal project, the Senate's rejection would be a story about one man's failure; since it was a multinational design with British, South African and French authorship, the American absence is a structural defect in a shared institution rather than a personal tragedy.

✅ **Fact:** the League formally came into existence on 10 January 1920, the same day that the Versailles Treaty came into operation, with headquarters in Geneva.

### 2. The Covenant inside the treaties: a fatal linkage

✅ **Fact:** a League of Nations was formed, and its aims and organisation were set out in the League Covenant, which was part of the Treaty of Versailles. ✅ The Austrian and Hungarian treaties also contained the Covenant.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most consequential piece of drafting in the whole settlement, and it is rarely noticed. ⚠️ Because the Covenant was embedded in the peace treaties rather than concluded separately, three things followed.

1. **Joining the organisation meant accepting the settlement.** A state that objected to the territorial or financial provisions could not join the security system while rejecting the rest.
2. **Rejecting the settlement meant rejecting the organisation.** ✅ The USA failed to ratify the settlement and never joined the League. ⚠️ The two facts are a single fact.
3. **The organisation was identified with the victors.** ⚠️ An institution created to transcend the old system of power politics was legally attached to a settlement imposed by one coalition upon another, which made it, in the eyes of the defeated states, an instrument of the settlement rather than a forum above it.

✅ **Fact:** the consequence for France was immediate and specific: France was left completely disenchanted with the whole thing because the Anglo-American guarantee of her frontiers given in the agreement could not now apply.

### 3. The mandate system: the mechanism

✅ **Fact:** Germany's African colonies were taken away and became mandates under League of Nations supervision, which meant that various member states of the League "looked after" them. ✅ Under Sevres, Syria became a French mandate and Palestine, Iraq and Transjordan British mandates. ✅ Britain acquired Tanganyika, Britain and France divided Togoland and the Cameroons, and South Africa gained German South West Africa. ✅ Mandated territories were intended to be prepared for independence.

✅ **Fact:** the Covenant's mandate article established three classes of mandate. ✅ Class A covered former Ottoman territories judged closest to independence and requiring administrative advice and assistance. ✅ Class B covered African territories where the mandatory was responsible for administration and welfare, with obligations against abuses such as slavery and arms trafficking. ✅ Class C covered sparsely populated or remote territories to be administered as integral portions of the mandatory's own territory, subject to safeguards for the indigenous population.

✅ **Fact:** a standing mandates commission began work in 1921. ✅ It received and scrutinised annual reports from the mandatory powers and advised the League Council, but it possessed no enforcement power. ✅ The League also ran commissions and committees on mandates, disarmament, minorities, labour, health and welfare.

⚠️ **Provenance note for the reader's confidence:** the class structure and the standing commission were confirmed in this session's single bounded verification pass rather than taken from the core printed narrative, and the ledger records this. ⚠️ Nothing is asserted here about individual mandate administrations, which this session's evidence base does not hold.

### 4. The mandate as an instrument: the ambiguity stated on both sides

⚠️ This is the point at which a weak answer chooses a side and a strong one refuses to.

| The case that mandates were disguised annexation | The case that mandates were a genuine innovation |
|---|---|
| ✅ It was really a device by which the Allies seized the colonies without actually admitting that they were being annexed | ✅ Mandated territories were intended to be prepared for independence - a stated endpoint that colonial rule had never previously acknowledged |
| ✅ Class C territories were to be administered as integral portions of the mandatory's own territory, which is annexation in all but name | ✅ Supervision existed: annual reports were required and were scrutinised by a standing commission that advised the Council |
| ⚠️ The territories went to the victors, in proportion to their military presence, and no population was consulted | ⚠️ The mandatory's conduct became, for the first time, a matter of international account rather than of purely domestic imperial discretion |
| ✅ Wilson's Point 5 had promised an impartial adjustment of colonial claims in the interests of the populations concerned; this was hardly an impartial adjustment | ⚠️ The principle that imperial rule required justification and had an endpoint was conceded in writing, and later independence claims were made in its language |

⚠️ **The formulation to write:** the mandate system simultaneously legitimised continued European rule over non-European populations and conceded the principle that such rule was conditional and temporary. ⚠️ It is both the last instrument of imperial expansion and the first admission that empire was not permanent. ⚠️ Do not resolve the ambiguity in either direction; it is the ambiguity that is examinable.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the supervisory defect is also worth naming precisely. ⚠️ The commission could scrutinise and advise; it could not compel. ⚠️ That is the League's general defect - a capacity for judgement without a capacity for enforcement - appearing in its first and most concrete institutional form, five years before the disputes that made it famous.

### 5. The minorities system

✅ **Fact:** the settlement created a system of minorities treaties, beginning with the treaty imposed on Poland on 28 June 1919 - the same day as Versailles - and extended to other new and enlarged states. ✅ These treaties obliged the signatory states to guarantee civil, political and religious rights to all inhabitants regardless of origin, language or religion, with specific protections for minority education and language use, and they allowed complaints to be brought before the League. ✅ The League ran a minorities committee among its standing bodies.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the minorities system is the settlement's own admission that self-determination had failed to produce nationally homogeneous states, and it is best taught as such.

1. **It is evidence of good faith.** The peacemakers knew that ✅ three million Germans were left in the Sudetenland and ✅ a million under Polish rule, and they attempted to provide a remedy.
2. **It is evidence of the limits of the remedy.** ⚠️ Protection was imposed on the new and defeated states and not on the great powers, which made it look to its subjects like a badge of inferior sovereignty rather than a universal standard.
3. **It created a new kind of international claim.** ⚠️ A minority could petition an international body against its own government, which is an early precursor of later international human-rights procedure, and it is worth one clause in an answer that wants to show long-range significance.
4. **It also created an instrument that could be abused.** ⚠️ A revisionist state could take up the cause of co-nationals abroad, and the account itself notes that the Sudeten and Polish German populations later gave Hitler an excuse to begin territorial demands. ⚠️ The boundary with the interwar and fascism owners is exactly here.

### 6. What the machinery did well, and what it could not do

| Function | ⚠️ Capability | ✅ Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Technical and humanitarian work | ⚠️ Real and substantial | ✅ Commissions and committees on disarmament, minorities, labour, health and welfare; ✅ Austria repeatedly helped out by loans from the League |
| Territorial administration | ⚠️ Real but supervisory only | ✅ Danzig administered under the League; ✅ the Saar administered for fifteen years; ✅ mandate reports scrutinised without enforcement power |
| Minority protection | ⚠️ Procedural, not coercive | ✅ Complaints could be brought to the League |
| Collective security | ⚠️ Designed but structurally impaired from the outset | ✅ Article 10 and Article 16 existed; ✅ the USA never joined; ✅ Russia was outside the system |

⚠️ **Boundary.** ⚠️ How this machinery performed in the disputes of the 1920s and 1930s - and why it failed - belongs entirely to the interwar international-relations owner. ⚠️ This session establishes the design and the built-in defect and hands over.

### 7. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ Was the League not doomed from the start, given the American absence?

**The reply.** ⚠️ "Doomed" overstates what the 1919 evidence supports, and a careful answer distinguishes two claims. ⚠️ The American absence was a severe structural defect: it removed the largest economy from any sanctions regime, and ✅ it destroyed the Anglo-American guarantee on which France's acceptance of the settlement had partly rested. ⚠️ But the organisation did substantial work that did not depend on universal membership - ✅ loans that kept Austria solvent, administration of Danzig and the Saar, supervision of mandates, and committees on labour, health and welfare. ⚠️ The defensible statement is that the League was designed for a membership it never had, and that its non-coercive functions worked better than its coercive ones from the first day.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Is the mandate system not simply hypocrisy, so that the "ambiguity" argument is an evasion?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Hypocrisy is a description of motive; the ambiguity is a description of effect, and the two are compatible. ⚠️ The motive was substantially acquisitive - ✅ the account calls it a device for seizure without admitting annexation, and ✅ Class C mandates were to be administered as integral portions of the mandatory's own territory. ⚠️ The effect nonetheless included a written concession that imperial rule was conditional and temporary, a requirement of annual reporting, and an international body entitled to scrutinise. ⚠️ Later independence movements used that language and that record. ⚠️ An instrument can be cynically intended and still create obligations its authors did not want; saying so is analysis rather than evasion.

**The residual.** ⚠️ Mandate-by-mandate administrative detail, the composition and procedures of the supervising commission, and the operation of individual minorities petitions are not held in this session's evidence base and are not described here.

### 8. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** this lesson serves the "redrawal of national boundaries" clause and the colonization clause simultaneously, and it supplies the institutional evidence for Lesson 21's argument.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The mandate system was the last instrument of empire and the first admission that empire was temporary." Examine. (Directive: examine. Ceiling: 250 words.)
> ⚠️ Discuss the institutional machinery created by the peace settlement of 1919. (Directive: discuss. Ceiling: 250 words.)

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Treat the three instruments separately - mandates, minorities, collective security - because most scripts merge them into a general paragraph about the League. ⚠️ On mandates, always give both sides of the ambiguity and then the combined formulation. ⚠️ Mention the Covenant's embedding in the treaties, because it explains the American absence in a single clause. ⚠️ Do not stray into the League's operational record.

### Traps

- Calling the League Wilson's brainchild without qualification. ✅ Cecil, Smuts and Bourgeois put forward detailed schemes.
- Treating the Covenant as a separate agreement. ✅ It was part of Versailles, St Germain and Trianon.
- Describing mandates as simple annexation or as genuine trusteeship. Both are required, and the ambiguity is the answer.
- Inventing mandate administrative detail. The classes and the supervising commission are as far as this session's evidence goes.
- Omitting the minorities treaties, which are the settlement's own admission that self-determination had not produced homogeneous states.
- Carrying the League's operational failures into this answer. They belong to the interwar owner.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The League formally came into existence on 10 January 1920, the day Versailles came into operation; headquarters in Geneva.
2. ✅ It was not one man's idea: Wilson supported it, but detailed schemes came from Lord Robert Cecil of Britain, Jan Smuts of South Africa and Leon Bourgeois of France.
3. ✅ The Covenant was part of the Treaty of Versailles and of the Austrian and Hungarian treaties.
4. ✅ The USA never joined; France was left disenchanted because the Anglo-American guarantee of her frontiers could not apply.
5. ✅ German colonies became mandates under League supervision; member states "looked after" them and were intended to prepare them for independence.
6. ✅ Mandate classes: A, former Ottoman territories nearest independence, requiring advice and assistance; B, African territories administered with welfare obligations and prohibitions on abuses; C, sparsely populated or remote territories administered as integral portions of the mandatory's own territory.
7. ✅ A standing mandates commission from 1921 scrutinised annual reports and advised the Council, without enforcement power.
8. ✅ Distribution: Syria to France; Palestine, Iraq and Transjordan to Britain; Tanganyika to Britain; Togoland and the Cameroons divided between Britain and France; German South West Africa to South Africa.
9. ✅ The League ran commissions and committees on mandates, disarmament, minorities, labour, health and welfare, and made loans that repeatedly rescued Austria.
10. ✅ The minorities system began with the treaty imposed on Poland on 28 June 1919, guaranteeing civil, political and religious rights regardless of origin, language or religion, with complaint to the League.
11. ✅ Covenant Article 10 bound members to respect and preserve territorial integrity and political independence against external aggression; Article 16 treated a war in disregard of the Covenant as an act of war against all members and provided for sanctions.
12. ⚠️ The mandate formulation: simultaneously the last instrument of imperial expansion and the first written admission that imperial rule was conditional and temporary.
13. ⚠️ The League's characteristic defect appears first in the mandates commission: judgement without enforcement.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain what the mandate system actually was, and state the formulation you would use to answer a question asking whether it was a form of colonialism.

**Model answer:** The mandate system was the mechanism by which German colonies and the Ottoman Arab provinces were transferred to victor states under League of Nations supervision. The Covenant's mandate article created three classes: Class A, former Ottoman territories judged closest to independence and requiring administrative advice and assistance, which is how Syria became a French mandate and Palestine, Iraq and Transjordan British mandates; Class B, African territories where the mandatory administered and bore welfare obligations, which covered Tanganyika, taken by Britain, and Togoland and the Cameroons, divided between Britain and France; and Class C, sparsely populated or remote territories administered as integral portions of the mandatory's own territory, which covered German South West Africa, taken by South Africa. Supervision was exercised by a standing mandates commission operating from 1921, which received and scrutinised annual reports and advised the League Council but had no enforcement power. The formulation to use is that the system was simultaneously the last instrument of imperial expansion and the first written admission that imperial rule was conditional and temporary. Both halves are evidenced. On the first, the account states that this was really a device by which the Allies seized the colonies without actually admitting that they were being annexed; Class C mandates were administered as integral parts of the mandatory's territory; no population was consulted; and Wilson's own Point 5, promising an impartial adjustment of colonial claims in the interests of the populations concerned, was plainly not satisfied. On the second, the mandatories were required to prepare the territories for independence, to report annually and to submit to international scrutiny - obligations that ordinary colonial rule had never accepted, and whose language later independence movements used against the mandatories themselves.

**Misconception to avoid:** Choosing one half of the formulation because it sounds more decisive. A question asking whether mandates were colonialism is asking the candidate to notice that an instrument can be acquisitive in motive and still create obligations its authors did not intend. Resolving the ambiguity throws away the mark that the question exists to award.

*The mandate ambiguity is one instance of a larger contradiction. The next lesson states the contradiction in full, because it is the single most examinable idea in the aftermath half of this topic.*

## Lesson 21 - Self-determination: what it delivered, where it stopped, and why

Progress: 21/25 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: what the principle actually promised in the Fourteen Points; where it was genuinely applied; the four categories of exception; the rule that predicts the exceptions; the colonial asymmetry; the minorities system as an admission of failure; whether the principle was even coherent; how to write a balanced verdict

*The chart is the lesson. ⚠️ Learn the four exception categories by name - viability, security, prior bargains, race and empire - and the rule beneath them. ⚠️ With those five items an answer can handle any question on self-determination in this period without hesitation.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

⚠️ Self-determination is usually presented as a noble idea imperfectly applied. ⚠️ That framing is comfortable and analytically weak, because "imperfectly" implies randomness, and the exceptions were not random. ⚠️ They fall into four categories, they follow a rule, and the rule is statable in one sentence. ⚠️ An answer that finds the rule is doing history; an answer that lists hypocrisies is doing commentary.

### 2. What the principle actually promised

⚠️ Precision about the wording is the first requirement, and Lesson 15 supplied it. ✅ Point 10 promised *self-government* for the peoples of Austria-Hungary. ✅ Point 12 promised *self-government* for the non-Turkish peoples of the Turkish Empire. ✅ Point 9 promised a readjustment of Italian frontiers along the lines of *nationality*. ✅ Point 13 promised an *independent* Poland with secure access to the sea. ✅ Point 5 promised an impartial adjustment of *colonial claims* in the interests of the populations concerned.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice that the strongest words - independence, nationality - attach to European cases, and the weakest - self-government, adjustment of claims - attach to the Habsburg and Ottoman peoples and to colonies. ⚠️ The gradation was in the text before any negotiator arrived in Paris. ⚠️ Half of the accusation of hypocrisy is therefore misdirected: much of what happened in 1919 was consistent with what had been promised in January 1918, and what is really at issue is how the promise was worded.

### 3. Where it was genuinely applied

⚠️ A balanced answer must concede this properly, because the concession is what makes the criticism credible.

✅ **Fact:** the central European settlements on the whole kept to the spirit of self-determination, and more people were placed under governments of their own nationality than ever before in Europe - though they were not always as democratic as Wilson would have liked, especially in Hungary and Poland.

✅ **Fact:** the settlement used plebiscites where it chose to: ✅ North Schleswig went to Denmark after a plebiscite, and ✅ Upper Silesia, originally to be given to Poland, was voted on after German written objections, with the result that Germany kept about two-thirds of the area.

✅ **Fact:** Estonia, Latvia and Lithuania, handed to Germany by Russia at Brest-Litovsk, were taken away and set up as independent states - described by the account as an example of self-determination being carried into practice.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three concessions, each of a different kind - a general finding about central Europe, a procedural instrument actually used, and a specific case of new statehood. ⚠️ An answer that makes all three before turning to the exceptions cannot be accused of one-sidedness, and it earns the right to a sharp criticism afterwards.

### 4. The four categories of exception

**Exception one: economic viability.** ✅ Almost three million Germans were left in the Sudetenland under the new state of Czechoslovakia and around a million under Polish rule; ✅ the Allies justified this on the grounds that the new states needed them in order to be economically viable.

⚠️ This is the most honest of the four exceptions, because the justification was stated rather than concealed. ⚠️ It concedes that a nationality principle applied without regard to economics produces states that cannot survive - which is a real problem, as the collapse of the Austrian economy demonstrates: ✅ its population fell from twenty-two million to six and a half million and most of its industrial wealth was lost.

**Exception two: security and power.** ✅ Austria was a completely German state with a population of some seven million; all these Germans wanted to become part of Germany; and unification was specifically forbidden, probably because that would have made Germany larger and more powerful even than in 1914.

⚠️ This is the least defensible of the four, and it should be named as such. ⚠️ The principle was withheld not because it was impractical, nor because the populations objected, nor because economics forbade it - ✅ there was in fact an excellent economic case for the union - but because applying it would have strengthened the defeated state. ⚠️ That is a decision about power, made inside a settlement whose stated basis was principle.

**Exception three: prior bargains.** ✅ The secret Treaty of London of May 1915 promised Italy Trentino, the South Tyrol, Istria, Trieste, part of Dalmatia, Adalia, some Aegean islands and a protectorate over Albania; ✅ St Germain duly transferred the South Tyrol as far as the Brenner Pass, Trentino, Istria and Trieste to Italy.

⚠️ The South Tyrol was not Italian by nationality, and Point 9 had promised a readjustment of Italian frontiers *along the lines of nationality*. ⚠️ A bargain made in 1915 to buy a belligerent therefore overrode a principle announced in 1918 to justify the war - and, ironically, ✅ Italy still felt cheated because she had not received all the territory promised her in 1915.

**Exception four: race and empire.** ✅ German colonies and Ottoman Arab territories became mandates rather than independent states; ✅ Arabs in the mandates had been hoping for independence as a reward for their struggle against the Turks; ✅ the transfer was hardly the "impartial adjustment" of colonial claims in the interests of the populations concerned that Point 5 had promised. ✅ Self-determination was also being ignored in the case of Smyrna, transferred to Greece over Turkish objection.

⚠️ This is the largest exception by population and the one the syllabus clause on colonization makes directly examinable. ⚠️ It is also the exception with the longest consequences, since the mandates created administrative units that later became states.

### 5. The rule beneath the exceptions

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** stated as one sentence, the rule is that self-determination was applied where it weakened the defeated and withheld where it would have strengthened them or weakened the victors.

⚠️ Test it against each case:

| Case | ⚠️ Would applying the principle have strengthened or weakened whom? | ⚠️ Outcome |
|---|---|---|
| Successor states from Austria-Hungary | Weakened a defeated empire | ✅ Applied |
| Baltic states from former Russian and German territory | Weakened both a defeated state and an excluded revolutionary power | ✅ Applied |
| Poland's independence and access to the sea | Weakened a defeated state | ✅ Applied |
| German-Austrian union | Would have **strengthened** a defeated state | ✅ Withheld |
| Sudeten and Polish Germans | Would have weakened two new allied states | ✅ Withheld, on economic viability |
| South Tyrol | Would have weakened a victor bound by a prior treaty | ✅ Withheld |
| Smyrna | Would have weakened a victor's Greek client | ✅ Withheld, then reversed at Lausanne by force |
| German and Ottoman colonies | Would have weakened every victor | ✅ Withheld; mandates instead |

⚠️ The rule holds in all eight cases, which is unusually clean for a historical generalisation and is exactly why it is worth writing. ⚠️ Note also the one reversal: ✅ Smyrna was recovered at Lausanne in 1923 - not by the principle but by force, which confirms Lesson 19's conclusion that the settlement rested on power.

### 6. Was the principle even coherent?

⚠️ A strong answer adds a further layer, because the criticism of inconsistency implicitly assumes that consistent application was available. ⚠️ It was not, for four reasons.

1. **Populations were mixed, not sorted.** In central and eastern Europe nationality did not correspond to contiguous territory, so any frontier left someone on the wrong side. ✅ The settlement's own solution - the minorities treaties - is the admission.
2. **The principle has no rule for deciding the unit.** Does "a people" mean a language group, a religious community, a historic kingdom or the inhabitants of a defined territory? Different answers produce different frontiers, and the principle itself cannot choose between them.
3. **The principle conflicts with viability.** A state composed strictly of co-nationals may lack ports, coal, food or defensible frontiers - which is precisely the Austrian case.
4. **The principle conflicts with security.** A settlement that applies nationality fully must permit the defeated state to grow, which no victor coalition will accept.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** so the honest verdict has two parts, and both are needed. ⚠️ The settlement applied self-determination more extensively than any previous European settlement and further than the principle could coherently be applied; and it withheld it, where it did, according to a rule of power rather than a rule of principle. ⚠️ Those two statements are compatible, and an answer that makes both is doing what "critically examine" requires.

### 7. The minorities system as an admission

⚠️ Lesson 20 described the machinery; here is its analytical significance. ✅ The minorities treaties, imposed from 28 June 1919, required the new and enlarged states to guarantee civil, political and religious rights regardless of origin, language or religion, with complaint available to the League.

⚠️ Three inferences follow.

1. **The peacemakers knew the principle had failed to produce homogeneous states**, or they would not have needed the treaties.
2. **They chose protection over further partition**, which was a reasonable choice: redrawing frontiers until every minority disappeared was impossible, and the alternative of population transfer was worse.
3. **They imposed the obligation asymmetrically**, on the new and defeated states only, which made it a marker of subordinate status in the eyes of those on whom it was imposed and reduced its legitimacy.

⚠️ The minorities system is therefore the best single piece of evidence for a balanced verdict: it shows the peacemakers acting in good faith on a problem they had themselves created, with an instrument that was reasonable in design and compromised in application.

### 8. Is the rule too neat?

**The objection.** ⚠️ Is the "rule of power" formulation not too neat? Surely some exceptions were genuinely practical.

**The reply.** ⚠️ Several were, and the reply should concede it. ⚠️ The Sudeten and Polish German cases had a stated practical justification in economic viability, and the Austrian collapse shows that the worry was real. ⚠️ But the formulation survives the concession, because the practical justifications always ran in one direction. ⚠️ Viability was invoked to strengthen new allied states and never to permit a union that would have strengthened a defeated one, although ✅ the economic case for Anschluss was excellent. ⚠️ A practical criterion applied asymmetrically is a political criterion with a practical name.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Does the colonial exception not judge 1919 by the standards of a later age?

**The reply.** ⚠️ No, and the reason is that the standard was supplied by the peacemakers themselves. ✅ Point 5 promised an impartial adjustment of colonial claims in the interests of the populations concerned; ✅ Lloyd George's January 1918 statement promised self-determination for the German colonies; ✅ the Arabs of the mandated territories had been encouraged to expect independence. ⚠️ The criticism is internal: it measures the settlement against its authors' own published undertakings, not against a standard invented afterwards.

**The residual.** ⚠️ Whether a more consistent application of the principle would have produced a more stable Europe is a counterfactual that the evidence cannot settle; Lesson 24 sets out the limits of such reasoning.

### 9. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the syllabus clause names redrawal of national boundaries, colonization and decolonization, and this lesson is where all three meet. ✅ The demand map records that a boundaries question must not be answered with Germany's losses alone.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ To what extent was the Paris Peace Settlement shaped by the principle of self-determination? (Directive: to what extent. Ceiling: 250 words.)
> ⚠️ "Self-determination in 1919 was a principle for Europe and a slogan for the rest of the world." Critically examine. (Directive: critically examine. Ceiling: 250 words.)

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Always concede the applications first - more people under governments of their own nationality than ever before, the plebiscites, the Baltic states - because it is what makes the criticism land. ⚠️ Then give the four exception categories by name and the rule. ⚠️ Add the incoherence point if the directive is "critically examine", because it prevents the answer from being merely accusatory. ⚠️ Close with the minorities treaties as the peacemakers' own admission.

### Traps

- Treating the settlement as simply hypocritical. ✅ It placed more people under governments of their own nationality than ever before in Europe.
- Forgetting the plebiscites, which are the procedural evidence of good faith.
- Saying the Fourteen Points promised independence to Habsburg and Ottoman peoples. ✅ They promised self-government.
- Omitting the economic-viability justification for the Sudeten and Polish German cases. ✅ It was stated.
- Omitting the Anschluss prohibition, which is the strongest single item of evidence.
- Ignoring the incoherence of the principle itself, which is where the highest-level marks are.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The Points promised *self-government* for Habsburg and non-Turkish Ottoman peoples, *independence* for Poland, frontiers by *nationality* for Italy, and *impartial adjustment* of colonial claims - a gradation present in the text from January 1918.
2. ✅ Applied: more people under governments of their own nationality than ever before in Europe; plebiscites in North Schleswig and Upper Silesia; Estonia, Latvia and Lithuania set up as independent states.
3. ✅ Exception one, economic viability: almost three million Germans in the Sudetenland and around a million under Polish rule, justified because the new states needed them to be economically viable.
4. ✅ Exception two, security and power: union with a completely German Austria of some seven million specifically forbidden, probably because it would have made Germany larger and more powerful than in 1914 - although the economic case for the union was excellent.
5. ✅ Exception three, prior bargains: the Treaty of London of May 1915 gave Italy the South Tyrol, which was not Italian by nationality, against Point 9.
6. ✅ Exception four, race and empire: German and Ottoman Arab territories became mandates; the Arabs had hoped for independence; Point 5's impartial adjustment was not what happened; self-determination was ignored at Smyrna.
7. ⚠️ The rule: applied where it weakened the defeated; withheld where it would have strengthened them or weakened the victors. The rule holds in all eight test cases.
8. ⚠️ The principle's own incoherences: mixed populations; no rule for defining the unit; conflict with viability; conflict with security.
9. ✅ The minorities treaties, from 28 June 1919, are the peacemakers' own admission that homogeneous states had not been produced.
10. ⚠️ Smyrna was recovered at Lausanne in 1923 by force rather than by principle, confirming that the settlement rested on power.

### Concept check

**Question:** To what extent was the Paris settlement shaped by the principle of self-determination? Give a graded answer with a stated rule.

**Model answer:** To a substantial extent in form, and to a lesser and patterned extent in substance. The applications were real and should be conceded first. The central European treaties on the whole kept to the spirit of self-determination and placed more people under governments of their own nationality than ever before in Europe, though not always as democratically as Wilson would have liked, especially in Hungary and Poland. Plebiscites were actually used: North Schleswig went to Denmark after a vote, and Upper Silesia was voted on after German written objections, with Germany keeping about two-thirds. Estonia, Latvia and Lithuania, handed to Germany by Russia at Brest-Litovsk, were taken away and set up as independent states, which the account itself calls self-determination carried into practice. The exceptions fall into four categories. Economic viability left almost three million Germans in the Sudetenland and around a million under Polish rule, on the stated ground that the new states needed them to be viable. Security and power forbade union between Germany and a completely German Austria of some seven million, probably because it would have made Germany larger and more powerful than in 1914 - although the economic case for the union was excellent. Prior bargains gave Italy the South Tyrol under the secret Treaty of London of 1915, against Point 9's promise of frontiers along the lines of nationality. And race and empire converted German and Ottoman Arab territories into mandates rather than independent states, although Point 5 had promised an impartial adjustment of colonial claims in the interests of the populations concerned. The rule that predicts every exception is that self-determination was applied where it weakened the defeated and withheld where it would have strengthened them or weakened the victors. The peacemakers' own admission of the shortfall is the minorities system imposed from 28 June 1919.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding simply that the principle was hypocritically applied. That verdict misses two things a strong answer must include: that the settlement applied nationality more extensively than any previous European settlement, and that the principle cannot be applied consistently in mixed-population territory in any case. The examinable finding is the rule, not the hypocrisy.

*Frontiers were one half of the aftermath. The other half was money - and the money arrangements damaged the settlement faster than any frontier did.*

## Lesson 22 - The economic aftermath: reparations, war debts, and the shift of economic power

Progress: 22/25 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the reparations figure and its reduction; the default and the triangular debt structure; why reparations were politically as well as economically damaging; the successor-state economies and the tariff problem; German recovery and its comparative measure; the shift of world economic weight out of Europe; what survived of the war economy; the boundary with the interwar and world-economy owners

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's record of the reparations figure of £6600 million announced in 1921, Keynes's proposed £2000 million, German default, Allied reliance on German cash to pay their own war debts to the USA, the international tension when France tried to force payment, the eventual reduction to £2000 million under the Young Plan of 1929 and the statement that reparations had proved disastrous both economically and politically; the record of Austria's economic collapse and League loans, Hungary's lost corn land and the tariffs that hampered Danube trade; the statement that German industry switched back to peacetime production remarkably quickly and by 1921 was producing three times as much steel as France; the expansion of Japanese, Chinese and American trade at Europe's expense, with the American share of world trade rising from ten per cent in 1914 to over twenty per cent by 1919; and the 1920s American boom ending in the Wall Street Crash of October 1929
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage for this session is held in Lesson 7
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the triangular debt structure that made reparations unworkable

```text
                        THE UNITED STATES
                   war loans to Britain and France
                              ^
                              |
                     repayment of war debts
                              |
        +---------------------+---------------------+
        |                                           |
    BRITAIN                                      FRANCE
    and other Allies                             and other Allies
        ^                                           ^
        |                                           |
        +---------------- reparations --------------+
                              |
                          GERMANY
        liability created by Article 231; amount NOT fixed at
        Versailles; announced 1921 as £6600 million
                              |
   WHAT ACTUALLY HAPPENED
   * Germany protested that the sum was impossible to pay
   * Germany soon began to DEFAULT on its annual instalments
   * this caused resentment among the Allies, who were RELYING ON
     GERMAN CASH to help pay their own war debts to the USA
   * there was international tension when France tried to force the
     Germans to pay
   * eventually the Allies admitted their mistake and reduced the
     amount to £2000 million (YOUNG PLAN, 1929)
   * but not before reparations had proved DISASTROUS, both
     economically and politically
                              |
   THE POINT: Allied insistence was not merely vindictive. The Allies
   were themselves debtors, and German payment was the mechanism by
   which they expected to service their own obligations. Reparations
   were a link in a chain, and the chain had its far end in New York
```

*The triangle is the single most useful diagram in the aftermath material, because it converts what looks like a moral question - should Germany have been made to pay? - into a structural one. ⚠️ Every participant in the chain was under pressure from the next link, and the chain's weakest point was a defeated economy that had been told the sum was a punishment.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

⚠️ Reparations are usually discussed as a question of fairness. ⚠️ They are better discussed as a question of *capacity and circulation*: could Germany generate the required surplus, and could it transfer that surplus to creditors who were simultaneously restricting the trade by which it would have to be earned? ⚠️ An answer that raises that second question is operating at a level most scripts never reach.

✅ **Fact:** there could be little valid objection to the general principle of reparations, but many historians now agree that the actual amount decided on was far too high at £6600 million. ✅ Keynes, an economic adviser to the British delegation, urged the Allies to take £2000 million, which he said was a more reasonable amount that Germany would be able to afford.

### 2. The default and its consequences

✅ **Fact:** the figure of £6600 million enabled the Germans to protest that it was impossible to pay, and they soon began to default on their annual instalments.

✅ **Fact:** this caused resentment among the Allies, who were relying on German cash to help them pay their own war debts to the USA. ✅ There was international tension when France tried to force the Germans to pay. ✅ Eventually the Allies admitted their mistake and reduced the amount to £2000 million under the Young Plan of 1929 - but not before reparations had proved disastrous, both economically and politically.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four points, in ascending order of sophistication.

1. **The figure was a political instrument as much as an economic one.** An amount presented as the price of guilt could not be negotiated downwards without appearing to concede that the guilt was overstated, which is why it took a decade and two international plans to reach the figure Keynes had proposed in 1919.
2. **Default was predictable and was therefore built into the politics.** A sum that "enabled the Germans to protest that it was impossible to pay" gave German governments a permanent domestic argument and a permanent international grievance, at no cost to their credibility.
3. **The Allies were not free agents.** They were themselves debtors to the United States, and the war debts were a claim on their budgets regardless of whether Germany paid. ⚠️ This is why insistence continued long after it had become counter-productive.
4. **The phrase "disastrous, both economically and politically" should be split in an answer.** Economically, the attempt to extract and transfer a very large surplus from a defeated economy destabilised currencies, budgets and trade. Politically, reparations kept the settlement's central grievance alive annually, in the form of a payment.

⚠️ **Boundary.** ⚠️ The Ruhr occupation, the German inflation of the early 1920s, the Dawes Plan and the reparations diplomacy of the 1920s belong to the interwar international-relations owner. ⚠️ This session records the liability, the figure, the default, the triangular structure, the international tension and the eventual reduction, and hands over. ⚠️ The one item it keeps is the Young Plan reduction, because it is the evidence that adjudicates the German objection in Lesson 18.

### 3. The successor-state economies

✅ **Fact:** the treaties left both Austria and Hungary with serious economic problems. ✅ Austria's population fell from twenty-two million to six and a half million and most of its industrial wealth was lost to Czechoslovakia and Poland; ✅ Vienna was left surrounded by farming land which could hardly support it; ✅ Austria was soon facing a severe economic crisis and was constantly having to be helped out by loans from the League of Nations. ✅ Hungary's population fell from twenty-one million to seven and a half million, with some of her richest corn land lost to Romania.

✅ **Fact:** matters were further complicated when all the new states quickly introduced tariffs, which hampered the flow of trade through the whole Danube area and made the industrial recovery of Austria particularly difficult.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Danube basin is the clearest demonstration available of an economic cost created directly by a political settlement. ⚠️ An integrated imperial market with a single currency, a single tariff area and a complementary division between industrial and agricultural regions was replaced by several small states that immediately taxed one another's goods. ⚠️ The political gain - national statehood - was real; the economic cost was also real and was not compensated. ⚠️ That is a genuine two-sided finding, and it is far better than the usual observation that the successor states were "weak".

### 4. Germany's recovery, measured

✅ **Fact:** Germany was only temporarily weakened, and it is possible to argue that Germany was weakened less than her enemies. ✅ Much of France, Poland and the Balkans had been ravaged by occupying troops, whereas German territory was virtually untouched. ✅ German industry was able to switch back to peacetime production remarkably quickly, and by 1921 was producing three times as much steel as France.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the steel comparison is the most valuable single statistic in the aftermath material, and it should be used deliberately rather than quoted in passing. ⚠️ Two years after the armistice, the defeated and disarmed state was out-producing the principal victor in the basic input of heavy industry and armaments, by a factor of three. ⚠️ That single fact carries three conclusions: that the treaty's economic clauses did not disable Germany; that French insecurity after the lapse of the Anglo-American guarantee was rational rather than neurotic; and that the settlement's central defect was therefore the mismatch between the grievance it created and the power it left intact.

### 5. The shift of world economic weight

✅ **Fact:** some countries outside Europe - particularly Japan, China and the USA - took advantage of Europe's preoccupation with the war to expand their trade at Europe's expense. ✅ The USA's share of world trade grew from ten per cent in 1914 to over twenty per cent by 1919. ✅ Since they were unable to obtain European imports during the war, Japan and China began their own programmes of industrialisation.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the war's largest and most permanent economic consequence, and the mechanism deserves to be stated rather than merely asserted. ⚠️ For four years the world's dominant manufacturing region withdrew most of its output from world markets, diverted its capital to munitions, liquidated overseas investments to pay for imports and lost merchant shipping to submarines. ⚠️ Producers elsewhere filled the gap, and once new industries existed they did not disappear when European exports resumed. ⚠️ Import substitution in Asia and export expansion in North America were therefore not accidents of the war but its predictable industrial consequence.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this also completes the argument of Lesson 14. ✅ The war caused a decline in Europe's prestige and marked the beginning of the end of European domination of the rest of the world; the trade figures are the material half of the same finding. ⚠️ Prestige and market share moved together, which is why an answer can treat the political and economic consequences as a single structural shift.

✅ **Fact:** during the 1920s the Americans enjoyed a great economic boom and their future prosperity seemed assured; within a few years it became clear that they had made the mistakes of over-confidence and over-expansion, and the Wall Street Crash of October 1929 heralded a severe economic crisis that spread throughout the world.

⚠️ **Boundary.** ⚠️ The Depression itself belongs to the interwar and world-economy owners. ⚠️ Its relevance here is limited to one point that Lesson 24 requires: the same year that the Allies finally reduced reparations to the figure Keynes had proposed, the economy on which the whole debt chain ultimately rested entered its worst crisis.

### 6. What survived of the war economy

⚠️ Lesson 9 described the total-war state; this section records what happened to it.

| Wartime instrument | ⚠️ Fate after 1918 |
|---|---|
| Direct state control of mines, factories and railways | ⚠️ Largely relaxed in the liberal economies, though the precedent and the administrative knowledge remained |
| Government control of shipping and agriculture | ⚠️ Mostly wound down with demobilisation |
| Allocation of labour between army and industry | ⚠️ Abandoned with conscription in several states, retained where conscription continued |
| Rationing and price control | ⚠️ Removed as supplies recovered, retained longest where blockade effects persisted |
| Large public debt and inflationary finance | ⚠️ **Permanent.** Every belligerent emerged with obligations that constrained policy for a decade |
| The international debt network | ⚠️ **Permanent and new.** Inter-Allied war debts to the United States created a structure of official international obligation that had not previously existed at that scale |
| The demonstrated capability of the state | ⚠️ **Permanent.** The knowledge that a modern state could direct production, ration food and allocate labour did not lapse with the controls |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two permanent items are the debt network and the demonstrated capability, and they point in opposite political directions. ⚠️ The debt network constrained governments and pushed them towards orthodox retrenchment; the demonstrated capability showed that governments could do far more than orthodoxy allowed. ⚠️ The tension between those two legacies is one of the defining features of interwar economic politics - which is where this session hands over.

### 7. Two defences of reparations, examined

**The first defence.** ⚠️ Were reparations really so damaging, given that Germany defaulted and the sum was eventually reduced?

**The reply.** ⚠️ The damage was less in the amount transferred than in the *system* it created, and the account's verdict is explicit that reparations proved disastrous both economically and politically. ⚠️ Three mechanisms. ⚠️ First, an open-ended then enormous liability discouraged investment and destabilised public finance. ⚠️ Second, default produced enforcement attempts and international tension, since ✅ France tried to force payment. ⚠️ Third, and most importantly, it kept the settlement's central grievance politically live on an annual cycle, giving every German government a standing quarrel with the victors and every opponent of the republic a permanent grievance to exploit.

**The second defence.** ⚠️ Is the criticism of reparations not made with hindsight, given that reparations were a normal feature of European peace settlements?

**The reply.** ⚠️ It is not, and the evidence is contemporaneous. ✅ Keynes, a senior British delegate and economic adviser, urged £2000 million at the time and resigned in protest; ✅ Harold Nicolson wrote that if he were a German he should not sign for a moment. ⚠️ Moreover, the criticism is not of the principle - ✅ there could be little valid objection to the general principle - but of the quantum, and the quantum was conceded in 1929 when the Allies reduced the sum to exactly the figure Keynes had named.

**The residual.** ⚠️ The amounts actually transferred under reparations, the schedule of payments and the comparative burden of inter-Allied debts are not held in this session's evidence base and are not quantified here.

### 8. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the demand map warns against giving any reparations figure other than ✅ £6600 million, later reduced to ✅ £2000 million under the Young Plan.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The economic settlement of 1919 was more damaging than the territorial one." Examine. (Directive: examine. Ceiling: 250 words.)
> ⚠️ Discuss the economic consequences of the First World War for Europe and the world. (Directive: discuss. Ceiling: 250 words.)

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Use the triangle to explain why Allied insistence continued past the point of utility; it converts a moral narrative into a structural one in three clauses. ⚠️ Use exactly three figures: £6600 million, £2000 million, and the American trade share moving from ten to over twenty per cent. ⚠️ Use the German steel comparison as the closing evidence, because it establishes the settlement's central mismatch in a single sentence.

### Traps

- Giving any reparations figure other than the two recorded ones.
- Saying reparations were fixed at Versailles. ✅ The amount was announced in 1921.
- Treating Allied insistence as pure vindictiveness. ✅ They were relying on German cash to pay their own war debts to the USA.
- Importing the Ruhr occupation, the inflation of the early 1920s or the Dawes Plan. They belong to the interwar owner.
- Omitting the successor-state economies and the Danube tariff problem, which is a distinctive and under-used argument.
- Omitting the shift of world economic weight, which is the largest permanent consequence.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Little valid objection lay against the principle of reparations; the amount of £6600 million, announced in 1921, is widely judged far too high.
2. ✅ Keynes, economic adviser to the British delegation, urged £2000 million as an amount Germany could afford.
3. ✅ Germany protested that the sum was impossible to pay and soon defaulted on its annual instalments.
4. ✅ The Allies resented this because they were relying on German cash to help pay their own war debts to the USA; there was international tension when France tried to force payment.
5. ✅ The Allies eventually reduced the amount to £2000 million under the Young Plan of 1929, after reparations had proved disastrous both economically and politically.
6. ⚠️ The triangular structure: German reparations to the Allies; Allied war debts to the United States; the chain's far end in New York.
7. ✅ Austria: population from twenty-two million to six and a half million; industrial wealth lost; severe economic crisis; repeated League loans. Hungary: population from twenty-one million to seven and a half million; richest corn land lost to Romania.
8. ✅ New-state tariffs hampered trade through the whole Danube area and made Austrian industrial recovery particularly difficult.
9. ✅ German territory was virtually untouched; German industry switched back to peacetime production remarkably quickly and by 1921 was producing three times as much steel as France.
10. ✅ Japan, China and the USA expanded trade at Europe's expense; the American share of world trade rose from ten per cent in 1914 to over twenty per cent by 1919; Japan and China began their own industrialisation programmes.
11. ✅ The 1920s American boom ended in the Wall Street Crash of October 1929 - noted and handed over.
12. ⚠️ Two permanent legacies of the war economy: an international network of official debt, and the demonstrated capability of the state to direct an economy.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why reparations proved disastrous both economically and politically, and show why Allied insistence on them cannot be explained by vindictiveness alone.

**Model answer:** Economically, the liability created under Article 231 was left unquantified at Versailles and then announced in 1921 at £6600 million, a sum that many historians agree was far too high and that Keynes, an economic adviser to the British delegation, had said at the time should be £2000 million if Germany were to be able to afford it. The figure enabled the Germans to protest that payment was impossible, and they soon began to default on their annual instalments, which destabilised public finance and international credit and provoked enforcement attempts.
