Politically, it converted the settlement's central grievance into an annual transaction, so that every German government had a standing quarrel with the victors and every opponent of the republic had a permanent grievance to exploit; the account's verdict is that reparations proved disastrous both economically and politically, and the Allies eventually admitted their mistake by reducing the sum to £2000 million under the Young Plan of 1929 - exactly the figure Keynes had proposed a decade earlier. Allied insistence cannot be explained by vindictiveness alone because the Allies were themselves debtors. They were relying on German cash to help pay their own war debts to the United States, so reparations were a link in a chain whose far end was in New York, and a failure of German payment threatened Allied budgets directly. That is why there was international tension when France tried to force the Germans to pay: it was an attempt to repair a broken circuit, not merely an act of punishment. The structural reading also explains the timing of the concession, since the reduction came only when the political and economic costs of insistence had become undeniable.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating reparations as a purely bilateral question between France and Germany. The obligation sat inside a triangular structure of official international debt that had no real precedent at that scale, and every party in it was under pressure from the next. An answer that draws the triangle explains both the insistence and the eventual retreat.

*Money and frontiers were the settlement's instruments. Its subjects were four empires that ceased to exist and two that grew - and the comparison between them is the single most examinable structural question in the aftermath.*

## Lesson 23 - Four empires destroyed, two empires enlarged: what replaced what

Progress: 23/25 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the four-variable explanation of imperial survival and collapse; Germany's transformation and the Weimar inheritance; Russia's transformation and its exclusion; the Habsburg dissolution and the successor-state problem; the Ottoman collapse and the Turkish republic; why the British and French empires grew; the one-sentence structural answer; the colonial and Asian consequences and the India handover

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's record that the empires which had dominated central and eastern Europe for over two hundred years disappeared almost overnight; the German revolution, the abdication of Wilhelm II, the declaration of a republic and the Weimar Republic's severe economic, political and social problems until 1933; the complete collapse of the Habsburg Empire with Karl I's abdication and the separation of Austria and Hungary; the two Russian revolutions of 1917; Italy's postwar debt and instability; the relative gains of Japan, China and the USA; the transfer of German colonies as mandates and Jeremy Black's final-stage-of-partition argument; the Turkish national revival under Mustafa Kemal and the revision at Lausanne; and the advanced material's instruction to use one bridge line on empire-wide change and to cross-link Indian detail rather than retell it
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage for this session is held in Lesson 7
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four variables that decide whether an empire survives a lost war

| Variable | **Habsburg** | **Ottoman** | **Russian** | **German** | **British** | **French** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Defeated?** | ✅ Yes; dismembered by St Germain and Trianon | ✅ Yes; Sevres imposed, then rejected | ✅ Yes; ✅ withdrew December 1917, Brest-Litovsk March 1918 | ✅ Yes; ✅ armistice 11 November 1918 | ✅ No - **victorious** | ✅ No - **victorious** |
| **Was empire the same thing as the state?** | ⚠️ Yes - a multinational dynastic state whose subject nationalities had organised national movements | ⚠️ Yes - a multinational empire with Turkish and Arab national movements emerging | ⚠️ Largely yes - a contiguous multinational empire | ⚠️ No - a nation-state with a small overseas empire | ⚠️ No - a nation-state with an overseas empire | ⚠️ No - a nation-state with an overseas empire |
| **Was self-determination applied to it?** | ✅ Yes - successor states created | ✅ Selectively - ✅ Arab territories became **mandates**, not independent states | ⚠️ Partly and against its will - ✅ Estonia, Latvia and Lithuania set up as independent states | ⚠️ Applied to its losses, withheld from its gains - ✅ union with Austria forbidden | ❌ No - ✅ it **received** German colonies as mandates | ❌ No - ✅ it **received** mandates |
| **Capacity to resist the settlement?** | ⚠️ None - the state had already disintegrated | ✅ **Real** - ✅ Mustafa Kemal rejected Sevres and forced Lausanne in 1923 | ⚠️ Outside the settlement entirely - ✅ ignored, because the powers would not negotiate with the Bolsheviks | ⚠️ None immediately - disarmed, Rhineland occupied; ⚠️ economically intact | ✅ Intact | ✅ Intact |
| **Outcome** | ✅ Replaced by small, mutually hostile, economically damaged successor states | ✅ Empire dissolved; a Turkish national state survived by force; Arab lands became mandates | ⚠️ Replaced by a revolutionary state outside the international system | ✅ Republic declared 9 November 1918; territorially reduced, economically intact, politically embittered | ⚠️ **Territorially enlarged in 1919** | ⚠️ **Territorially enlarged in 1919** |

*The decisive row is the second. ⚠️ Where the empire *was* the state, national self-determination destroyed the state; where empire was an overseas possession of a nation-state, defeat abroad did not dissolve the centre - and victory abroad enlarged it. ⚠️ That single distinction answers the whole question, and it is the sentence a strong answer builds around.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

✅ **Fact:** the empires which had dominated central and eastern Europe for over two hundred years disappeared almost overnight.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the puzzle is that they disappeared while the British and French empires, which fought the same war and lost comparable proportions of their manpower, emerged larger. ⚠️ Since both sets of states were empires and both were exhausted, the explanation cannot be "the war destroyed empires". ⚠️ It has to identify what distinguished the two groups, and the four-variable table does exactly that.

⚠️ Two of the four variables do no discriminating work on their own. ⚠️ Defeat is necessary but not sufficient: ✅ Germany was defeated and territorially reduced without ceasing to exist, and ✅ the Ottoman state was defeated and produced a surviving national republic. ⚠️ Capacity to resist explains the *terms* a state obtained, not whether it survived: ✅ Turkey's capacity produced Lausanne but did not preserve the empire. ⚠️ The discriminating variable is the second - whether empire and state were the same thing - with the third, the selective application of self-determination, determining which victors gained.

### 2. Germany transformed

✅ **Fact:** in Germany, hardship and defeat caused a revolution: Kaiser Wilhelm II was compelled to abdicate and a republic was declared. ✅ Over the next few years the Weimar Republic experienced severe economic, political and social problems, and in 1933 it was brought to an end when Hitler became German Chancellor.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the German transformation was **regime change without state dissolution**, and the distinction matters. The territory was reduced but coherent; the population was largely homogeneous; the industrial base was intact and recovering, ✅ producing three times as much steel as France by 1921. ⚠️ What changed was the form of government, and the new form inherited every liability of the old one: the armistice, the treaty, the reparations bill, the war-guilt clause, and a myth that it had stabbed the army in the back.

⚠️ **Boundary.** ⚠️ The Weimar Republic's history and the rise of Nazism belong to the fascism owner. ⚠️ This session establishes the inheritance in one sentence and hands over.

### 3. Russia transformed and excluded

✅ **Fact:** in Russia the pressures of war caused two revolutions in 1917: the first overthrew the tsar, and the second brought Lenin and the Bolsheviks to power. ✅ Russia withdrew in December 1917 and was taken out of the war by Brest-Litovsk. ✅ At the peace conference Russia was ignored, because the powers did not want to negotiate with its Bolshevik government. ✅ The Baltic territories that Brest-Litovsk had handed to Germany were taken away and set up as independent states.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Russia is the unique case in the table because it was defeated, transformed *and* excluded. ⚠️ The structural consequence for the settlement was severe: the largest state in Europe was outside the system that governed eastern Europe, while the second-largest, Germany, was inside it under protest. ⚠️ Every new state between them - Poland, the Baltic states, Czechoslovakia - existed in a space defined by two great powers with a shared interest in revision and no stake in the settlement. ⚠️ Lesson 24 uses this; ✅ the account records the same structural fact from the other direction when it notes that the successor states were small, mutually hostile and militarily weak.

⚠️ **Boundary.** ⚠️ The revolutions themselves and the Soviet state belong to the Russian Revolution owner.

### 4. The Habsburg dissolution and the successor-state problem

✅ **Fact:** the Habsburg Empire collapsed completely; the last emperor, Karl I, was forced to abdicate in November 1918; the various nationalities declared themselves independent; and Austria and Hungary split into two separate states.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the successor states inherited four problems simultaneously, and naming them is more useful than naming the states.

1. **Frontiers containing minorities.** ✅ Three million Germans in the Sudetenland and a million under Polish rule, retained on grounds of economic viability.
2. **Economies severed from their markets.** ✅ Austria reduced to six and a half million with most industrial wealth lost; ✅ new tariffs hampering trade through the whole Danube area.
3. **Mutual hostility.** ⚠️ Nearly every frontier in the region was disputed by at least one neighbour, and ✅ Hungary in particular had lost Slovakia, Ruthenia, Croatia, Slovenia, Transylvania and the Banat.
4. **Military weakness in a gap between two great powers.** ⚠️ Small armies in a space bounded by Germany and Soviet Russia.

⚠️ Together these produce the standard formulation: the settlement replaced one large multinational state in central Europe with a belt of small, mutually hostile and militarily weak states, which was a power vacuum rather than a balance.

### 5. The Ottoman collapse and the Turkish exception

✅ **Fact:** the Ottoman state was defeated, signed the armistice of Mudros on 30 October 1918, and had Sevres imposed on it in 1920, transferring Eastern Thrace, Aegean islands and Smyrna to Greece, Adalia and Rhodes to Italy and the Arab provinces to Britain and France as mandates. ✅ Led by Mustafa Kemal, the Turks rejected the treaty, drove the Greeks out of Smyrna and obtained the revision of 1923 at Lausanne, recovering Eastern Thrace including Constantinople, and Smyrna. ✅ Turkey was the first state to challenge the Paris settlement successfully.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Ottoman case splits in two, and saying so is the mark of a well-prepared answer. ⚠️ The *empire* died: its Arab provinces became mandates and never returned. ⚠️ The *national state* survived, by force, and rewrote its treaty. ⚠️ So the Ottoman collapse confirms both propositions of this lesson at once - that multinational empires did not survive national self-determination, and that capacity to resist determined the terms on which the successor state emerged.

### 6. The empires that grew

✅ **Fact:** German colonies became mandates: ✅ Britain acquired Tanganyika, ✅ Britain and France divided Togoland and the Cameroons, and ✅ South Africa gained German South West Africa. ✅ Under Sevres, Syria became a French mandate and Palestine, Iraq and Transjordan British mandates. ✅ Jeremy Black's argument is that the war led to the final stage of the partition of Africa.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the conclusion is uncomfortable and precise. ⚠️ The First World War destroyed the empires in which nationality and sovereignty were the same question and enlarged the empires in which they were not. ⚠️ The victors' own imperial reckoning was not abolished by 1919; it was deferred - and the deferral lasted until after 1945, which is where the decolonisation owner takes over.

⚠️ Note also the relative gains outside Europe: ✅ Japan, China and the USA expanded trade at Europe's expense, ✅ Japan and China began their own industrialisation programmes, and ✅ Japan ended the war as a principal Allied power with mandated territory. ⚠️ The redistribution of 1919 was therefore not only from the defeated to the victors but from Europe to the world.

### 7. The colonial and Asian consequences, and the India handover

⚠️ Three consequences can be stated on this session's evidence and should be kept distinct.

1. **Territorial:** ✅ the German and Ottoman colonial and imperial territories were redistributed as mandates, completing the partition of Africa and creating the administrative units of the modern Middle East.
2. **Political:** ⚠️ populations mobilised for a war justified in the language of self-government were governed afterwards under mandates and unreformed colonial administrations, and ✅ the Arabs of the mandated territories had expected independence as a reward for their struggle. ⚠️ The gap between wartime language and post-war provision became a permanent argument available to anti-colonial movements.
3. **Economic:** ✅ Japan and China industrialised during the European withdrawal from world markets, which began the long shift of manufacturing capacity towards Asia.

⚠️ **The India boundary, stated once.** ⚠️ Indian soldiers and resources supported the imperial war effort, and wartime mobilisation followed by post-war disappointment sharpened Indian demands for political reform. ⚠️ Everything beyond that sentence - the constitutional reforms, the repressive legislation, Jallianwala Bagh, Khilafat and the beginning of the Gandhian mass phase - belongs to the Modern Indian History owners and is not taught here.

### 8. Comparison: two empires that fought the same war, one destroyed and one enlarged

| Axis | **Austria-Hungary** | **Britain** |
|---|---|---|
| Relationship of empire to state | ⚠️ Identical: the empire *was* the state | ⚠️ Separable: a nation-state possessing an overseas empire |
| Effect of national movements | ⚠️ Fatal - ✅ nationalities declared independence and the state dissolved | ⚠️ Serious but not fatal in 1919 - the metropole was unaffected by colonial nationalism at this date |
| Effect of defeat or victory | ✅ Defeated and dismembered | ✅ Victorious and enlarged |
| Application of self-determination | ✅ Applied fully | ✅ Not applied at all; ✅ it received mandates |
| Outcome by 1923 | ✅ Two small republics and a belt of successor states | ⚠️ The largest territorial extent in its history |
| Long-run consequence | ⚠️ A permanent revisionist grievance in central Europe | ⚠️ An imperial reckoning postponed to after 1945 |

⚠️ **The one-sentence structural answer:** the First World War destroyed the empires in which nationality and sovereignty were the same question and enlarged the empires in which they were not, which is why 1919 dissolved the Habsburg and Ottoman states and handed their territories, as mandates, to victors whose own imperial reckoning was deferred.

### 9. Is the explanation circular?

**The objection.** ⚠️ Is the "empire equals state" variable not simply a restatement of the outcome? Multinational empires collapsed because they were multinational, which explains nothing.

**The reply.** ⚠️ It would be circular if multinationality alone were the claim. ⚠️ It is not: the claim is about the *interaction* of three things - a multinational structure, organised national movements inside it, and a victorious coalition that had announced national self-determination as a principle of settlement. ⚠️ Remove any one and the outcome changes. ⚠️ The Russian empire was multinational and did not dissolve into successor states across its whole extent, because the third element did not apply to it - it was outside the settlement. ⚠️ The British empire was multinational in its possessions but not in its metropole, so the principle was simply not applied to it. ⚠️ The explanation is therefore relational, not tautological.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Did the war not in fact weaken the British and French empires, whatever the map showed in 1919?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Yes, in ways the map conceals, and an answer should say so. ⚠️ Both emerged as debtors; both had mobilised colonial populations whose political claims were thereby strengthened; both had demonstrated to those populations that European power was divisible and defeatable; and both acquired new territories under a legal form that conceded the principle of eventual independence. ⚠️ ✅ The account's own judgement that the war marked the beginning of the end of European domination points the same way. ⚠️ The precise statement is that the maritime empires were territorially enlarged and structurally weakened in 1919, and that the contradiction was resolved after 1945.

**The residual.** ⚠️ How far colonial nationalism was accelerated by the war as opposed to by later developments is not settled by this session's evidence base and belongs to the decolonisation owner.

### 10. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the syllabus clause names redrawal of national boundaries, colonization and decolonization; this lesson is the one that connects all three to the imperial question.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The First World War destroyed some empires and enlarged others." Explain this paradox. (Directive: explain. Ceiling: 250 words.)
> ⚠️ Examine the consequences of the First World War for the imperial order. (Directive: examine. Ceiling: 250 words.)

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Build the answer on the four-variable table, and say explicitly which variable is doing the discriminating work. ⚠️ Give the Ottoman split - the empire died, the national state survived by force - because it demonstrates the argument twice in one case. ⚠️ Close with the one-sentence structural answer, which is the highest-value single sentence in the aftermath material. ⚠️ Hand over on India in one clause.

### Traps

- Saying the war destroyed empires. ✅ It destroyed four and enlarged two.
- Explaining the collapses by defeat alone. ✅ Germany was defeated and did not dissolve.
- Forgetting that Russia was transformed *and* excluded, which is what creates the eastern European vacuum.
- Treating the Ottoman case as a single outcome. The empire died and the national state survived.
- Omitting the mandates when discussing the imperial order. They are where the victors grew.
- Importing Weimar's history, Soviet history, fascism or Indian nationalism. Each has its own owner.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The empires which had dominated central and eastern Europe for over two hundred years disappeared almost overnight.
2. ⚠️ Four variables: defeat; whether empire and state were the same thing; whether self-determination was applied; and capacity to resist. The second discriminates.
3. ✅ Germany: revolution, abdication of Wilhelm II, a republic declared, the Weimar Republic with severe economic, political and social problems until Hitler became Chancellor in 1933 - regime change without state dissolution.
4. ✅ Russia: two revolutions in 1917; withdrawal in December 1917; Brest-Litovsk; ignored at the peace conference because the powers would not negotiate with the Bolsheviks; the Baltic states set up as independent.
5. ✅ Austria-Hungary: complete collapse; Karl I abdicated in November 1918; nationalities declared independence; Austria and Hungary split.
6. ⚠️ The successor states inherited four problems: minorities inside their frontiers; economies severed from their markets; mutual hostility; and military weakness in a gap between two great powers.
7. ✅ Ottoman: Mudros 30 October 1918; Sevres imposed 1920; rejected under Mustafa Kemal; Lausanne 1923 restored Eastern Thrace including Constantinople, and Smyrna; the first state to challenge the Paris settlement successfully.
8. ⚠️ The Ottoman case splits: the empire died, the national state survived by force.
9. ✅ The victors grew: Tanganyika to Britain; Togoland and the Cameroons divided between Britain and France; German South West Africa to South Africa; Syria a French mandate; Palestine, Iraq and Transjordan British mandates.
10. ✅ Jeremy Black: the war led to the final stage of the partition of Africa.
11. ⚠️ The one-sentence structural answer: the war destroyed the empires in which nationality and sovereignty were the same question and enlarged those in which they were not.
12. ⚠️ The maritime empires were territorially enlarged and structurally weakened in 1919; the contradiction was resolved after 1945.
13. ⚠️ India: one bridge sentence on mobilisation and post-war disappointment, then hand over.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Habsburg and Ottoman empires did not survive the war while the British and French empires emerged larger, and identify the variable that does the real explanatory work.

**Model answer:** Four variables are relevant and only one discriminates. Defeat is necessary but not sufficient: Germany was defeated and dismembered without ceasing to exist, and the Ottoman state was defeated and produced a surviving national republic. Capacity to resist explains the terms a state obtained rather than its survival, as the Turkish case shows - Mustafa Kemal rejected Sevres, drove the Greeks from Smyrna and obtained Lausanne in 1923, recovering Eastern Thrace including Constantinople and Smyrna, but did not thereby preserve the empire, whose Arab provinces became French and British mandates. The application of self-determination determines which victors gained, since it was applied fully to Austria-Hungary, selectively to the Ottoman lands, and not at all to Britain and France, which received German colonies as mandates. The variable that does the real work is whether empire and state were the same thing. In the Habsburg and Ottoman cases the empire was the state: they were multinational dynastic structures whose subject nationalities had organised national movements, so when the victors adopted national self-determination as a principle of settlement, applying it dissolved the state itself - and in the Habsburg case the nationalities declared independence before the peace conference even met, so that its task was to formalise and recognise what had taken place. In the British and French cases empire was an overseas possession of a nation-state, so defeat or exhaustion abroad did not dissolve the metropole, and victory abroad enlarged the possession: Britain acquired Tanganyika and parts of Togoland and the Cameroons, France most of Togoland and the Cameroons and the Syrian mandate, and South Africa German South West Africa. The structural answer is that the war destroyed the empires in which nationality and sovereignty were the same question and enlarged those in which they were not.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the war was a defeat for empire as such. In 1919 it was the opposite for the victors: the maritime empires reached their largest extent, under a legal form that nonetheless conceded for the first time that imperial rule was conditional and temporary. They were territorially enlarged and structurally weakened at the same time, and the contradiction was resolved only after 1945.

*Everything is now in place for the argument that examiners actually set: whether this settlement was doomed, and whether a line can honestly be drawn from 1919 to what came after.*

## Lesson 24 - Was the settlement doomed? Responsibility, continuity, historiography and counterfactuals

Progress: 24/25 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the recorded verdict and its six weaknesses; the revisionist-versus-status-quo division; the harsh verdict and the softer verdict set side by side; the contemporary judgements; the Bell thesis on the Depression; what a continuity claim from 1919 to 1939 can and cannot support; the rules for counterfactual reasoning; the verdict to write

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's verdict section on the peace settlement, including the division of Europe into revisionist and status-quo states, the lukewarm support of the latter, the American failure to ratify, French disenchantment at the lapse of the Anglo-American guarantee, Italy's sense of being cheated, Russia's exclusion, Germany's temporary weakening and rapid industrial recovery, and the statement of the great failing; Harold Nicolson's and Gilbert White's remarks and Keynes's resignation; the judgement that many historians now see the settlement as a workable compromise and that the peacemakers cannot be blamed for Hitler's rise or for the Second World War; and P. M. H. Bell's argument in Origins of the Second World War in Europe (2007) that European recovery was cut off in its prime by the great depression and the advent of Hitler
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage for this session is held in Lesson 7
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the two verdicts, placed side by side

| | **THE HARD VERDICT** | **THE SOFTER VERDICT** |
|---|---|---|
| Core claim | ⚠️ The settlement created grievance without lasting enforcement | ⚠️ It may have been the best compromise available in 1919 |
| ✅ Evidence | ✅ It divided Europe into states that wanted to revise it and states that wanted to preserve it, and the latter were only lukewarm in their support | ✅ Given the intricacy of the problems, an American delegate remarked that "it is not surprising that they made a bad peace: what is surprising is that they managed to make peace at all" |
| ✅ Evidence | ✅ The USA failed to ratify the settlement and never joined the League | ✅ Many historians now see the settlement "as a workable compromise", and perhaps the best achievable in difficult circumstances |
| ✅ Evidence | ✅ France was left disenchanted because the Anglo-American guarantee of her frontiers could not apply | ✅ The peacemakers cannot be blamed for Hitler's rise to power, and certainly not for the Second World War |
| ✅ Evidence | ✅ Italy felt cheated; ✅ Russia was ignored because the powers would not negotiate with the Bolsheviks | ✅ P. M. H. Bell argues that in the early 1920s Europe, including Germany, was beginning to recover well, and that "the outline of a successful European recovery was cut off in its prime by the great depression and its dreadful consequence, the advent of Hitler" |
| ✅ Evidence | ✅ Germany was only temporarily weakened and soon strong enough to challenge certain terms; ✅ arguably weakened less than her enemies, since German territory was virtually untouched while France, Poland and the Balkans had been ravaged; ✅ by 1921 German industry was producing three times as much steel as France | ⚠️ Central Europe had already been settled on the ground before the conference met, so the peacemakers' freedom of action was far smaller than the criticism assumes |
| ✅ The recorded great failing | ✅ "The great failing of the peace settlement was that it left the Germans with a sense of resentment and grievance, but did not leave them too weak to retaliate and seek revenge" | ⚠️ - |
| ✅ Contemporary voices | ✅ Harold Nicolson: "If I were the Germans, I shouldn't sign for a moment"; ✅ Keynes resigned in protest and came home | ✅ Gilbert White, as above |

*The two columns are not a balance to be split down the middle. ⚠️ They are two questions: *was the settlement defective?* and *were the peacemakers culpable?* - and the recorded evidence answers the first yes and the second largely no. ⚠️ Separating them is what turns a fence-sitting answer into a graded verdict.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

⚠️ Two claims are routinely merged and should not be. ⚠️ The first is that the settlement was unstable - that it contained features which made its own breakdown more likely. ⚠️ The second is that it caused the Second World War. ⚠️ The first is well supported. ⚠️ The second is explicitly resisted by the evidence base, and an answer that asserts it without qualification will be marked down.

✅ **Fact:** the account's conclusion is that this collection of peace treaties was not a conspicuous success.

### 2. The six recorded weaknesses

✅ The account's list is specific, and each item names a different kind of failure.

| # | ✅ Weakness | ⚠️ Kind of failure |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | It divided Europe into states which wanted to revise the settlement - Germany being the main one - and those which wanted to preserve it; the latter were only lukewarm in their support | **Structural**: it created a permanent political division about its own legitimacy |
| 2 | The USA failed to ratify the settlement and never joined the League | **Institutional**: the organisation was built without its principal architect |
| 3 | France was left completely disenchanted because the Anglo-American guarantee of her frontiers could not now apply | **Security**: the state with the greatest interest in enforcement lost the support it had bargained for |
| 4 | Italy felt cheated because she had not received all the territory promised her in 1915 | **Coalition**: a victor became a revisionist |
| 5 | Russia was ignored, because the powers did not want to negotiate with its Bolshevik government | **Geopolitical**: the largest European state was outside the system |
| 6 | Germany was only temporarily weakened and was soon strong enough to challenge certain of the terms | **Enforcement**: the defeated state retained the capacity to revise |

✅ **Fact:** the advanced material adds two further weaknesses to the same list: that some minority questions were left unresolved, and that Germany remained economically strong enough to recover and retaliate politically.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read items 1 to 6 as a single argument rather than as a list. ⚠️ Three of the four powers that might have enforced the settlement were removed from the enforcement equation - the United States by non-ratification, Russia by exclusion, Italy by grievance - leaving France, which had lost its guarantee, and Britain, which had never wanted severity. ⚠️ Meanwhile the state with the strongest motive to revise retained the economic capacity to do so. ⚠️ That is a description of a settlement whose enforcement mechanism was dismantled in the same eighteen months in which it was created.

### 3. The great failing, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** since Germany was still the strongest power in Europe economically, the great failing of the peace settlement was that it left the Germans with a sense of resentment and grievance, but did not leave them too weak to retaliate and seek revenge.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the topic's central formulation and it should be understood as a statement about a *relation*, not about severity. ⚠️ A settlement can be stable in two ways: by being generous enough that the defeated accept it, or by being enforced strongly enough that the defeated cannot alter it. ⚠️ The 1919 settlement chose neither. ⚠️ Every subordinate finding in this lesson is an instance of that relation: severity without enforcement, grievance without incapacity, principle without consistency, an institution without its architect.

⚠️ The corresponding answer-writing line is: the settlement was unstable less because it was harsh than because it was unenforced; it created grievances proportionate to a victory whose costs the victors were unwilling to continue paying.

### 4. The softer verdict, stated fairly

⚠️ A "critically evaluate" answer that gives only the hard verdict has done half the work. ⚠️ Three defences are recorded and all three are serious.

**Defence one: the problems were intractable.** ✅ Gilbert White, one of the American delegates, put it well when he remarked that, given the intricacy of the problems involved, "it is not surprising that they made a bad peace: what is surprising is that they managed to make peace at all". ⚠️ Lesson 16's four constraints supply the substance of this defence.

**Defence two: the settlement was workable.** ✅ With the availability of new sources, many historians find themselves in sympathy with that assessment and argue that the settlement can now be seen "as a workable compromise", and perhaps the best that could have been achieved under difficult circumstances.

**Defence three: the later causes were decisive.** ✅ There were some mistakes, but the peacemakers cannot be blamed for Hitler's rise to power, and certainly not for the Second World War. ✅ P. M. H. Bell, in *Origins of the Second World War in Europe* (2007), argues that in the early 1920s Europe, including Germany, was beginning to recover well from the after-effects of the war, and that the tragedy was that "the outline of a successful European recovery was cut off in its prime by the great depression and its dreadful consequence, the advent of Hitler".

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Bell thesis is the most important of the three because it is a claim about *causal sequence* rather than about fairness, and it is therefore testable in principle. ⚠️ It asserts that a recovery was under way and was interrupted by an external shock. ⚠️ If that is right, then the line from 1919 to 1939 runs through 1929 and cannot be drawn directly. ⚠️ The candidate does not have to accept the thesis, but an answer that does not engage with it is arguing against a position nobody now defends.

### 5. What a continuity claim can and cannot support

⚠️ This is the most common place for a good answer to overreach. ⚠️ Four graded statements, from safest to least safe.

| Claim | ⚠️ Status |
|---|---|
| The settlement created grievances that later political movements used | ✅ **Fully supported.** The account records that the Sudeten and Polish German populations gave Hitler an excuse to begin territorial demands, and that Article 231 and reparations were destabilising |
| The settlement's enforcement structure was defective from the outset | ✅ **Fully supported** by the six weaknesses |
| The settlement made a second conflict more likely than a settlement with either genuine reconciliation or sustained enforcement | ⚠️ **Defensible as an inference**, provided it is presented as a comparative probability rather than as a cause |
| The settlement caused the Second World War | ❌ **Not supported.** ✅ The peacemakers cannot be blamed for Hitler's rise, and certainly not for the Second World War; ✅ the Depression and the advent of Hitler are recorded as the decisive later ruptures |

⚠️ **The sentence to write:** the settlement supplied the grievances and removed the means of enforcing itself; the Depression and the political movements of the 1930s supplied the rest, and a causal line drawn straight from Versailles to 1939 omits the decisive intervening rupture.

### 6. Counterfactuals: the rules

⚠️ Counterfactual reasoning is legitimate in this topic and is easy to do badly. ⚠️ Four rules make it defensible.

1. **Change one variable, not the world.** "If reparations had been set at £2000 million" is a disciplined counterfactual; "if the peacemakers had been wiser" is not, because it specifies no mechanism.
2. **Change something that was actually proposed at the time.** ✅ Keynes proposed £2000 million; ✅ Clemenceau proposed an independent Rhineland and French annexation of the Saar; ✅ some historians think German participation in the negotiations was reasonable. ⚠️ These were live options, which is what makes the counterfactual historical rather than speculative.
3. **Trace the mechanism, and stop when it becomes untraceable.** A counterfactual can support a claim about the next link in a chain; it cannot support a claim about an outcome twenty years and several unrelated shocks later.
4. **State the counterfactual as a question about what the evidence permits, not as a conclusion.**

⚠️ **Three disciplined counterfactuals for this topic:**

| Counterfactual | ⚠️ What the evidence permits |
|---|---|
| If reparations had been set near £2000 million in 1921 | ⚠️ The default cycle, the enforcement crises and the annual renewal of the grievance would have been less likely; ✅ the Allies reached that figure in 1929 anyway, which shows it was feasible. ⚠️ It does not follow that German revisionism would have disappeared, since the territorial and war-guilt grievances were separate |
| If the United States had ratified and joined the League | ⚠️ ✅ The Anglo-American guarantee to France would have applied, removing the specific cause of French disenchantment, and a sanctions regime would have included the largest economy. ⚠️ It does not follow that collective security would have worked, which is a question for the interwar owner |
| If Germany had been admitted to the negotiations | ✅ Some historians think this might have led to a toning-down of some harsher terms and would certainly have deprived Hitler of the "Diktat" argument. ⚠️ It does not follow that Germany would have accepted the territorial terms, which were the object of revisionism in any case |

⚠️ Notice the shape of all three: a specific permitted inference followed by an explicit limit. ⚠️ That shape is what makes counterfactual reasoning an asset in an answer rather than a liability.

### 7. The historiographical map

⚠️ Four positions, each with its recorded evidence.

| Position | ⚠️ Claim | ✅ Anchored in |
|---|---|---|
| **Punitive-peace critique** | The settlement was too harsh and produced revanchism | ✅ Keynes's resignation and his £2000 million figure; ✅ Nicolson's remark; ✅ the reparations verdict |
| **Unenforced-peace critique** | The settlement was not too harsh but was not sustained | ✅ The six weaknesses; ✅ the great failing; ✅ Lausanne as the proof case |
| **Workable-compromise revision** | It was the best available in 1919 and functioned tolerably until 1929 | ✅ White's remark; ✅ the "workable compromise" assessment; ✅ Bell's recovery thesis |
| **Exculpatory limit** | Whatever its faults, the peacemakers are not responsible for 1939 | ✅ The peacemakers cannot be blamed for Hitler's rise or for the Second World War |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the second and third positions are compatible, and combining them produces the strongest available answer: the settlement was a workable compromise whose enforcement mechanism was dismantled almost immediately, and it therefore failed for reasons that were partly built into it and partly supplied by later events. ⚠️ The first position survives only in respect of reparations, where it was conceded in 1929; the fourth is a limit on all three.

### 8. Criticism and reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ If the peacemakers are not responsible for 1939, why does this topic matter for understanding the interwar period at all?

**The reply.** ⚠️ Because supplying the conditions and causing the outcome are different relations. ⚠️ The settlement supplied a divided Europe of revisionist and status-quo states, a set of minority populations available to be claimed, a grievance renewed annually by reparations, an enfeebled security structure and a defeated power with intact industrial capacity. ⚠️ That is a description of an unstable environment, and instability is what makes an external shock politically decisive. ⚠️ The honest formulation is that 1919 built a structure that could not absorb a shock, and 1929 delivered one.

**The second objection.** ⚠️ Is the "unenforced rather than harsh" verdict not simply a preference for enforcement, which would have required permanent occupation and repression?

**The reply.** ⚠️ It is a diagnosis rather than a recommendation, and that distinction should be stated. ⚠️ The finding is that the settlement required either reconciliation or enforcement to be stable and received neither. ⚠️ Whether sustained enforcement was politically available to democracies whose electorates had just endured total war is genuinely doubtful, and that doubt is part of the finding rather than an objection to it. ⚠️ It may well be that no stable settlement was available on the terms the victors could sell at home, which is a stronger and sadder conclusion than blaming Clemenceau.

**The residual.** ⚠️ Whether a more generous settlement would have produced a reconciled Germany is not decidable on the available evidence, and the counterfactual rules in section 6 forbid asserting it.

### 9. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the advanced material records the framework for this question directly: mention the peacemakers' aims; show successes, including some self-determination, formal peace and new states; show failures, including no American participation, Italian and German grievance and weak enforcement; and end with the later depression and Hitler as force multipliers.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Why did the peace settlement of 1919 fail to secure a durable peace in Europe? (Directive: explanatory "why". Ceiling: 250 words.)
> ⚠️ "The Treaty of Versailles caused the Second World War." Critically examine. (Directive: critically examine. Ceiling: 250 words.)
> ⚠️ Was the peace settlement of 1919 doomed from the start? (Directive: evaluative question. Ceiling: 250 words.)

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Separate the two questions in your first sentence - defective and culpable are different - and answer them differently. ⚠️ Use three or four of the six weaknesses, not all six. ⚠️ Always include the Bell thesis or the "workable compromise" assessment, because an answer that gives only the critique is arguing against a straw position. ⚠️ Close with the graded continuity statement: the settlement supplied the conditions and removed the means of enforcing itself; the Depression and the politics of the 1930s supplied the rest.

### Traps

- Asserting that Versailles caused the Second World War. ✅ The peacemakers cannot be blamed for Hitler's rise, and certainly not for the war.
- Giving only the critique and ignoring the workable-compromise position and the Bell thesis.
- Treating "harsh" as the explanation. ✅ The recorded failing is grievance combined with retained capacity.
- Forgetting the American non-ratification and its specific effect on France.
- Forgetting that Italy, a victor, became a revisionist.
- Using undisciplined counterfactuals. Change one variable that was actually proposed, trace the mechanism, and state the limit.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The collection of peace treaties was not a conspicuous success.
2. ✅ Weakness one: Europe divided into revisionist states, Germany the main one, and status-quo states that were only lukewarm.
3. ✅ Weakness two: the USA failed to ratify and never joined the League.
4. ✅ Weakness three: France was disenchanted because the Anglo-American guarantee of her frontiers could not apply.
5. ✅ Weakness four: Italy felt cheated of the territory promised in 1915.
6. ✅ Weakness five: Russia was ignored because the powers would not negotiate with the Bolsheviks.
7. ✅ Weakness six: Germany was only temporarily weakened and soon strong enough to challenge certain terms; the advanced material adds unresolved minority questions and German economic strength.
8. ✅ The great failing: it left the Germans with resentment and grievance but not too weak to retaliate and seek revenge.
9. ⚠️ The formulation: unstable less because harsh than because unenforced.
10. ✅ Softer verdict: Gilbert White on the surprise being that they made peace at all; many historians now see it as a workable compromise; the peacemakers cannot be blamed for Hitler or for the Second World War.
11. ✅ Bell, *Origins of the Second World War in Europe* (2007): Europe including Germany was recovering well in the early 1920s, and the outline of a successful recovery was cut off in its prime by the great depression and the advent of Hitler.
12. ✅ Contemporary critics: Nicolson - if I were the Germans, I shouldn't sign for a moment; Keynes resigned in protest and came home.
13. ⚠️ Four counterfactual rules: change one variable; change something actually proposed; trace the mechanism and stop; present as permitted inference with a stated limit.
14. ⚠️ Graded continuity: the settlement supplied the grievances and removed the means of enforcing itself; the Depression and the politics of the 1930s supplied the rest.

### Concept check

**Question:** "The Treaty of Versailles caused the Second World War." State how far the recorded evidence supports this, and give the formulation you would write instead.

**Model answer:** The evidence supports a great deal short of the claim. It fully supports the finding that the settlement was defective in ways that made later breakdown more likely: it divided Europe into revisionist states, Germany chief among them, and status-quo states that were only lukewarm in their support; the United States failed to ratify the settlement and never joined the League; France was left disenchanted because the Anglo-American guarantee of her frontiers could not apply; Italy felt cheated of territory promised in 1915; Russia was ignored because the powers would not negotiate with its Bolshevik government; and Germany was only temporarily weakened, being virtually untouched territorially and producing three times as much steel as France by 1921. It also supports the finding that specific provisions became instruments for later revisionism, since the three million Germans in the Sudetenland and the million under Polish rule gave Hitler an excuse to begin territorial demands. What the evidence does not support is the causal claim itself. The account states that the peacemakers cannot be blamed for Hitler's rise to power, and certainly not for the Second World War; many historians now see the settlement as a workable compromise and perhaps the best achievable in difficult circumstances; and P. M. H. Bell argues that in the early 1920s Europe including Germany was recovering well, and that the outline of a successful European recovery was cut off in its prime by the great depression and its dreadful consequence, the advent of Hitler. The formulation to write is this: the settlement's great failing was that it left the Germans with resentment and grievance but not too weak to retaliate, so it supplied the conditions for revisionism and simultaneously dismantled its own enforcement; the Depression and the politics of the 1930s supplied the decisive rupture, and a line drawn straight from 1919 to 1939 omits it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Substituting a hedge for a verdict. "There were faults on both sides" is not an answer. The disciplined position is that the settlement was defective and the peacemakers were largely not culpable, that these are answers to two different questions, and that the causal chain to 1939 runs through an intervening economic catastrophe that no peace conference could have foreseen or prevented.

*Everything taught in this session now has to survive contact with a three-hour paper. The final lesson is about nothing else.*

## Lesson 25 - How all of this is converted into marks

Progress: 25/25 | Stage: Synthesis | Subtopic: the six directive families and their answer spines; the opening-sentence decisions; the evidence budget; the date control; the prohibited formulations; the ten-marker and twenty-marker structures; what to do when the question straddles two owners

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the directive and demand map held by this topic's core material, its mark-scaled structures for ten, fifteen and twenty marks, its qualified thesis templates, its verdict scaffolds and its factual-risk control list; and the advanced material's two answer frameworks on the harshness of Versailles and on the failure of the 1919 settlement
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage for this session is held in Lesson 7
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: six directive families, and what each is really testing

| If the question says | ✅ It is really testing | ❌ Do not write | ⚠️ Spine |
|---|---|---|---|
| *Why did it become a war of attrition?* | The technology-tactics mismatch, not incompetence | A battle list | Relation → five difficulties → break-in/break-through/exploitation → 1918 as proof |
| *Why did the Central Powers lose?* | Cumulative causation across fronts, sea and economy | One battle or one ally | Sort by kind → nominate one → show the four-step chain |
| *Effects on Europe and the wider world* | Institutional **and** social effects, plus the shift of world power | Casualty statistics | Institutional / social / structural, in that order |
| *Discuss the peace settlement* | The **cluster** of treaties and their structural instability | Versailles alone | Name five treaties → six weaknesses → the great failing → Lausanne |
| *Total war* | Mobilisation of whole societies, not the scale of fighting | "It was a big war" | Three claims → four features → the limits → the selection mechanism |
| *Redrawal of national boundaries* | Successor states, minorities and mandates | Only Germany's losses | Dissolution before Paris → the four exception categories → the rule → minorities treaties |

*⚠️ Identify the family in the first ten seconds. ⚠️ Every one of the six has a different spine, a different evidence set and a different verdict shape, and a script that writes the wrong spine cannot recover by writing it well.*

### 1. The opening-sentence decisions

⚠️ Four decisions are made before the first full stop, and they determine most of the mark.

1. **Which of the three questions is this?** Military course, total war and society, or the settlement. Name it in a subordinate clause.
2. **Which owner does it belong to?** If the stem is about causes, alliances, militarism or the July crisis, it belongs to the previous topic and this material supplies at most a sentence. If it is about the League's operation, appeasement or the Depression, it belongs to the interwar owner.
3. **What is the verdict shape?** Cumulative causation, graded audit, two-part relation, or concede-then-criticise.
4. **What is the one formulation this answer will be built around?** Choose it before writing, from the list in section 5.

### 2. Mark-scaled structures

| Marks | ⚠️ Structure |
|---|---|
| **10 marks, 150 words** | Thesis → **two** mechanisms with named evidence → one consequence → verdict. No historiography, no more than two figures, no boundary excursions |
| **15 marks, 250 words** | Thesis → course or provision by category → the mechanism that explains it → the strongest counter-position → verdict with a qualification |
| **20 marks, 250 words** | All of the above → **plus** the social dimension and the settlement's structural instability or the boundary legacy → verdict |

⚠️ Note that 15 and 20 mark answers in this subject are both written to a 250-word ceiling; the difference is in the number of dimensions covered, not in length. ⚠️ That means a 20-marker must compress each element further, which is why the categories and formulations in this lesson matter more at 20 than at 10.

### 3. The evidence budget

⚠️ A 250-word answer can carry about six items of hard evidence before it becomes a list. ⚠️ Spend them deliberately.

| Budget line | ⚠️ Recommended spend |
|---|---|
| Dates | Two or three, and only load-bearing ones - ✅ 11 November 1918; ✅ 28 June 1919; ✅ Lausanne 1923 |
| Figures | Two, each doing argumentative work - ✅ £6600 million and ✅ £2000 million; or ✅ the American trade share from ten to over twenty per cent; or ✅ German steel at three times French output by 1921 |
| Named instruments | Two or three - ✅ Article 231; ✅ the Covenant; ✅ the Treaty of London 1915 |
| Named persons | Two at most - ✅ Keynes; ✅ Mustafa Kemal; ✅ Foch; ✅ Ludendorff |
| Historians | One, and only in an evaluative question - ✅ Todman on memory; ✅ Sheffield on Haig; ✅ Bell on the Depression |
| Formulation | One, carried through the whole answer |

### 4. Date control

⚠️ These must never be confused, because each marks a distinct military, diplomatic or legal stage.

| ✅ Date | Stage |
|---|---|
| September 1914 | Marne |
| November 1914 | Ottoman entry |
| 1915 | Gallipoli; Italian entry in May; Bulgarian entry in October |
| 1916 | Verdun and the Somme; Jutland on 31 May; Brusilov in June |
| January 1917 | Unrestricted submarine warfare begins |
| April 1917 | United States entry |
| December 1917 | Russian withdrawal |
| 3 March 1918 | Brest-Litovsk |
| 8 August 1918 | Allied counter-offensive at Amiens |
| 29 September 1918 | Bulgarian armistice at Salonica |
| 30 October 1918 | Ottoman armistice at Mudros |
| 3 November 1918 | Austro-Hungarian armistice at Villa Giusti |
| 9 November 1918 | German republic declared |
| 11 November 1918 | German armistice |
| 18 January 1919 | Paris conference opens |
| 28 June 1919 | Versailles signed; the Polish minorities treaty signed |
| 1919 | St Germain; Neuilly |
| 10 January 1920 | Versailles in force; the League formally exists |
| 1920 | Trianon; Sevres |
| 1921 | Reparations announced at £6600 million |
| 1923 | Lausanne |
| 1929 | Young Plan reduces reparations to £2000 million |

### 5. The formulations worth memorising

⚠️ Each is a complete analytical claim that can organise an answer.

1. ⚠️ **Attrition:** trench deadlock arose because defensive firepower had outrun the means of movement and communication; the war became a contest of endurance because neither side could convert local success into strategic breakthrough.
2. ⚠️ **Defeat:** the Central Powers were not defeated at a point but exhausted across a system - blockade, a second front, a failed submarine gamble and the arrival of American resources.
3. ⚠️ **Total war:** total war destroyed the states least able to sustain total extraction.
4. ⚠️ **Effects:** the war destroyed four empires, created the first universal peace organisation, and shifted the world's economic centre out of Europe - three consequences no belligerent had sought.
5. ⚠️ **Settlement:** the settlement was unstable less because it was harsh than because it was unenforced; it created grievances proportionate to a victory the victors were unwilling to keep paying for.
6. ⚠️ **Self-determination:** it was applied where it weakened the defeated and withheld where it would have strengthened them or weakened the victors.
7. ⚠️ **Empires:** the war destroyed the empires in which nationality and sovereignty were the same question and enlarged the empires in which they were not.
8. ⚠️ **Lausanne:** where a defeated state had the capacity to resist and the victors lacked the will to compel, the settlement was simply rewritten - which shows that the treaties rested on power, not on consent.

### 6. Prohibited formulations and factual-risk controls

⚠️ Each of these will cost marks with an informed examiner.

1. ❌ Treating Versailles as the whole settlement. ✅ Name St Germain, Trianon, Sevres and Lausanne, and Neuilly.
2. ❌ Giving a reparations figure other than ✅ £6600 million, later reduced to ✅ £2000 million under the Young Plan.
3. ❌ Saying Article 231 was called the "war-guilt clause" in the treaty text. ✅ It fixed blame and provided the legal basis for reparations; the political label attached afterwards.
4. ❌ Saying Germany was fully occupied before surrendering. ✅ Germany asked for an armistice before deep invasion of German soil, and no enemy troops had set foot on German soil.
5. ❌ Saying the USA won the war by troop numbers alone. ✅ Food, merchant ships, credit and morale also mattered, and by the end of 1917 only one American division had been in action.
6. ❌ Saying Turkey accepted Sevres. ✅ Turkish resistance forced revision through Lausanne.
7. ❌ Dating the armistice other than ✅ 11 November 1918.
8. ❌ Naming mandate administrations, mandate procedures or individual minority petitions beyond the classes and the supervising commission recorded here.
9. ❌ Saying the mandate system was simply a disguise for annexation, or that it was a genuine trusteeship. ✅ The ambiguity must be stated on both sides.
10. ❌ Claiming the war "ended empire". ✅ It destroyed the Habsburg and Ottoman empires and enlarged the British and French; the maritime empires ended after 1945.
11. ❌ Asserting a settled death toll for the Armenian catastrophe, or attributing it collectively to a people rather than to the government, party and ministers responsible.
12. ❌ Asserting an influenza mortality figure, a colonial-participation figure without the "on the standard estimates" qualifier, or a total for the war's dead.
13. ❌ Asserting that Versailles caused the Second World War.
14. ❌ Importing the causes of 1914, the League's operational record, the Russian revolutions, fascism, post-1945 decolonisation or Indian wartime politics.
15. ❌ Repeating the three source-inherited errors. ✅ Kut-al-Amara surrendered on 29 April 1916, not March 1916. ✅ The *Lusitania* was sunk on 7 May 1915, with roughly 1,959 aboard and 1,198 killed, of whom 128 were Americans; "almost two thousand dead" conflates those aboard with those killed. ✅ Versailles detached Memel to the Principal Allied and Associated Powers, and Lithuanian control followed afterwards.

### 7. When the question straddles two owners

⚠️ Three common straddles and the correct handling of each.

| Straddle | ⚠️ Handling |
|---|---|
| A question on the war's causes that reaches into its course | ⚠️ The previous owner leads. This session supplies at most the opening condition - the failure of the short war - and hands back |
| A question on the interwar period that reaches back to 1919 | ⚠️ This session supplies the six weaknesses and the great failing in two sentences, and the interwar owner supplies everything after 1921 |
| A question on colonialism or decolonisation that reaches into the mandates | ⚠️ This session supplies the mandate mechanism, its classes, the supervising commission and the ambiguity formulation, and hands over at 1945 |

⚠️ In every case, signal the handover explicitly in the answer with a clause such as "the interwar record of the League lies outside this question". ⚠️ Examiners read that as control, not as evasion.

### 8. A worked opening, for each of the three questions

⚠️ Three model openings, each about forty words, each making all four opening-sentence decisions at once.

> ⚠️ **Military:** "The war lasted because neither coalition could convert local success into strategic result: defensive firepower had outrun movement and communication, and every alternative to frontal attrition - Gallipoli, Italy, Mesopotamia - had been tried and had failed within 1915."

> ⚠️ **Society:** "Total war names the mobilisation of whole societies rather than the scale of fighting: states took direction of mines, factories, railways, shipping and agriculture, allocated labour by administrative decision, and treated enemy civilian food supply as a primary target."

> ⚠️ **Settlement:** "The settlement was five treaties, not one, and its defect was relational rather than moral: it left Germany resentful and not too weak to retaliate, while dismantling the enforcement structure - American ratification, the guarantee to France, Italian satisfaction - on which its own durability depended."

### 9. The objection to a technique lesson, and the reply

**The objection.** ⚠️ Does a lesson on directive families, evidence budgets and memorised formulations not encourage exactly what examiners penalise - a pre-fabricated answer bolted onto whatever stem appears?

**The reply.** ⚠️ The risk is real and the remedy is built into the design rather than added as a warning. ⚠️ Note what this lesson does *not* supply: it contains no model paragraphs, no ready-made introductions beyond three forty-word openings that are frames rather than content, and no answer that could be transcribed. ⚠️ What it supplies is a *routing* apparatus - which question is being asked, which owner holds the evidence, which verdict shape the directive requires - and routing cannot be pre-fabricated because it depends on the stem. ⚠️ The eight formulations are the closest thing to prepared material, and each is a contestable analytical claim rather than a passage: writing "the settlement was unstable less because it was harsh than because it was unenforced" commits the candidate to defending it from evidence in the next three sentences, which is the opposite of a template.

**What remains true in the objection.** ⚠️ A candidate who memorises the formulations without the twenty-four lessons behind them will produce fluent assertions that collapse under a follow-up demand for evidence. ⚠️ This lesson is therefore the last lesson and not a substitute for the others, and it is deliberately the shortest in the session.

### 10. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** this lesson serves every demand in the directive map rather than one of them, and it is the only lesson in the session that introduces no new historical content.

**How it is examined, indirectly**

> ⚠️ Any stem from the six directive families. The material here is what converts the preceding twenty-four lessons into a script written under time pressure, and it is graded through them.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Rehearse the routing, not the prose: take an unseen stem, name the family, the owner, the verdict shape and the formulation in under ninety seconds, and then stop. ⚠️ Rehearse the controls separately, because they are breached under time pressure rather than through ignorance. ⚠️ Rehearse the social paragraph on its own, since "effect on the society" is printed in the syllabus and is the element most often thin in a finished answer.

### Traps

- Writing a narrative when the directive asks for an explanation.
- Answering a settlement question with Versailles alone.
- Spending the evidence budget on decoration instead of argument.
- Confusing the four armistice dates, or the five treaties and their states.
- Failing to signal a boundary when a question straddles owners.
- Omitting the social dimension in a twenty-mark effects answer, where it is explicitly examinable.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ Six directive families: attrition; defeat; effects; settlement; total war; boundaries. Each has its own spine.
2. ⚠️ Four opening-sentence decisions: which of the three questions; which owner; which verdict shape; which formulation.
3. ⚠️ 10 marks: thesis, two mechanisms, one consequence, verdict. 15 marks: add the counter-position and a qualification. 20 marks: add the social dimension and the structural legacy.
4. ⚠️ Evidence budget: about six hard items - two or three dates, two figures, two or three instruments, two names, one historian, one formulation.
5. ✅ Date control: Marne September 1914; Ottoman entry November 1914; Gallipoli 1915; Verdun and the Somme 1916; Jutland 31 May 1916; unrestricted submarine warfare January 1917; US entry April 1917; Russian withdrawal December 1917; Brest-Litovsk 3 March 1918; Amiens 8 August 1918; Salonica 29 September, Mudros 30 October, Villa Giusti 3 November, German republic 9 November, armistice 11 November 1918; Paris opens 18 January 1919; Versailles 28 June 1919; in force 10 January 1920; Trianon and Sevres 1920; reparations 1921; Lausanne 1923; Young Plan 1929.
6. ⚠️ Eight memorised formulations, one per major demand.
7. ❌ Fifteen prohibited formulations and factual-risk controls.
8. ⚠️ Three straddles: causes, interwar, colonialism - signal the handover explicitly.

### Concept check

**Question:** A 15-mark question reads: "Examine the effects of the First World War on Europe and the wider world." Plan the answer in under ninety seconds: state the family, the spine, the evidence budget and the closing formulation.

**Model answer:** The family is effects, so the demand is institutional and social effects plus the shift of world power, and the prohibited response is a casualty list. The spine has three movements. Institutional: four empires destroyed and their political systems replaced - a German republic declared on 9 November 1918 after the Kaiser's abdication; the Habsburg Empire dissolved by its own nationalities before the peace conference met; Bolshevik Russia withdrawn and then excluded from the settlement; the Ottoman empire partitioned into mandates with a Turkish national state emerging by force at Lausanne in 1923 - together with a new international organisation whose Covenant took effect on 10 January 1920. Social: the permanent enlargement of state capacity over mines, factories, railways, shipping and agriculture, the administrative allocation of labour, the entry of women into war industries, the treatment of blockaded civilians as strategic targets, and the mobilisation of colonial soldiers, labourers and revenues. Structural: the decline in Europe's prestige and the beginning of the end of European domination, with the American share of world trade rising from ten per cent in 1914 to over twenty per cent by 1919 while Japan and China began their own industrialisation programmes. The evidence budget is one date - 11 November 1918 - one institutional marker, the Covenant in force on 10 January 1920, one figure, the trade share, and one comparative figure, German steel at three times French output by 1921 if the answer needs to show that the defeated recovered fastest. The closing formulation is that the war destroyed four empires, created the first universal peace organisation and shifted the world's economic centre out of Europe - three consequences no belligerent had sought.

**Misconception to avoid:** Planning by recalling content rather than by identifying the demand. The content of this topic is large enough that an unplanned answer will produce whatever is most vivid, which is usually the fighting. Ninety seconds spent naming the family, the spine and the closing formulation is the highest-return investment available in the examination hall.

✅ **Fact:** the verified question position for this topic is unambiguous: across **2018-2025 and 2026**, **no** General Studies question is routed to this topic as its owner.

✅ **Fact:** the routing ledgers held in this repository confirm that position across every audited span. The complete Mains routing for **2018-2023** returns **three** General Studies Paper I questions tagged to World History - one each in 2019, 2021 and 2023 - and **none** is routed here. The routing for **2024-2025** returns two World History questions, and although one of them concerns the First World War directly, it is routed to the **preceding topic** on the world in 1914 and the outbreak of the war, which owns causation and the balance-of-power demand. The complete **2026** General Studies Paper I ledger contains no World History item at all. The three Prelims routing ledgers for **2018-2023**, **2024-2025** and **2026** were searched for this topic's principal names and themes - First World War, world war, Versailles, League of Nations, Ottoman, armistice, Trianon, mandate, Wilson - and return **nil** in every span for this topic.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that is a routing result and not a judgement about importance, and the preparation consequence is specific rather than reassuring. ✅ World History is directly owned by the General Studies Paper I clause naming world wars, redrawal of national boundaries, colonization and decolonization and their forms and effect on the society, and ✅ this topic fits three of those phrases directly. ⚠️ A topic that has not yet been set as an owner is not a topic that will not be set; it is a topic whose questions must be prepared from its own answer architecture rather than rehearsed from past papers. ⚠️ The practical implication is that the original Mains practice below is not supplementary here - it is the only rehearsal available, and the probable framings recorded by the two canonical owners are the best available guide to what a question would look like.

⚠️ **One further consideration raises the probability rather than lowering it.** ✅ The immediately preceding topic was examined in 2024 on the First World War's causation. ⚠️ Where an examiner has recently tested the causes of a conflict, the course, the consequences and the settlement become live adjacent demands, and this topic owns all three.

## Pre-2018 demands and the textbook-question control

⚠️ **Provenance note:** the routing ledgers held in this repository begin at **2018**, so no verbatim pre-2018 question text is held locally, and none is reconstructed or rendered here.

⚠️ **A separate control is required for this topic specifically.** The core printed account used in this session ends its chapter with three study questions: on the reasons the war was not over by Christmas 1914; on why the 1919 settlement provoked so much opposition among the Germans; and on the extent to which the Paris settlement was shaped by the principle of self-determination. ⚠️ **These are textbook questions, not UPSC previous-year questions.** They appear in this session only as *probable framings*, they are labelled as inferences wherever used, and they must never be presented to anyone as examination precedent. ⚠️ Their value is that they identify what a standard account treats as the chapter's examinable core, which is why the roadmap gives each of them a dedicated lesson.
