✅ **Fact:** the complete **2026 General Studies Paper I** ledger covers all its questions and **none** is routed to World History. ✅ The 2026 Prelims, CSAT, General Studies Paper II, General Studies Paper III, Essay and static-gap ledgers likewise return **no** World History item, and a name-and-theme search across all of them for this topic returns **nil**. ⚠️ The subject's most recent owned Mains questions therefore remain those of 2024 and 2025, and neither is owned by this topic.

## Objective-question control

✅ **Fact:** no verified objective question touching this topic's names or themes was found in any Prelims ledger for 2018-2023, 2024-2025 or 2026. ⚠️ **No objective item is therefore displayed in this session**, no key is asserted or inferred anywhere, and no stem is reconstructed from memory. ⚠️ Where a candidate encounters an objective item on this topic elsewhere, the elimination material is the factual-risk control list of Lesson 25 section 6 together with the date control of section 4, because every item on those lists is a distinction on which a close-option question would be built: 1915 against 1917 for submarine warfare; April 1917 for American entry against December 1917 for Russian withdrawal; the four armistice dates; the five treaties and their states; Sevres against Lausanne; £6600 million against £2000 million; and the mandate classes.

## What to rehearse, given a nil return

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a topic with no owned question is prepared differently from one with a bank of past papers, and the difference is a matter of method rather than of effort.

1. **Rehearse architectures, not answers.** The six directive families in Lesson 25 exhaust what can realistically be asked, and each has a spine. Practise producing the spine from the stem in under ninety seconds.
2. **Rehearse the controls, because they are what a first attempt gets wrong.** Fifteen items in the factual-risk list, of which the treaty-cluster control, the reparations figures and the armistice date are the most frequently breached.
3. **Rehearse the social paragraph separately.** ✅ The syllabus clause names *effect on the society*, and the total-war material of Lesson 9 is the part most likely to be thin under time pressure because it requires mechanisms rather than sentiment.
4. **Rehearse the hand-offs.** Five boundaries, one clause each. A script that trespasses into the causes of 1914, the League's operational record, the Russian revolutions, fascism or Indian wartime politics loses time and gains nothing.
5. **Use the recorded probable framings as the practice bank.** ✅ The two canonical owners record six between them: why the war became a prolonged war of attrition; the effects of the war on Europe and the wider world; the major terms and weaknesses of the post-1919 settlement; a critical evaluation of the Treaty of Versailles; why the settlement failed to secure a durable peace; and whether the war was the first total war of the modern age. ⚠️ The six original questions below are built to cover all six framings.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These run across lesson blocks rather than within a single lesson. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks in that block.

## Block A - The war as fought, Lessons 2 to 8

### Cumulative check A1 - from a failed plan to a chosen strategy

**Question:** Trace the path from the failure of the German plan in September 1914 to the deliberate adoption of attrition in 1916, and identify at each stage what had been ruled out.

**Model answer:** Four stages, each closing an option. The first is the Marne in September 1914, which ruled out a short war: the plan for a rapid western victory was ruined, France would not be knocked out in six weeks, Germany faced full-scale war on two fronts, the war of movement ended with trench lines eventually stretching from the Alps to the Channel, and there was time for the British navy to bring its blockade to bear. The second is the trench condition itself, which ruled out breakthrough by frontal weight: wire had to be cleared by bombardment that destroyed surprise, aircraft and balloons spotted the approach, magazine rifles and machine-guns made frontal assault suicidal and cavalry useless, the barrage cratered the ground the attacker had to cross, and any ground won formed a salient whose flanks were vulnerable. The third is 1915, which ruled out going round: the western attacks at Neuve Chapelle, Loos and in Champagne produced no decisive breakthrough; Gallipoli failed when the March naval attack ran into mines and destroyed surprise, was abandoned in December, and turned out to be the last chance of helping Russia through the Black Sea; Italy was bought with the secret Treaty of London in May 1915 and made little headway; and the Mesopotamian force was besieged at Kut and surrendered on 29 April 1916. The fourth is 1916, in which what remained was exhaustion pursued deliberately: Verdun was designed to draw in and destroy the best French troops, and the Somme was launched to relieve Verdun, take over more of the line and prevent German reinforcement of the east. Attrition was therefore a residue, not a preference.

**Misconception to avoid:** Describing 1915 as a series of blunders and 1916 as a failure of imagination. Every stage closed a specific option, and the options were closed in a logical order. An answer that can name what each stage ruled out is explaining the war; an answer that lists campaigns is narrating it.

### Cumulative check A2 - the two blockades, the Ottoman front and the shape of the global war

**Question:** Using the naval war and the Ottoman fronts together, explain how a European conflict acquired decisive non-European dimensions.

**Model answer:** The two are connected by a single geographical fact and a single strategic logic. The Ottoman entry in November 1914 meant that Turkey could cut Russia's main supply and trade route from the Black Sea into the Mediterranean, which isolated a great power from its allies without a battle being fought in Europe. That isolation drove the Gallipoli campaign, whose failure was the last chance of helping Russia by that route and therefore contributed to the collapse that removed Russia from the war in December 1917. The naval war supplies the other half. Britain's three aims were to blockade the Central Powers, to keep Allied trade routes open and to transport and supply the army through the Channel ports, and it achieved all three by mid-1918; the blockade caused desperate food shortages among German civilians and crippled exports. Germany, unable to contest the surface after its armed surface ships were destroyed by the end of 1914 and after the High Seas Fleet retired to Kiel following Jutland on 31 May 1916, replied beneath it, and the unrestricted campaign from January 1917 peaked in April with 430 ships lost and Britain down to about six weeks' corn supply. The counter was organisational - convoys, escorted by British, American and Japanese destroyers - and the political consequence was the entry of the United States. The global dimensions are therefore not decorative: an Asian entry helped destroy a European great power, an ocean-wide commerce war brought in a transatlantic one, and the ships that defeated the submarine were escorted in part by a Japanese navy. Add the extra-European theatres, the Indian-raised forces in Mesopotamia, the Anzac landings and the African campaigns, and the war is global in participation, resources and consequences.

**Misconception to avoid:** Proving the war's global character by listing distant battles. Most extra-European fighting was strategically secondary. What made the war global was that an Asian entry could strangle a European power's supply, that the commerce war ran across the Atlantic, and that the settlement redistributed non-European territory.

## Block B - Total war, collapse and the reckoning, Lessons 9 to 14

### Cumulative check B1 - why the societies broke before the armies

**Question:** Explain why the political systems of four states collapsed while those of the victors did not, and connect the answer to the concept of total war.

**Model answer:** Total war is the mobilisation of whole societies by the state - direction of industry, transport and food, administrative allocation of labour, management of opinion, and the treatment of the enemy's civilian population as a strategic target. The concept explains the collapses because it functions as a selection mechanism: the states destroyed were those whose administrative and political systems could not sustain indefinite extraction. Russia is the clearest case. It could mobilise six and a quarter million men by the end of 1914 and could not arm a third of them; it never recovered from the equipment losses at Tannenberg and the Masurian Lakes; its supply route through the Black Sea was closed; and the recorded causes of its withdrawal in December 1917 are continuous heavy losses, lack of arms and supplies, problems of transport and communications, and utterly incompetent leadership - three of which are failures of industrial and administrative capacity. Austria-Hungary was a multinational dynastic state whose subject nationalities had organised national movements, and it disintegrated as the nationalities declared themselves independent before the peace conference even met. Germany sustained extraction longest but broke at the end: the combination of military defeat and dire food shortages, themselves produced by the blockade, caused war-weariness, mutiny in the navy, destruction of morale in the army and revolution at home, with the Kaiser compelled to abdicate and a republic declared on 9 November 1918. The Ottoman state, defeated and exhausted, signed at Mudros on 30 October 1918. The victors also suffered the limits of mobilisation - the French army mutinied in 1917 after Nivelle's Champagne attack - but their capacity and their consent held, assisted from April 1917 by American food, merchant ships and credit.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the collapses as a consequence of defeat alone. Germany was defeated and did not dissolve as a state; Austria-Hungary dissolved before it was formally defeated. The variable is the capacity of the political and administrative system to bear total extraction, not the verdict on the battlefield.

### Cumulative check B2 - the numbers, and how to use them

**Question:** State the discipline governing the use of quantitative evidence in this topic, and demonstrate it by using two figures correctly and refusing two others.

**Model answer:** The discipline has five rules: use only figures recorded in the permitted sources; state the measure on which each is compiled; introduce them with a qualifier such as "on the figures given in the standard accounts"; never total, average or extrapolate; and prefer the comparative use, where a figure does argumentative work, over the decorative use. Two figures used correctly: first, on the recorded figures, the Germans lost over 280,000 killed at Verdun with no territorial gains, which is evidence not of scale but of the uncontrollability of attritional arithmetic by the side that initiates it. Second, on the recorded figures, France lost around twenty per cent of men of military age, which is evidence of demographic depth in the worst-affected belligerent and is the correct measure for a claim about a generation, where absolute totals are not. Two figures refused: no total for the war's dead is given, because adding the recorded national figures would produce a number no source supports and because the recorded figures explicitly exclude those crippled by the war and civilian casualties; and no mortality figure is given for the 1918-19 influenza pandemic or for the Armenian catastrophe, because estimates in both cases vary very widely between authorities and the variation is part of the historical dispute. The corrective case completes the discipline: Dan Todman argues that the lost-generation interpretation is something of a myth, that casualties were severe but not the wholesale destruction of a generation, and that overall just twelve per cent of fighting men died, the 20,000 British killed on the first day of the Somme being untypical of the war as a whole.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that refusing a figure weakens an answer. Visible discipline about evidence is itself a mark-earning demonstration, and an examiner who knows the material recognises "estimates vary very widely" as expertise rather than as ignorance.

## Block C - The settlement, Lessons 15 to 21

### Cumulative check C1 - the settlement as a cluster, audited

**Question:** Name the five treaties with their states and their principal transfers, and then identify the three findings that convert this recall into an argument.

**Model answer:** Versailles, 1919, with Germany: Alsace-Lorraine to France; Eupen, Moresnet and Malmedy to Belgium; North Schleswig to Denmark after a plebiscite; West Prussia and Posen to Poland with Danzig a free city under League administration; Memel detached to the Principal Allied and Associated Powers; the Saar under League administration for fifteen years with France using its coal mines; the Baltic states established as independent; Anschluss forbidden; the African colonies to mandates; an army of 100,000 without conscription, tanks, armoured cars, military aircraft or submarines and only six battleships; the Rhineland demilitarised and occupied; the War Guilt clause; and reparations announced in 1921 at £6600 million. St Germain, 1919, with Austria: Bohemia and Moravia to Czechoslovakia; Dalmatia, Bosnia and Herzegovina to what became Yugoslavia; Bukovina to Romania; Galicia to Poland; the South Tyrol to the Brenner Pass, Trentino, Istria and Trieste to Italy. Neuilly, 1919, with Bulgaria: territory to Greece including the Aegean coastline, and to Yugoslavia and Romania. Trianon, 1920, with Hungary: Slovakia and Ruthenia to Czechoslovakia; Croatia and Slovenia to Yugoslavia; Transylvania and the Banat of Temesvar to Romania. Sevres, 1920, with Turkey: Eastern Thrace, Aegean islands and Smyrna to Greece; Adalia and Rhodes to Italy; the Straits permanently open; Syria a French mandate and Palestine, Iraq and Transjordan British mandates - rejected, and revised by Lausanne in 1923. Three findings convert this into argument. First, central Europe was settled before the conference met, because the Habsburg nationalities had declared independence and the conference's task was to formalise and recognise what had taken place. Second, the treaties produced economically unviable states: Austria fell from twenty-two million to six and a half million with most of its industrial wealth lost, Hungary from twenty-one million to seven and a half, and new tariffs hampered trade through the whole Danube area. Third, Lausanne proves that the settlement rested on power: where a defeated state had the capacity to resist and the victors lacked the will to compel, the treaty was simply rewritten.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the recall of the five treaties as the answer. The names and transfers are the entry price; the marks lie in the three findings, and especially in the third.

### Cumulative check C2 - self-determination and the mandate, as one argument

**Question:** Show that the treatment of European minorities and the treatment of colonial populations in 1919 follow from a single rule, and state what the peacemakers did to mitigate the consequence.

**Model answer:** The rule is that self-determination was applied where it weakened the defeated and withheld where it would have strengthened them or weakened the victors, and both sets of cases satisfy it. In Europe it was applied to dissolve a defeated multinational empire into successor states, applied by plebiscite in North Schleswig and Upper Silesia, and applied to create Estonia, Latvia and Lithuania from territory Brest-Litovsk had given Germany - which the account calls self-determination carried into practice. It was withheld where application would have strengthened a defeated state: union with a completely German Austria of some seven million was specifically forbidden, probably because it would have made Germany larger and more powerful than in 1914, even though the economic case for the union was excellent. It was withheld where application would have weakened new allied states: almost three million Germans were left in the Sudetenland and around a million under Polish rule, justified on the ground that the new states needed them to be economically viable. Outside Europe the same rule produced mandates rather than independence: German colonies and Ottoman Arab territories were transferred to the victors under the Covenant's three classes, although Point 5 had promised an impartial adjustment of colonial claims in the interests of the populations concerned, and although the Arabs had been encouraged to expect independence. The mitigation was the minorities system, beginning with the treaty imposed on Poland on 28 June 1919, which required guarantees of civil, political and religious rights regardless of origin, language or religion, with complaint available to the League. That system is simultaneously evidence of good faith, an admission that the principle had not produced homogeneous states, and a source of grievance, since the obligation was imposed only on the new and defeated states.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the European and colonial cases as separate stories, one about hypocrisy in Europe and one about racism outside it. They are two applications of one rule about power, and stating the rule once and then testing it against both sets of cases is far stronger than two paragraphs of complaint.

## Block D - Consequences and verdict, Lessons 22 to 25

### Cumulative check D1 - the whole topic in one causal chain

**Question:** Construct a single causal chain from the failure of the short war in 1914 to the instability of the settlement in 1923, using at least eight recorded links.

**Model answer:** The chain runs as follows. One: the German plan for a rapid western victory failed at the Marne in September 1914, leaving a two-front war, a continuous fortified line and time for the British blockade. Two: defensive firepower had outrun the attacker's means of movement and communication, so no breakthrough was available, and the alternatives tried in 1915 - Gallipoli, Italy, Mesopotamia - all failed. Three: attrition was therefore adopted deliberately in 1916 at Verdun and on the Somme, and its cost forced both coalitions into total mobilisation of industry, transport, food, labour and opinion. Four: the blockade produced desperate food shortages in Germany, and desperation produced the unrestricted submarine campaign of January 1917, which failed against convoys and brought the United States into the war in April 1917. Five: total extraction destroyed the states least able to bear it - Russia withdrew in December 1917 after equipment failure, supply isolation and two revolutions, and was taken out by Brest-Litovsk on 3 March 1918. Six: the troops released allowed Ludendorff's spring offensive, which had to succeed before American manpower arrived, reached within forty miles of Paris and failed into an awkward bulge; the Allied counter-offensive from 8 August broke the Hindenburg Line by the end of September. Seven: the coalition collapsed from its weakest member - Bulgaria on 29 September, the Ottoman state at Mudros on 30 October, Austria-Hungary at Villa Giusti on 3 November - and Germany's own society broke into naval mutiny and revolution, producing a republic on 9 November and an armistice on 11 November, with no enemy troops on German soil. Eight: the conference that opened on 18 January 1919 therefore faced a dissolved Habsburg Empire, an excluded Russia, an absent Germany, prior secret treaties and mobilised electorates, and produced five treaties whose enforcement structure was dismantled by American non-ratification, French disenchantment and Italian grievance - until Turkey demonstrated at Lausanne in 1923 that the settlement rested on power.

**Misconception to avoid:** Producing a chronology instead of a chain. Each link must state a mechanism that produces the next, and the test is whether removing a link breaks the sequence. A list of dated events in order is not a causal chain.

### Cumulative check D2 - the verdict, graded

**Question:** Deliver a graded verdict on the settlement that distinguishes defect from culpability and states the limits of any continuity claim.

**Model answer:** The settlement was defective and the peacemakers were largely not culpable, and those are answers to two different questions. On defect, the recorded weaknesses are precise: Europe was divided into revisionist states, Germany chief among them, and status-quo states that were only lukewarm; the United States failed to ratify and never joined the League; France was left disenchanted because the Anglo-American guarantee of her frontiers could not apply; Italy felt cheated of territory promised in 1915; Russia was ignored because the powers would not negotiate with its Bolshevik government; and Germany was only temporarily weakened, being territorially untouched and producing three times as much steel as France by 1921. The great failing is stated exactly: it left the Germans with resentment and grievance but did not leave them too weak to retaliate and seek revenge - which is a statement about the relation between severity and enforcement rather than about severity alone. On culpability, the defences are serious. Central Europe had settled itself before the conference met; the secret treaties bound the Allies in advance; an American delegate remarked that given the intricacy of the problems it was surprising they made peace at all; many historians now regard the settlement as a workable compromise and perhaps the best achievable; and the peacemakers cannot be blamed for Hitler's rise to power, and certainly not for the Second World War. On continuity, four graded statements: that the settlement created grievances later movements used is fully supported; that its enforcement structure was defective from the outset is fully supported; that it made a second conflict comparatively more likely than either genuine reconciliation or sustained enforcement is a defensible inference; and that it caused the Second World War is not supported, since P. M. H. Bell argues that Europe including Germany was recovering well in the early 1920s and that the outline of a successful recovery was cut off in its prime by the great depression and the advent of Hitler.

**Misconception to avoid:** Offering balance instead of a verdict. "There is truth on both sides" is the least valuable sentence available. The disciplined position separates two questions, answers each differently, and grades the continuity claim into four statements of descending strength.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ These six questions are original and are built to cover the six probable framings recorded by the two canonical owners. ⚠️ Each model answer has been word-counted against its stated ceiling and follows the same discipline: claim, named evidence, analysis, qualification. ⚠️ Verified counts, excluding the question stem and the scoring note: **Question 1 = 150 / 150 · Question 2 = 148 / 150 · Question 3 = 246 / 250 · Question 4 = 249 / 250 · Question 5 = 249 / 250 · Question 6 = 250 / 250**. ⚠️ The scoring note after each identifies what an examiner would reward in that specific answer and is different every time.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

> ⚠️ Explain why the First World War did not end quickly, despite the expectations of 1914. *(Directive: explain.)*

**Model answer.** The short war failed for structural reasons. The German plan for a rapid western victory collapsed at the Marne in September 1914 after Belgian resistance delayed the capture of Brussels for over two weeks, leaving Germany in a two-front war with the war of movement over and trench lines running from the Alps to the Channel. Within it, defence held a decisive material advantage: wire had to be cleared by bombardment that destroyed surprise, machine-guns made frontal assault suicidal, and cratered ground and salients penalised even successful attacks. Politically, war aims were formulated after the fighting began and were mutually exclusive - German buffer zones, French recovery of Alsace-Lorraine, British insistence on Belgium, Habsburg survival against Serbia - so a negotiated settlement was almost impossible. Qualification: the east saw more movement throughout, and 1918 was mobile again, so the deadlock was a condition of method and circumstance, not a law.

⚠️ **Scoring note.** At this ceiling the examiner is looking for two mechanisms and a political cause, not a narrative. The answer earns its marks by pairing a technical reason with a diplomatic one and by closing with a qualification that pre-empts the obvious counter-example. Adding a third mechanism would force the removal of the qualification, which would be a bad trade.

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

> ⚠️ Bring out the significance of the mandate system established after the First World War. *(Directive: bring out.)*

**Model answer.** The mandate system transferred Germany's colonies and the Ottoman Arab provinces to victor states under League supervision, in three classes: former Ottoman territories requiring advice and assistance; African territories administered with welfare obligations; and remote territories administered as integral parts of the mandatory's own territory. Britain took Tanganyika, Britain and France divided Togoland and the Cameroons, South Africa gained German South West Africa; Syria became a French mandate and Palestine, Iraq and Transjordan British mandates. Its significance is double. It was really a device by which the Allies seized colonies without admitting annexation, and it plainly failed Wilson's promise of an impartial adjustment of colonial claims in the populations' interests. It was also the first international instrument to make imperial rule conditional and temporary in principle, requiring preparation for independence and annual reporting to a supervising commission. Qualification: that commission could scrutinise and advise, but could not compel.

⚠️ **Scoring note.** The whole mark here turns on refusing to resolve the ambiguity. A script that calls mandates "colonialism in disguise" and stops has answered half the question; so has one that calls them trusteeship. The closing qualification about enforcement is what converts the double verdict into an argument rather than a hedge.

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ "The First World War was the first total war of the modern age." Critically examine. *(Directive: critically examine.)*

**Model answer.** The claim holds, with two significant limits. Total war means the mobilisation of whole societies rather than the scale of fighting, and the evidence is specific. States took direct control of the economy: mines, factories and railways were centralised from 1915, shipping and agriculture were brought under government control, and a Ministry of National Service was created to allocate men between the army and industry, while Britain introduced conscription for the first time in May 1916. Labour was substituted at scale, with women taking places in factories and other jobs previously performed by men. Civilians became strategic targets by design: the British blockade caused desperate food shortages among the German civilian population and crippled exports, while the German unrestricted submarine campaign from January 1917 aimed to starve Britain, reaching its peak in April 1917 with 430 ships lost and Britain down to about six weeks' corn supply. Consent was manufactured continuously through newspapers, posters, films and advertisements. Two limits qualify the claim. Mobilisation was uneven and in places failed: Russia had six and a quarter million men mobilised by the end of 1914 with a third of them without rifles, which is incapacity rather than totality. And consent was contested throughout, with French army mutinies in 1917, German labour unrest and war-weariness, naval mutiny in 1918 and two Russian revolutions. The strongest formulation is therefore causal rather than descriptive: total war functioned as a selection mechanism that destroyed the states least able to sustain indefinite extraction.

⚠️ **Scoring note.** A "critically examine" directive requires the limits to occupy real space, not a closing clause. This answer spends roughly a fifth of its words on them and converts the concept into a causal claim at the end, which is the move that distinguishes an examination of the idea from an illustration of it. Note also that no casualty figure appears; the question is about mobilisation, and figures about death would be evidence for a different claim.

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ Account for the defeat of the Central Powers in the First World War. *(Directive: account for.)*

**Model answer.** They were not defeated at a point but exhausted across a system, and the causes are of five different kinds. Structurally, the failure of the plan for a rapid victory left Germany in a two-front war from September 1914, a burden every other cause compounded. At sea, Allied power was decisive: the blockade caused desperate food shortages among German civilians and crippled exports while keeping Allied armies supplied. The one cause that was a German decision rather than a German condition was the unrestricted submarine campaign of January 1917, which failed against convoys escorted by British, American and Japanese destroyers and brought the United States into the war - the account itself calls it a mistake. Materially, American entry brought vast new resources, made up for Russia's departure in December 1917, and let the Allies out-produce the enemy, which in the end proved decisive. In command, Lloyd George and Clemenceau were probably more competent than their opposite numbers, unity under Foch helped, and the August 1918 method - hundreds of tanks in short sharp jabs at several points on a wide front - broke the Hindenburg Line by late September. Finally the coalition failed from its weakest member: Bulgaria was defeated on 29 September 1918, Austria at Vittorio Veneto and Turkey in October, after which military defeat and dire food shortages produced naval mutiny and revolution at home. Qualification: these causes interacted rather than accumulated, the blockade producing the desperation that produced the submarine gamble that produced American entry.

⚠️ **Scoring note.** The marks here are in the sorting and in the nomination. Any competent script can list six reasons; this one classifies them by kind, identifies the only one that was a decision, and closes by showing three of them operating as a chain. The qualification is doing analytical work rather than hedging, which is the difference between a good 15-marker and an average one.

## Question 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ Examine the effects of the First World War on Europe and the wider world, distinguishing institutional from social consequences. *(Directive: examine.)*

**Model answer.** Three categories of effect, and the third is the largest. Institutionally, four imperial systems ended: revolution in Germany produced the abdication of Wilhelm II and a republic on 9 November 1918; the Habsburg Empire collapsed as its nationalities declared independence and Austria and Hungary split; two Russian revolutions brought the Bolsheviks to power; and the Ottoman empire was partitioned into mandates while a Turkish national state emerged by force at Lausanne in 1923. Their place was taken by small successor states, a cluster of five treaties and a new international organisation whose Covenant took effect on 10 January 1920. Socially, the war permanently enlarged state capacity over mines, factories, railways, shipping and agriculture, introduced administrative allocation of labour, brought women into war industries, made civilian populations strategic targets through blockade, and mobilised colonial soldiers, labourers and revenues across Asia and Africa. The demographic cost was severe and uneven - France lost around twenty per cent of men of military age - although Todman argues that the lost-generation interpretation is something of a myth and that overall just twelve per cent of fighting men died. Structurally, the war ended Europe's primacy: prestige declined, the empires that had dominated central and eastern Europe for over two hundred years disappeared almost overnight, the American share of world trade rose from ten per cent in 1914 to over twenty per cent by 1919, and Japan and China began their own industrialisation. Qualification: the maritime empires were simultaneously enlarged in 1919 and structurally weakened.

⚠️ **Scoring note.** At 20 marks the examiner expects three dimensions and a structural verdict, and expects the social dimension to be mechanism-led rather than sentimental. What lifts this answer is the deliberate use of a historiographical corrective inside the social paragraph - conceding severity while refusing the exaggerated version - and the closing paradox, which converts an effects list into an argument about the imperial order.

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ "The peace settlement of 1919-23 failed because it was unenforced rather than because it was harsh." Critically evaluate. *(Directive: critically evaluate.)*

**Model answer.** The proposition is largely correct, and its strength lies in relocating the defect from content to enforcement. On severity, the German objections grade very differently. The Fourteen Points complaint fails: the Points were never official, Germany ignored them in January 1918, and two further points on civilian damage and disarmament were added before the armistice with German knowledge. The territorial complaint is weak, since most losses were defensible on nationality grounds and Upper Silesia was conceded after a vote in which Germany kept about two-thirds. The complaint about inconsistent self-determination is strong - almost three million Germans in the Sudetenland, around a million under Polish rule, and union with a completely German Austria forbidden - and the complaint about reparations is strongest, since the £6600 million announced in 1921 was reduced to the £2000 million Keynes had proposed, under the Young Plan of 1929. On enforcement, the structure was dismantled at once: the United States failed to ratify and never joined the League, France was disenchanted when the Anglo-American guarantee lapsed, Italy felt cheated, and Russia was excluded. Germany meanwhile was virtually untouched territorially and by 1921 was producing three times as much steel as France, so the great failing was that the settlement left Germans resentful without leaving them too weak to retaliate. Lausanne in 1923 proves the point: where a defeated state could resist and the victors would not compel, the treaty was simply rewritten. Qualification: enforcement may have been politically unavailable to electorates exhausted by total war.

⚠️ **Scoring note.** The distinguishing feature of this answer is that it tests the proposition instead of illustrating it: the severity question is graded objection by objection before the enforcement argument is made, so the conclusion is earned rather than asserted. The closing qualification is deliberately uncomfortable - it concedes that the recommended alternative may not have been available - which is the mark of an evaluation rather than a preference.

# REMEDIATION

⚠️ These are the errors that recur most often in scripts on this topic. ⚠️ Each entry names the error, explains why it is wrong, and gives the corrected formulation in a form that can be transferred straight into an answer.

## Error family A - the settlement

**A1. "The Treaty of Versailles was the peace settlement."**
⚠️ Wrong because there were five treaties with five defeated states, negotiated between 1919 and 1923, and the most analytically important of them was rejected.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "The settlement comprised Versailles with Germany, St Germain with Austria, Neuilly with Bulgaria, Trianon with Hungary and Sevres with Turkey, the last of which was rejected and rewritten as Lausanne in 1923."

**A2. "Versailles caused the Second World War."**
⚠️ Wrong as stated: the peacemakers cannot be blamed for Hitler's rise, and certainly not for the war; the recovery of the early 1920s was interrupted by the Depression.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "The settlement supplied the grievances and dismantled its own enforcement; the Depression and the politics of the 1930s supplied the decisive rupture."

**A3. "Article 231 was the war-guilt clause of the treaty."**
⚠️ Wrong in its implication: the clause fixed blame on Germany and her allies and provided the legal basis for reparations; the political label attached to it afterwards.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Article 231 assigned responsibility as the legal foundation of reparations liability and became known politically as the war-guilt clause."

**A4. "Reparations were fixed at Versailles."**
⚠️ Wrong: the amount was not decided at Versailles and was announced in 1921.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Versailles created an unquantified liability; the figure of £6600 million was announced in 1921 and reduced to £2000 million under the Young Plan of 1929."

**A5. "The peacemakers carved up Austria-Hungary."**
⚠️ Wrong: the empire disintegrated as its nationalities declared independence before the conference met, and the conference's task was to formalise and recognise what had taken place.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "The empire dissolved itself in 1918; the conference recognised the successor states, fixed the frontiers between them and imposed minorities obligations as the price of recognition."

**A6. "Turkey accepted Sevres."**
⚠️ Wrong: the Turks rejected it under Mustafa Kemal, drove the Greeks from Smyrna and obtained revision.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Turkey was the first state to challenge the Paris settlement successfully, replacing Sevres with Lausanne in 1923 and recovering Eastern Thrace including Constantinople, and Smyrna."

## Error family B - the war

**B1. "New weapons caused the stalemate."**
⚠️ Wrong because the same technologies ended it once recombined; a catalogue is not an explanation.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Defensive firepower and field fortification had outrun the attacker's means of movement, supply and communication; the deadlock ended when armour, dispersed frontages and infantry-artillery-air coordination were combined in 1918."

**B2. "The whole war was trench warfare."**
⚠️ Wrong: there was more movement in the east throughout, Brusilov advanced a hundred miles in 1916, and 1918 in the west was mobile in both directions.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Trench deadlock was a western-front condition between 1914 and early 1918; the eastern front never closed, and the final campaign was a war of movement."

**B3. "Germany surrendered in 1918 after being invaded."**
⚠️ Wrong twice: an armistice is not a surrender, and no enemy troops had set foot on German soil.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Germany signed an armistice on 11 November 1918 while its army stood on foreign soil, which is why the claim that it was never defeated was available afterwards - and it was refuted by the fact that Ludendorff himself demanded the armistice on 3 October, expecting defeat in spring 1919."

**B4. "America won the war."**
⚠️ Wrong in its mechanism: by the end of 1917 only one American division had been in action.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "American entry supplied food, merchant ships and credit at once, a psychological effect on both sides, and manpower only from mid-1918 - which is why it changed the German timetable before it changed the battlefield."

**B5. "Jutland was a German victory."**
⚠️ Incomplete: tactically arguable, strategically decisive against Germany.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Germany lost eleven ships to Britain's fourteen and could claim the exchange, but the High Seas Fleet stayed in Kiel for the rest of the war, leaving British control of the surface complete."

**B6. "Gallipoli was a foolish adventure."**
⚠️ Incomplete: it had four serious strategic objectives and failed on execution.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Gallipoli sought to reopen Russia's supply route, eliminate the weakest Central Power, recruit three Balkan neutrals and open a southern front against Austria; the March naval attack failed on mines and destroyed the surprise on which the April landings depended."

## Error family C - evidence and attribution

**C1. Totalling casualty figures or inventing a war total.**
⚠️ Wrong because the recorded figures are national, are compiled on different measures, and explicitly exclude the crippled and civilian casualties.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "On the figures given in the standard accounts, Germany lost almost two million military dead and France 1.5 million; these exclude civilian casualties and the disabled, and no reliable total is asserted here."

**C2. Collective attribution for the Armenian catastrophe.**
⚠️ Wrong because it erases the decision-makers, implicates people who were not responsible, and cannot be evidenced.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "In 1915 the Ottoman government, controlled by the Committee of Union and Progress, ordered the arrest of the Armenian community's leadership on 24 April and enacted the Temporary Law of Deportation on 27 May; the Allied governments denounced the killings on 24 May 1915 as crimes against humanity and civilisation; the scholarly consensus treats the events as genocide, a characterisation the Republic of Turkey officially rejects."

**C3. Asserting a settled toll for a contested mortality.**
⚠️ Wrong for the Armenian deaths, the 1918-19 influenza pandemic, civilian deaths from blockade and colonial labour deaths.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Estimates vary very widely between authorities and the variation is itself part of the historical dispute."

**C4. Asserting colonial participation numbers as facts.**
⚠️ Wrong because the figures differ by definition and by authority.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "On the standard estimates, over 1.3 million Indian soldiers served overseas and around 140,000 Chinese labourers worked in France; the figures differ between authorities and should not be totalled."

**C5. Treating textbook study questions as previous-year questions.**
⚠️ Wrong and dangerous, because it creates a false record of examination precedent.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "No General Studies question in the audited ledgers for 2018-2026 is routed to this topic as its owner; the framings used here are probable framings recorded by the canonical owners, not past questions."

**C6. Reproducing a source-inherited date or figure error.**
⚠️ Three of them occur in the standard printed account used here and are repeated in most scripts, which is precisely why an examiner notices when they are corrected.
✅ **Correct formulations:** "The Kut-al-Amara garrison surrendered on **29 April 1916**, not in March 1916." · "The *Lusitania* was sunk on **7 May 1915**; roughly **1,959 were aboard** and **1,198 were killed**, of whom **128 were Americans** - those aboard and those killed are different quantities, and 'almost two thousand dead' conflates them." · "Versailles **detached Memel to the Principal Allied and Associated Powers**; its passage to Lithuania came afterwards and is not a treaty provision."

## Error family D - boundaries

**D1. Importing the causes of 1914.**
✅ **Correct handling:** one clause at most - that the war began from the July crisis - then proceed.

**D2. Narrating the Russian revolutions.**
✅ **Correct handling:** use only the recorded causes of the withdrawal, its date and its consequences for the fighting and for the conference.

**D3. Carrying the League's operational record into a 1919 answer.**
✅ **Correct handling:** teach the Covenant, the mandates and the minorities machinery as creations of 1919, note the built-in defect of the American absence, and hand over.

**D4. Writing Indian nationalism into a World History answer.**
✅ **Correct handling:** one sentence - wartime mobilisation and post-war disappointment sharpened colonial demands for reform - then hand over.

**D5. Importing the Depression, the Ruhr occupation or the inflation of the early 1920s.**
✅ **Correct handling:** name the reparations liability, the default and the Young Plan reduction, and route the rest to the interwar owner.

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Master chronology

```text
1914  Aug-Sep  Belgian resistance delays Brussels for over two weeks;
               German advance reaches within 20 miles of Paris; the
               French government withdraws to Bordeaux
      Sep      MARNE - plan for rapid victory ruined; two fronts; war
               of movement over; blockade becomes possible
      Aug-Sep  TANNENBERG and the MASURIAN LAKES - Russian equipment
               catastrophe; 6.25 million mobilised, a third unarmed
      Oct-Nov  Ypres holds the Channel ports; OTTOMAN ENTRY closes
               Russia's Black Sea route
      Dec      Serbia expels an Austrian invasion
1915  Mar-Dec  GALLIPOLI: naval attack fails on mines; landings in
               April including Anzacs, and in August; withdrawal in
               December
      Apr      Gas at Ypres blows back on its users
      7 May    LUSITANIA sunk: c.1,959 aboard, 1,198 killed, of whom
               128 were Americans
      May      ITALY declares war on Austria-Hungary under the secret
               TREATY OF LONDON
      Apr-May  ARMENIAN CATASTROPHE: arrests of 24 April; Allied
               declaration of 24 May; Tehcir Law of 27 May
      Oct      BULGARIA joins the Central Powers; Serbia overrun
      Dec      Kut-al-Amara besieged (surrenders 29 Apr 1916)
1916  Feb-Jun  VERDUN - designed to destroy the French army
      May      British CONSCRIPTION introduced for the first time
      31 May   JUTLAND - indecisive; the High Seas Fleet stays in Kiel
      Jun      BRUSILOV advances 100 miles, takes 400,000 prisoners
      Jul-Nov  THE SOMME - three aims; 20,000 killed on the first day
      Aug      ROMANIA enters, is occupied, loses its wheat and oil
      Dec      LLOYD GEORGE becomes prime minister
1917  Jan      UNRESTRICTED SUBMARINE WARFARE begins
      Apr      Peak: 430 ships lost; Britain down to six weeks' corn;
               UNITED STATES ENTERS
      Apr-Nov  Nivelle's failure and French mutinies; PASSCHENDAELE;
               CAMBRAI - 381 massed tanks breach the line
      Oct      CAPORETTO -> Allied Supreme War Council; Clemenceau
      Nov      BALFOUR DECLARATION (2 Nov); Bolsheviks take power
      Dec      RUSSIA WITHDRAWS; JERUSALEM falls (11 Dec)
1918  3 Mar    BREST-LITOVSK
      Mar-May  LUDENDORFF OFFENSIVE - breakthrough on the Somme; 40
               miles from Paris; an awkward bulge; FOCH in command
      8 Aug    AMIENS - tanks, short jabs, several points, wide front
      Sep      HINDENBURG LINE broken; BULGARIA defeated (29 Sep)
      3 Oct    Ludendorff demands an armistice
      30 Oct   MUDROS (Ottoman)
      3 Nov    VILLA GIUSTI (Austria-Hungary); Karl I abdicates
      9 Nov    German republic declared
      11 Nov   ARMISTICE - Germany not invaded
1919  18 Jan   PARIS CONFERENCE OPENS; Council of Ten -> Council of Four
      28 Jun   VERSAILLES signed; POLISH MINORITIES TREATY signed
      1919     ST GERMAIN (Austria); NEUILLY (Bulgaria)
1920  10 Jan   Versailles in force; LEAGUE formally exists
      1920     TRIANON (Hungary); SEVRES (Turkey)
      Mar      US SENATE refuses ratification
1921           Reparations announced: £6600 million
1923           LAUSANNE replaces Sevres
1929           YOUNG PLAN reduces reparations to £2000 million
```

## The causal engine

```text
   THE SHORT WAR FAILS (Marne, September 1914)
                 |
   +-------------+--------------+
   |                            |
 TWO FRONTS                 TRENCH CONDITION
 a standing strain          defence outruns attack
   |                            |
   +-------------+--------------+
                 |
   ALTERNATIVES TRIED AND FAILED IN 1915
   (Gallipoli, Italy, Mesopotamia, western attacks)
                 |
   ATTRITION ADOPTED DELIBERATELY IN 1916
                 |
   TOTAL MOBILISATION OF SOCIETIES
   state control of industry, transport, food; labour allocated;
   women in war work; opinion managed; CIVILIANS TARGETED
                 |
   +-------------+--------------+
   |                            |
 BLOCKADE BITES            EXTRACTION EXCEEDS CAPACITY
 food shortage in          in Russia, Austria-Hungary,
 Germany                   the Ottoman state
   |                            |
 DESPERATION ->            REVOLUTIONS AND DISSOLUTION
 UNRESTRICTED                   |
 SUBMARINE WAR             RUSSIA LEAVES (Dec 1917)
 (Jan 1917)                     |
   |                       GERMAN TROOPS RELEASED
 USA ENTERS                     |
 (Apr 1917)  ------------> A DEADLINE IS CREATED
   |                            |
 MATERIAL SUPERIORITY      LUDENDORFF ATTACKS (Mar 1918)
   |                            |
 THE AUGUST METHOD  <----- AND FAILS INTO A SALIENT
 IS AFFORDABLE                  |
   |                       BEST GERMAN TROOPS CONSUMED
   +-------------+--------------+
                 |
   COALITION COLLAPSES FROM THE EDGES (Sep-Nov 1918)
                 |
   GERMAN SOCIETY BREAKS -> mutiny, revolution, republic
                 |
   ARMISTICE WITHOUT INVASION -> the undefeated-army myth
                 |
   A CONFERENCE FACING: a dissolved empire, an excluded Russia,
   an absent Germany, prior secret treaties, mobilised electorates
                 |
   FIVE TREATIES + A COVENANT + MANDATES + MINORITIES TREATIES
                 |
   ENFORCEMENT DISMANTLED: no American ratification, no guarantee
   to France, an aggrieved Italy, an excluded Russia
                 |
   GRIEVANCE WITHOUT INCAPACITY -> LAUSANNE PROVES THE POINT (1923)
```

## Master comparison: the three questions, side by side

| Axis | **The war as fought** | **Total war and society** | **The settlement** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Central problem | Why no quick decision? | What did mobilisation do to states and peoples? | What did the victors build and why did it not hold? |
| Decisive evidence | Marne; the five trench difficulties; the blockade; convoys; the August method | State control of industry, transport and food; conscription; women's war work; blockade hardship; propaganda | Five treaties; the Covenant; mandates; minorities treaties; the six weaknesses |
| Key date | 11 November 1918 | May 1916 (British conscription) | 28 June 1919 and 1923 |
| Key figure | ✅ 430 ships lost in April 1917 | ⚠️ None - the argument is mechanism-led | ✅ £6600 million, reduced to £2000 million |
| Characteristic error | A battle list | "It was a big war" | "The Treaty of Versailles" |
| Verdict shape | Cumulative causation | Concept plus limits | Graded audit, then a relation |
| Formulation | Exhausted across a system | A selection mechanism | Unenforced rather than harsh |
