## Answer-writing spine

```text
   STEP 1  NAME THE FAMILY (10 seconds)
           attrition | defeat | effects | settlement | total war |
           boundaries
   STEP 2  CHECK THE OWNER
           causes -> previous topic | League record, appeasement,
           Depression -> interwar topic | revolutions -> Russia topic |
           fascism -> fascism topic | post-1945 -> decolonisation topic |
           Indian politics -> Modern Indian History
   STEP 3  CHOOSE THE FORMULATION (one, carried throughout)
   STEP 4  BUILD THE SPINE
           10 marks: thesis + two mechanisms + one consequence + verdict
           15 marks: add the counter-position and a qualification
           20 marks: add the social dimension and the structural legacy
   STEP 5  SPEND THE EVIDENCE BUDGET
           2-3 dates | 2 figures | 2-3 instruments | 2 names |
           1 historian (evaluative questions only)
   STEP 6  CLOSE WITH A VERDICT, NOT A SUMMARY
   STEP 7  RUN THE CONTROLS
           five treaties named? reparations figures right? armistice
           date right? no invented casualty total? no collective
           attribution? no claim that Versailles caused 1939?
```

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## 1. The three questions, and the boundary of the topic

- ⚠️ One title, three questions: the war as fought; total war and society; the settlement and its instability. Each has its own evidence base and verdict shape.
- ✅ Owned: fronts and phases; the trench condition; the war at sea; the Ottoman fronts; the war beyond Europe; total war; the ruptures of 1917; 1918 and the armistices; the reckoning; war aims; the Paris machinery; the five treaties; the Covenant, mandates and minorities system; self-determination; economic consequences; imperial transformation; the responsibility and continuity debate.
- ❌ Not owned: the causes of 1914 and the July crisis; the League's operational record, reparations diplomacy after 1921, collective security and appeasement; the Russian revolutions themselves; fascism; post-1945 decolonisation; Indian wartime and post-war politics.
- ✅ Syllabus anchors: world wars; redrawal of national boundaries; colonization and decolonization; effect on the society.

## 2. Belligerents and the arithmetic of entry

- ✅ Allies: Britain and her empire (with troops from Australia, Canada, India, New Zealand), France, Russia (left December 1917), Serbia, Belgium, Japan, Italy (May 1915), Romania (August 1916), USA (April 1917).
- ✅ Central Powers: Germany, Austria-Hungary, Turkey (November 1914), Bulgaria (October 1915).
- ⚠️ The Central Powers gained two small allies by October 1915 and nothing after; the Allies gained three, one of them the largest economy in the world, and lost one, within 1917.

## 3. 1914: the failure of the short war

- ✅ Belgian resistance delayed the capture of Brussels for over two weeks, giving Britain time and leaving the Channel ports free for the British Expeditionary Force.
- ✅ Germans reached within twenty miles of Paris; the French government withdrew to Bordeaux; supply problems and marching fatigue slowed the advance.
- ✅ Marne, September 1914, French under Joffre; Germans driven back to the Aisne and dug in.
- ✅ Four consequences: the plan for rapid victory ruined; full-scale war on two fronts; the war of movement over, with trench lines eventually from the Alps to the Channel; time for the British blockade.
- ✅ Antwerp fell; the British Expeditionary Force held Ypres, probably saving Dunkirk, Calais and Boulogne.
- ✅ East: Tannenberg (August) and the Masurian Lakes (September) after Hindenburg was recalled; Russian success in Galicia; 6.25 million mobilised by year-end with a third without rifles; the Russians never recovered.
- ✅ Ottoman entry threatened Russia's Black Sea route; Serbia expelled an Austrian invasion.

## 4. The trench condition

- ✅ Defending weapons were more deadly than attacking ones; fixed machine-guns behind wire beat attackers relying on preceding bombardment.
- ✅ Five difficulties: wire cleared by bombardment that destroyed surprise; aircraft and balloons spotting approach roads; magazine rifles and machine-guns making frontal assault suicidal and cavalry useless; cratered ground and further machine-gun fire after a breach; salients whose flanks were vulnerable.
- ⚠️ Break-in was common, break-through rare, exploitation absent before 1918; the core problem was a speed differential - defenders moved reserves by rail faster than attackers crossed cratered ground on foot.
- ✅ Gas at Ypres in 1915 blew back on the Germans; the Allies replied in kind.
- ⚠️ The description is western-front specific: more movement in the east, and 1918 was mobile in both directions.

## 5. 1915: the alternatives tried and exhausted

- ✅ West: Neuve Chapelle, Loos, Champagne, a German attack at Ypres - no decisive breakthrough.
- ✅ East: Warsaw and the whole of Poland captured.
- ✅ Gallipoli: pressed by Churchill; four objectives - reopen Russia's supply route, eliminate the weakest Central Power, recruit Bulgaria, Greece and Romania, and attack Austria from the south.
- ✅ March naval attack failed on mines and destroyed surprise; April landings including Anzacs; August landings; December withdrawal.
- ✅ Consequences: morale; the last chance of helping Russia via the Black Sea; probably decided Bulgaria; Salonika landing too late to save Serbia.
- ✅ Bulgaria entered in October 1915 and Serbia was overrun.
- ✅ Mesopotamia: force protecting Anglo-Persian oil besieged at Kut-al-Amara from December 1915; surrendered 29 April 1916.
- ✅ Italy declared war in May 1915 under the secret Treaty of London promising Trentino, the South Tyrol, Istria, Trieste, part of Dalmatia, Adalia, Aegean islands and a protectorate over Albania; little headway.

## 6. 1916: attrition as strategy

- ✅ Verdun from February under Falkenhayn, designed to draw in and destroy the best French troops; Petain defended; abandoned in June; French about 315,000, Germans over 280,000 killed, no territorial gains.
- ✅ Somme 1 July to November; three aims - relieve Verdun, take over more of the line, prevent German reinforcement of the east; 20,000 killed and 60,000 injured on the first day; Haig continued for over four months; advances of a few hundred yards to seven miles on a thirty-mile front; the real importance was the blow to German morale.
- ✅ British conscription introduced for the first time, May 1916.
- ✅ Somme losses killed or wounded: Germans 650,000; British 418,000; French 194,000.
- ✅ "Lions led by donkeys" - a German officer after a disastrous attack in 1915; Laffin 1988 prosecuting; Harris 2008 balanced; Terraine 1963 and Sheffield 2011 defending.
- ✅ Asquith resigned; Lloyd George prime minister from December 1916; as Minister of Munitions from May 1915 he improved shell and machine-gun supply, backed the Stokes light mortar and the tank, and took control of mines, factories and railways.
- ✅ Brusilov, June 1916: requested to relieve Verdun; broke the front, advanced 100 miles, took 400,000 prisoners.
- ✅ Romania entered in August, was occupied, and lost its wheat and oil to Germany.
- ✅ Hindenburg's memoirs: Germany could not have survived many more campaigns with losses like Verdun and the Somme.

## 7. The war at sea

- ✅ Both fleets cautious; Churchill on Jellicoe as the only man who could have lost the war in an afternoon; sixteen German Dreadnoughts against twenty-seven British.
- ✅ Three Allied aims: blockade; keep Allied trade routes open; transport and supply the army through the Channel ports - all achieved by mid-1918.
- ✅ Falkland Islands; nearly all German armed surface ships destroyed by the end of 1914.
- ✅ The blockade required stopping and searching neutral ships; the USA objected strongly.
- ✅ *Lusitania*, 7 May 1915: armed and carrying munitions as the Germans knew; roughly 1,959 aboard, 1,198 killed, 128 Americans among the dead; Wilson concluded the USA must take sides; Bethmann toned the campaign down.
- ✅ Jutland, 31 May 1916: the only fleet action; indecisive; Germans lost eleven ships to Britain's fourteen; the High Seas Fleet stayed in Kiel; British surface control complete.
- ✅ Unrestricted submarine warfare from January 1917; peak April 1917 with 430 ships lost; Britain down to about six weeks' corn supply.
- ✅ Convoys, insisted on by Lloyd George and escorted by British, American and Japanese destroyers, drastically reduced losses; the campaign was a mistake because it brought in the USA.

## 8. The Ottoman fronts and the Armenian catastrophe

- ✅ Ottoman entry November 1914 closed Russia's main supply and trade route.
- ✅ Sarikamish, 22 December 1914 to 17 January 1915: catastrophic Ottoman defeat.
- ✅ Kut-al-Amara besieged from December 1915, surrendered 29 April 1916; Baghdad later taken; Jerusalem fell 11 December 1917; the two captures gave Britain control of vast oil supplies.
- ✅ Arab revolt from 10 June 1916; T. E. Lawrence associated with it; the Arabs expected independence as a reward.
- ✅ Three incompatible undertakings: to Arab leaders; Anglo-French Sykes-Picot, May 1916; the Balfour Declaration, 2 November 1917.
- ✅ 24 April 1915: arrests of Armenian community leaders and intellectuals in Constantinople, ordered through the Interior Ministry under Talat.
- ✅ 24 May 1915: Britain, France and Russia denounced the killings as "crimes against humanity and civilisation" - the first diplomatic use of the phrase.
- ✅ 27 May 1915: Temporary Law of Deportation (Tehcir Law).
- ✅ Authority: the Ottoman government controlled by the Committee of Union and Progress; Talat, Enver and Cemal conventionally identified as principally responsible.
- ✅ Scholarly consensus treats the events as genocide; the Republic of Turkey officially rejects that characterisation. ⚠️ Estimates range widely, commonly cited between about 800,000 and 1.5 million; no figure is asserted.
- ✅ Armistice of Mudros, 30 October 1918.

## 9. The war beyond Europe

- ✅ Troops from India, Australia, New Zealand, Canada and South Africa; fighting spread into the eastern Mediterranean, Asia and Africa.
- ✅ Japan an Allied belligerent; Japanese destroyers escorted convoys.
- ✅ German colonies became mandates: Tanganyika to Britain; Togoland and the Cameroons divided between Britain and France; German South West Africa to South Africa.
- ✅ Jeremy Black: the war produced the final stage of the partition of Africa.
- ⚠️ Three categories of colonial participation: combatant service; military labour; resources and finance.
- ⚠️ Standard estimates, to be labelled as such: over 1.3 million Indian soldiers overseas; several hundred thousand French colonial recruits; around 140,000 Chinese labourers in France.
- ✅ India boundary: one bridge sentence - wartime mobilisation and post-war disappointment sharpened colonial demands for reform.

## 10. Total war at home

- ✅ Definition: it involved not just armies and navies but entire populations, and was the first big conflict between modern industrialised nations.
- ✅ Features: new methods and weapons - tanks, submarines, bombers, machine-guns, heavy artillery, mustard gas; women taking men's places in factories and other jobs; severe civilian hardship from blockade in the Central Powers and Russia; governments organising ordinary people as never before; propaganda through newspapers, posters, films and advertisements.
- ✅ Concrete instance: control of mines, factories and railways from 1915; shipping and agriculture under government control; a small war cabinet; a Ministry of National Service; the convoy system.
- ⚠️ Franchise change followed closely in several states - Britain 1918, Germany under the new republic, the USA 1920 - but war work did not simply cause it; movements predated the war and much wartime employment was reversed.
- ⚠️ Limits: uneven mobilisation, with Russia the counter-case; contested consent, including French mutinies of 1917, German naval mutiny of 1918 and two Russian revolutions; reversed gains; colonial asymmetry.
- ⚠️ The causal conclusion: total war destroyed the states least able to sustain total extraction.

## 11. 1917: the two ruptures

- ✅ West: Nivelle's failure and French mutinies handled by Petain; Passchendaele June to November, British casualties 324,000 against 200,000 German for four miles; Cambrai, where 381 massed tanks breached the line and lack of reserves prevented exploitation, becoming the model for 1918.
- ✅ Caporetto in October; Italian morale revived; the Allied Supreme War Council created; Clemenceau became premier.
- ✅ Russia withdrew in December 1917; recorded causes - continuous heavy losses, lack of arms and supplies, transport and communication problems, utterly incompetent leadership; two revolutions; the Bolsheviks willing to make peace; Brest-Litovsk 3 March 1918.
- ✅ Consequence: the entire weight of German forces could be thrown west; without the USA the Allies would have been hard pressed; Russia was later ignored at the conference.
- ✅ USA entered April 1917: caused by the U-boat campaign and the German approach to Mexico offering Texas, New Mexico and Arizona; the tsar's overthrow removed American reluctance.
- ✅ American contributions in order: food, merchant ships and credit; psychological effect on both sides; men - one division in action by end-1917, over half a million by mid-1918, around two million in total.

## 12. 1918 and the end of the war

- ✅ Ludendorff's offensive: launched to win before too many US troops arrived and before discontent produced revolution; breakthrough on the Somme in March; within forty miles of Paris by the end of May; momentum lost, creating an awkward bulge; Foch in overall command.
- ✅ The Allied counter-offensive from 8 August near Amiens: hundreds of tanks, short sharp jabs, several different points, a wide front rather than one narrow front; the Germans forced to withdraw their entire line; no salient formed.
- ✅ Hindenburg Line broken by the end of September.
- ✅ Ludendorff demanded an armistice on 3 October for three stated reasons: milder terms on the Fourteen Points; saving Germany from invasion; preserving the army's discipline and reputation.
- ✅ Exhaustion evidence: best troops lost; young inexperienced replacements; American totals around two million; Spanish flu; some 350,000 German troops surrendered in the last three months.
- ✅ Armistices: Salonica 29 September (Bulgaria); Mudros 30 October (Ottoman); Villa Giusti 3 November (Austria-Hungary); 11 November (Germany).
- ✅ German revolution: naval mutiny; Wilhelm II compelled to abdicate; republic declared 9 November - before the armistice.
- ✅ Habsburg dissolution: Karl I abdicated in November 1918; the nationalities declared independence; Austria and Hungary split; decisions were taken before the conference met.
- ✅ Germany was not invaded and no enemy troops set foot on German soil; it was soon widely accepted that the armies had not been defeated; returning soldiers were welcomed as heroes.
- ⚠️ Refutation of the myth: the Hindenburg Line was broken; Ludendorff himself demanded the armistice expecting defeat in spring 1919; mass surrenders; all allies had capitulated.

## 13. Why it lasted, and why they lost

- ✅ Duration: even balance; progressive globalisation with new belligerents; incompatible war aims; the tactical condition favouring defence; propaganda. ✅ Kitchener predicted nearer three years than three months.
- ✅ Defeat, sorted: structural (two fronts); maritime (blockade decisive); self-inflicted (the submarine campaign, a mistake because it brought in the USA); material (American resources and out-production); command and coalition (Lloyd George, Clemenceau, Foch, and Haig's learning); attritional strain; coalition failure from the edges; political collapse at home.
- ✅ Sheffield's four Haig contributions: pre-1914 army reform; transformation of the British Expeditionary Force 1916-18; the wearing-down function of the Somme, Arras and Third Ypres; and the 1918 generalship, including tanks, avoidance of salients, infantry in buses and infantry-artillery-air coordination.
- ⚠️ The four-step chain: blockade → food shortage → submarine gamble → American entry → material superiority.

## 14. The human reckoning

- ✅ Recorded military deaths: Germany almost 2 million; Russia 1.7 million; France 1.5 million; Austria-Hungary over 1 million; Britain and empire about 1 million; Italy around 530,000; Turkey 325,000; Serbia 322,000; Romania 158,000; USA 116,000; Bulgaria 49,000; Belgium 41,000. ✅ Excludes the crippled and civilian casualties.
- ✅ France lost around twenty per cent of men of military age.
- ✅ Todman (2005): the lost-generation interpretation is something of a myth; overall just twelve per cent of fighting men died; the 20,000 British killed on the first day of the Somme was not typical.
- ✅ Spanish flu added to German difficulties in the final retreat. ⚠️ Global mortality estimates vary very widely; none asserted.
- ✅ Decline in Europe's prestige; the beginning of the end of European domination; the central and eastern European empires disappeared almost overnight.

## 15. War aims and the Fourteen Points

- ✅ At the outbreak none had specific aims, except Germany and Austria on preserving the Habsburg Empire by destroying Serbia; aims were listed later, perhaps to give troops clear objectives.
- ✅ Lloyd George, January 1918: defence of democracy; Alsace-Lorraine; restoration of Belgium and Serbia; an independent Poland; democratic self-government for the Habsburg nationalities; self-determination for the German colonies; an international organisation; German reparations.
- ✅ Wilson's Fourteen Points, January 1918, in four groups: general principles (1-4); the colonial clause (5); territorial and national settlements (6-13); the institution (14).
- ✅ Two additions before the armistice: Germany to pay for damage to civilian population and property, and to be reduced to "virtual impotence"; Germany knew of them.
- ⚠️ Four incompatible commitments: Treaty of London 1915; Sykes-Picot 1916; undertakings to Arab leaders; Balfour Declaration 1917.

## 16. Paris and the machinery

- ✅ Conference opened 18 January 1919; Council of Ten became Council of Four - Wilson, Lloyd George, Clemenceau, Orlando.
- ⚠️ Positions: France sought a harsh peace to cripple Germany; Britain sought a less severe settlement to preserve a customer while bound by an election fought on severity; the USA sought a lenient settlement on the Points, disillusioned by Brest-Litovsk.
- ✅ Had Clemenceau prevailed, the Rhineland would have become independent and France would have annexed the Saar.
- ✅ Four constraints: central Europe already settled on the ground; prior secret treaties; the absence of Germany and the exclusion of Russia; mobilised electorates.
- ✅ Germany excluded from discussions; all written criticisms ignored except one.
- ✅ Versailles signed 28 June 1919 in the Hall of Mirrors; in force 10 January 1920; the US Senate refused ratification in March 1920.
- ✅ Contemporary verdicts: White - surprising they made peace at all; Nicolson - I shouldn't sign for a moment; Keynes resigned in protest.

## 17. Versailles: the six categories

- ✅ Territory: Alsace-Lorraine to France; Eupen, Moresnet and Malmedy to Belgium; North Schleswig to Denmark after a plebiscite; West Prussia and Posen to Poland; Memel detached to the Principal Allied and Associated Powers, Lithuanian control following later.
- ✅ Special regimes: Danzig a free city under League administration because its population was wholly German; the Saar under League administration for fifteen years with French use of the coal mines, then a vote.
- ✅ Colonies: the African colonies became mandates under League supervision, "looked after" by member states.
- ✅ Military: 100,000 troops; no conscription; no tanks, armoured cars, military aircraft or submarines; six battleships; the Rhineland permanently demilitarised, comprising the left bank and a fifty-kilometre strip on the right, occupied by Allied troops for at least ten years.
- ✅ Responsibility and payment: the War Guilt clause fixing blame on Germany and her allies and proposing the ex-Kaiser's trial; reparations not fixed at Versailles, announced in 1921 at £6600 million.
- ✅ Institution: the League formed, with aims and organisation in the Covenant, which was part of the treaty.
- ✅ Also: Estonia, Latvia and Lithuania set up as independent states - self-determination carried into practice; union with Austria forbidden.

## 18. The German objections, graded

- ✅ Dictated peace: understandable; undercut by Brest-Litovsk; excluding Germany was imprudent and gave Hitler the "Diktat" argument.
- ✅ Fourteen Points: probably invalid - never official, ignored by Germany in January 1918, and supplemented before the armistice.
- ✅ Territorial transfers: largely invalid; most losses defensible on nationality; Upper Silesia conceded after a vote in which Germany kept about two-thirds.
- ✅ Self-determination inconsistency: valid and serious - a million Germans under Polish rule, almost three million in the Sudetenland, union with a seven-million German Austria forbidden.
- ✅ Colonies: probably valid on the stated principle - hardly an impartial adjustment, and a device for seizure without admitting annexation.
- ✅ Disarmament: valid to some extent; no other power disarmed; enforcement impossible because Germany exploited every loophole.
- ✅ Article 231: valid as to procedure - a six-week Special Commission on War Responsibility - and as to political effect; the substantive causation question belongs to the previous owner.
- ✅ Reparations: valid as to quantum; Keynes urged £2000 million; the Allies reduced the sum to £2000 million under the Young Plan of 1929.
- ⚠️ Verdict: harsh enough to embitter Germany, not harsh enough to render it permanently incapable of recovery.

## 19. The other treaties

- ✅ St Germain 1919 (Austria): Bohemia and Moravia, with ten million people, to Czechoslovakia; Dalmatia, Bosnia and Herzegovina to what became Yugoslavia; Bukovina to Romania; Galicia to Poland; the South Tyrol to the Brenner Pass, Trentino, Istria and Trieste to Italy.
- ✅ Trianon 1920 (Hungary): Slovakia and Ruthenia to Czechoslovakia; Croatia and Slovenia to Yugoslavia; Transylvania and the Banat of Temesvar to Romania; delayed by Bela Kun's communist seizure of power and its overthrow.
- ✅ Both contained the League Covenant; on the whole they kept to the spirit of self-determination and placed more people under governments of their own nationality than ever before in Europe.
- ✅ Austria's population fell from 22 million to 6.5 million with most industrial wealth lost; Hungary's from 21 million to 7.5 million; new tariffs hampered Danube trade; Austria needed repeated League loans; there was an excellent economic case for union with Germany.
- ✅ Neuilly 1919 (Bulgaria): the Aegean coastline to Greece and territory to Yugoslavia and Romania; at least a million Bulgars under foreign governments.
- ✅ Sevres 1920 (Turkey): Eastern Thrace, Aegean islands and Smyrna to Greece; Adalia and Rhodes to Italy; the Straits permanently open; Syria a French mandate; Palestine, Iraq and Transjordan British mandates.
- ✅ Rejected under Mustafa Kemal; the Greeks driven from Smyrna; Italian and French withdrawal from the Straits leaving British troops at Chanak; Lausanne 1923 restored Eastern Thrace including Constantinople, and Smyrna; Turkey was the first state to challenge the Paris settlement successfully.

## 20. The Covenant, mandates and minorities

- ✅ The League formally existed from 10 January 1920, headquarters Geneva; not one man's idea - Cecil, Smuts and Bourgeois put forward detailed schemes.
- ✅ The Covenant was part of the treaties, so joining the organisation meant accepting the settlement, and rejecting the settlement meant rejecting the organisation - which is what the United States did.
- ✅ Mandate classes: A, former Ottoman territories nearest independence; B, African territories with welfare obligations; C, remote territories administered as integral portions of the mandatory's territory.
- ✅ A standing mandates commission from 1921 scrutinised annual reports and advised the Council without enforcement power; committees also covered disarmament, minorities, labour, health and welfare.
- ✅ Minorities treaties from 28 June 1919, beginning with Poland: civil, political and religious rights regardless of origin, language or religion, with complaint to the League.
- ✅ Covenant Article 10 on territorial integrity and political independence; Article 16 on treating a war in disregard of the Covenant as war against all members, with sanctions.
- ⚠️ Mandate formulation: simultaneously the last instrument of imperial expansion and the first written admission that imperial rule was conditional and temporary.

## 21. Self-determination: the rule

- ✅ Applied: more people under governments of their own nationality than ever before in Europe; plebiscites in North Schleswig and Upper Silesia; Estonia, Latvia and Lithuania created.
- ✅ Withheld, in four categories: economic viability (Sudeten and Polish Germans); security and power (Anschluss forbidden); prior bargains (the South Tyrol under the Treaty of London); race and empire (mandates instead of independence; Smyrna to Greece).
- ⚠️ The rule: applied where it weakened the defeated, withheld where it would have strengthened them or weakened the victors.
- ⚠️ The principle's own incoherence: mixed populations; no rule for defining the unit; conflict with viability; conflict with security.
- ✅ The minorities treaties are the peacemakers' own admission of the shortfall.

## 22. The economic aftermath

- ✅ Reparations: principle largely unobjectionable; £6600 million announced 1921 widely judged too high; Keynes urged £2000 million; German default; Allied reliance on German cash to service their own American war debts; international tension when France tried to force payment; reduction to £2000 million under the Young Plan of 1929, after reparations had proved disastrous economically and politically.
- ⚠️ The triangular structure: German reparations to the Allies; Allied war debts to the United States.
- ✅ Successor-state economies damaged; tariffs hampered Danube trade.
- ✅ German territory virtually untouched; industry switched back quickly; by 1921 Germany was producing three times as much steel as France.
- ✅ Japan, China and the USA expanded trade at Europe's expense; the American share of world trade rose from ten per cent in 1914 to over twenty per cent by 1919; Japan and China began industrialisation programmes; the 1920s American boom ended in the Wall Street Crash of October 1929.
- ⚠️ Two permanent legacies of the war economy: an international network of official debt, and the demonstrated capability of the state to direct an economy.

## 23. Empires destroyed and enlarged

- ⚠️ Four variables: defeat; whether empire and state were the same thing; whether self-determination was applied; capacity to resist. The second discriminates.
- ✅ Germany: regime change without state dissolution - abdication, a republic on 9 November 1918, the Weimar Republic's severe problems until 1933.
- ✅ Russia: two revolutions, withdrawal, Brest-Litovsk, and exclusion from the settlement.
- ✅ Austria-Hungary: complete collapse; the successor states inherited minorities, severed economies, mutual hostility and military weakness between two great powers.
- ✅ Ottoman: the empire died and a national state survived by force.
- ✅ Britain and France territorially enlarged in 1919 through mandates; ⚠️ structurally weakened at the same time; the reckoning deferred to after 1945.
- ⚠️ The structural sentence: the war destroyed the empires in which nationality and sovereignty were the same question and enlarged the empires in which they were not.

## 24. The verdict and the continuity debate

- ✅ Six recorded weaknesses: the revisionist/status-quo split with lukewarm defenders; American non-ratification; the lapsed guarantee to France; Italian grievance; Russian exclusion; Germany only temporarily weakened. ✅ Plus unresolved minority questions and German economic strength.
- ✅ The great failing: resentment and grievance without incapacity to retaliate.
- ✅ The softer verdict: White on the surprise that peace was made at all; the "workable compromise" assessment; the peacemakers not blameable for Hitler or for 1939; Bell on recovery cut off by the Depression and the advent of Hitler.
- ⚠️ Continuity graded: grievances created (supported); enforcement defective (supported); a second conflict made comparatively more likely (defensible inference); caused the Second World War (not supported).
- ⚠️ Counterfactual rules: change one variable that was actually proposed; trace the mechanism; state the limit.

## 25. Examination controls

- ⚠️ Six directive families: attrition; defeat; effects; settlement; total war; boundaries.
- ⚠️ Evidence budget: two or three dates, two figures, two or three instruments, two names, one historian, one formulation.
- ✅ Date control: Marne September 1914; Ottoman entry November 1914; Gallipoli 1915; Verdun and the Somme 1916; Jutland 31 May 1916; unrestricted submarine warfare January 1917; US entry April 1917; Russian withdrawal December 1917; Brest-Litovsk 3 March 1918; Amiens 8 August 1918; Salonica 29 September, Mudros 30 October, Villa Giusti 3 November, republic 9 November, armistice 11 November 1918; Paris opens 18 January 1919; Versailles 28 June 1919, in force 10 January 1920; Trianon and Sevres 1920; reparations 1921; Lausanne 1923; Young Plan 1929.
- ❌ Prohibited: Versailles as the whole settlement; any reparations figure other than the two recorded; Article 231 described as titled the war-guilt clause; Germany fully occupied before surrender; America winning by troop numbers; Turkey accepting Sevres; invented mandate detail; a one-sided verdict on mandates; the claim that the war ended empire; a settled Armenian death toll or collective attribution; an influenza figure; unqualified colonial-participation figures; the claim that Versailles caused the Second World War; and any import from the five adjacent owners.
- ❌ Also prohibited, because the standard textbook carries them: a March 1916 date for the Kut-al-Amara surrender (✅ 29 April 1916); an April 1915 date or "almost two thousand dead" for the *Lusitania* (✅ 7 May 1915; roughly 1,959 aboard, 1,198 killed, 128 of them Americans); and the statement that Versailles gave Memel to Lithuania (✅ it detached Memel to the Principal Allied and Associated Powers).

## Construction record for this edition

- ⚠️ Lesson count derived from the dependency audit, not from any source's chapter structure: five lessons on the war's course, three on its distinct theatres and dimensions, one on total war, three on the end of the war and its reckoning, six on the settlement, three on its consequences, one on the verdict and one synthesis lesson.
- ⚠️ Structural variation is content-led: Lessons 1, 13, 21 and 24 are argument-mapped; Lessons 2, 4, 5, 10, 11 and 12 are chronological with analytical sorting; Lesson 3 is a single technical condition taught as a filter sequence; Lessons 6 and 19 are built on paired and five-way comparison tables; Lesson 7 carries a separate evidentiary-discipline apparatus; Lessons 17 and 20 are instrument-by-instrument; Lesson 18 is a graded audit; Lesson 25 is a technique lesson with no new content.
- ⚠️ The objection-and-reply block is present in every lesson and is deliberately not a single shell. Its heading is content-specific in eighteen lessons and takes the neutral form in six; its internal labels are recast in seven lessons where the exchange is a test, a defence or a misreading rather than a plain objection; and Lessons 1 and 25 carry differently shaped blocks - two paired objections with an unsettled residual, and a single objection with a concession - because each concerns the session's method rather than its history.
- ⚠️ Three defects in the core printed account are corrected rather than reproduced, each stated once in the lesson that teaches it and itemised in the source ledger: the Kut-al-Amara surrender date, the *Lusitania* date and casualty description, and the Memel shorthand.
- ⚠️ Every original Mains model answer is word-counted against its stated ceiling, and the counts are printed at the head of that section.
- ⚠️ Casualty figures appear only where the core printed account records them, always with the measure stated, never totalled, and always with the standing warning that authorities differ.
- ⚠️ The Armenian material is stated with named state, party, ministers, instruments and dates, with the characterisation dispute reported on both sides and no mortality figure asserted.
- ⚠️ One current linkage is used, in Lesson 7, and is not repeated.
- ⚠️ No compiled objective-question corpus appears anywhere in this file.

## Source-inherited errors corrected in this edition

⚠️ Three factual defects were carried by the core printed account and have been corrected in the teaching, the visuals, the revision notes, the register notes, the traps, the remediation set and the master chronology. No external research beyond confirming these three items was conducted for this correction.

| Item | ❌ As printed in the core account | ✅ As stated in this session | Where corrected |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Kut-al-Amara surrender** | "besieged by Turks at Kut-el-Amara from December 1915 until March 1916, when it was forced to surrender" | The garrison capitulated on **29 April 1916**, after a siege of about five months | Lesson 4 sections 5 and the traps list; Lesson 7 section 2 and its revision notes; the master chronology; register notes 5 and 8; cumulative check A1; remediation C6; Lesson 25 control 15. ⚠️ The transliteration is also standardised to **Kut-al-Amara** throughout |
| **Lusitania date and casualties** | "In April 1915 the British liner Lusitania was sunk"; "out of almost two thousand dead, 128 were Americans" | Sunk on **7 May 1915**; roughly **1,959 aboard**, **1,198 killed**, of whom **128 were Americans**. ⚠️ The printed figure conflates those aboard with those killed | Lesson 6 section 4, its traps, revision notes and concept check; the master chronology; register note 7; remediation C6; Lesson 25 control 15 |
| **Memel under Versailles** | "Memel was given to Lithuania" - the account's shorthand for a two-stage process | Versailles **detached Memel from Germany to the Principal Allied and Associated Powers**; ⚠️ Lithuanian control followed afterwards and is not a Versailles provision | Lesson 17 section 1, its visual, traps, revision notes and concept check; cumulative check C1; register note 17; remediation C6; Lesson 25 control 15 |

⚠️ **Why this is recorded here rather than in the lessons.** Each correction is stated once inside the lesson that teaches the fact, in ordinary teaching language, so that a learner sees the corrected fact and the reason for the correction together. This table exists so that an independent reviewer can see at a glance where the session departs from its own core source and why.

## Evidence and limitation controls applied throughout

1. ⚠️ Casualty and mortality figures appear only where the core printed account records them, with the measure stated, never totalled, averaged or extrapolated, and always introduced with a qualifier.
2. ⚠️ No figure is asserted for the Armenian catastrophe, the influenza pandemic, civilian blockade deaths or colonial labour deaths; the ranges and their contestedness are stated instead.
3. ⚠️ Responsibility for mass violence is attributed to named governments, parties, ministries and ministers, never to a nation, religion or people.
4. ⚠️ Contested characterisations are reported as contested with the positions named, and are not resolved by this session.
5. ⚠️ Standard estimates drawn from the bounded verification pass are labelled as estimates wherever they appear in teaching.
6. ⚠️ The damaged page in the available scan is disclosed rather than silently filled, and the material it covers is presented in its standard form with the limitation stated.
7. ⚠️ Boundary material belonging to the causes topic, the interwar topic, the Russian Revolution topic, the fascism topic, the decolonisation topic and the Modern Indian History owners is excluded, with the handover stated in ordinary teaching language.
